Henry asks whether the philosophy of consciousness has not, since the dawn of modern philosophy, opened "une autre dimension d'existence et d'essence" (another dimension of existence and essence), "un autre mode de la réalité" (another mode of reality) in an ontological sense. SZ asks the opposite question and answers it negatively: the modern titles of the subject ("Subjekt, Ich, Vernunft, Geist, Person") remain "unbefragt auf Sein und Struktur ihres Seins" (unquestioned as to the being and structure of their being, SZ 22), and Kant, taking over Descartes's position, lacks "eine vorgängige ontologische Analytik der Subjektivität des Subjekts" (SZ 24). Henry's question is the one SZ closes, put again; it sets the section's wager that the philosophy of consciousness is an ontology after all.

Henry says that the opposition of consciousness and being holds only on a pre-philosophical plane, because one of its terms stayed indeterminate; Greek ontology "demeure très souvent naïve" (as Heidegger noted) in taking the given being for granted, and when thought asks what makes the being possible it lifts "l'ambiguïté" of the word *étant*, which says both something and its "étantité" (beingness): "Le grec ὄν, « étant », dit encore Heidegger, cache en soi une essence propre d'étantité" (n. 32, H 161; the GA 5 run treats this quotation). Taking beingness into account divides the ontic from the ontological in the being itself, so that the concept of being ceases to designate "indistinctement [...] l'étant et son fondement ontologique", as it still does in Sartre. The division is SZ's ontic-ontological distinction (SZ 11–13) and the thesis that being is not itself a being ("Das Sein des Seienden »ist« nicht selbst ein Seiendes", SZ 6). The naïveté of Greek ontology is SZ's thesis in other words: it proceeds "ohne jedes ausdrückliche Wissen um den dabei fungierenden Leitfaden" (without any explicit knowledge of the guiding thread at work, SZ 26). What is Henry's is the claim that the division arises only with the conception of "étantité", which SZ does not use, and the criticism of Sartre, which has no counterpart in SZ.

Henry argues that the classical opposition of consciousness and thing does not coincide with that of consciousness and being: the analysis of the "chose" falls under the same dialectic as that of the being, and "la conscience n'est-elle pas précisément la choséité même de la chose et, comme telle, l'essence de celle-ci ? Ce n'est pas à l'être, en fait, que suppose, dans son concept, la conscience, c'est bel et bien à l'étant" (is consciousness not precisely the thinghood of the thing and, as such, its essence? It is not to being, in fact, that consciousness is opposed in its concept, but to the being); modern idealism thus conceals "un mode nouveau et proprement philosophique de questionner", the question of the condition of possibility of the thing; and the philosophy of consciousness is "l'accomplissement de la philosophie antique de l'être, elle est un terme et non un commencement" (the fulfilment of the ancient philosophy of being, an end and not a beginning). The last claim has its model in SZ's continuity thesis: Descartes inherits the *ens creatum* (SZ 24–25), and Kant's "ontologische Grundorientierung [...] die griechische bleibt" (basic ontological orientation remains the Greek, SZ 26). The inference that consciousness is the thinghood of the thing is Henry's. SZ's relevant sentence asks the ontological origin of thinghood: "Dinglichkeit selbst bedarf erst einer Ausweisung ihrer ontologischen Herkunft" (thinghood itself first needs a demonstration of its ontological origin, SZ 46), and it does not answer that the origin is consciousness.

Henry says that the concept of consciousness is the element by which modern philosophy thinks the possibility for the thing to manifest itself; if existence is the "form" of being, consciousness "n'est rien d'autre que cette forme" (is nothing other than this form); Fichte says explicitly that "conscience soit l'existence absolue ou la manifestation et la révélation de l'être dans son unique forme possible" (n. 33, VB 143–144); the "même pouvoir ontologique de manifestation" is thought by Fichte under "existence, forme, représentation, manifestation, révélation, image, *conscience*, *être au sens philosophique*"; consciousness is existence as it arises in the tearing of being, "cette forme seule et unique de toute manifestation possible" (nn. 34–35, VB 143, 141). The paragraph is Fichte's equation, read as the structure of the monism. It makes no SZ claim. It sets up the identification of consciousness with representation (¶9), which will be used against SZ's treatment of *Vorstellen* as a derivative mode (SZ 62: "im »nur« Vorstellen [...] bin ich nicht weniger beim Seienden draußen", in "merely" representing I am no less outside with beings).

Henry says that consciousness designates the essence of manifestation as interpreted by monism, and is therefore presented as an act of separating from being, rising above it, taking distance and opposing it; "*La conscience n'est elle-même rien d'autre que l'aliénation de l'être, c'est-à-dire l'être comme tel.*" Schelling's absolute identity "ne peut jamais arriver à la conscience" for lack of duality, and the highest term "se divise pour apparaître" (nn. 36–37, IT 333–334, 337–338). The paragraph is Schelling and the argument of §10 applied to consciousness. SZ is independent of it. The point of contact is negative: SZ treats consciousness as one of the titles under which the subject-position is carried on (SZ 22, 46) and does not identify it with the alienation of being.

Henry says that Schelling's *System of Transcendental Idealism* sets an irreducible opposition between intelligence and action, which belongs to the essence of manifestation: "L'intelligence doit se dégager complètement de la production pour que la conscience puisse naître" (n. 38, IT 205); "Tant que l'intelligence ne diffère pas de son action, aucune conscience de celle-ci n'est possible" (n. 39, IT 212); the opposition is "à elle-même en tant qu'active". The paragraph is Schelling, and independent of SZ.

Henry says that Böhme's thought was already commanded by an inner opposition in the life of the absolute; *Schiedlichkeit* (separability) is the condition of consciousness, thought together with otherness, mirror and doubling; this interpretation dominates Schelling's later work (myth, creation) as an advent of consciousness through division; "Cette séparation, [...] cette *Scheidung*, n'est-elle pas la condition de toute connaissance consciente ?" (n. 40, Jankélévitch). The paragraph is independent of SZ.

Henry says that the interpretation of consciousness through the alienation of being is not confined to the post-Kantians and Hegel (n. 41, "Cf. *infra*, Appendice") but dominates the philosophy of consciousness (n. 42, Sartre; n. 43, Renouvier) and is illustrated by its comprehension as "représentation": representing is making present, the represented is ontic, the representation "se réfère [...] à un processus ontologique", that of alienation; Fichte: existence is "représentation ou de conscience de l'être" (nn. 44–45); "La représentation ne désigne nullement, par suite, un mode particulier de la vie de la conscience"; "C'est pourquoi Heidegger peut dire que « *la représentation (Vor-stellen) règne sur tous les modes de la conscience* »" (n. 46, H 133), and representation "présente sur le mode de la représentation" (n. 47, H 134), presence of what "survient devant" in a milieu of exteriority. The treatment of the two quotations from the *Holzwege* essay, and the question whether the essay's exposition of modern subjectity is Heidegger's doctrine of consciousness, belongs to the GA 5 run and is not repeated. The SZ side is as follows. SZ gives the same thought about presence-making not of consciousness but of the Greek and modern understanding of being: *noein* has "die temporale Struktur des reinen »Gegenwärtigens« von etwas" (the temporal structure of the pure "making-present" of something), and what shows itself in it is understood "in Rücksicht auf – Gegenwart" (SZ 26). Henry transfers the structure from the Greek understanding of being to the essence of consciousness, in the sentences that follow the essay's. SZ also denies that representing is a leaving of the world: "im »lediglich« daran »denken« bin ich nicht weniger beim Seienden draußen in der Welt als bei einem originären Erfassen" (in merely thinking of it I am no less with beings outside in the world than in an originary grasp, SZ 62). The unit is a Re-inscription of SZ 26 with a Mediation by the essay, and it conflicts with SZ 62 on whether representing involves exteriority.

Henry says that philosophy of consciousness often specifies representation as certainty, and asks whether certainty modifies the essence of manifestation; since Descartes "l'être vrai *(ens verum)* est interprété comme l'être certain *(ens certum)*", which is not a new interpretation of the truth of beings but the moment it first becomes a problem; the truth of beings consists in being represented, "arraché à la nuit" (torn from the night) and brought into light; representation "est la vérité comprise en un sens ontologique". The *ens verum / ens certum* step is not SZ's. What SZ says of Descartes's certainty is at its start: Descartes thought "mit dem absoluten »Gewißsein« des cogito der Frage nach dem Seinsinn dieses Seienden enthoben zu sein" (SZ 24). Henry's account supplies a reading of that certainty that SZ does not give, as the ontological truth of representing, and it is consistent with SZ 24. Against it SZ later says that certainty is not representing but "das Gewißsein des Erschlossenen" (being certain of what is disclosed), whose kind follows the kind of truth in question, so that the apodictic evidence of theoretical knowing of the present-at-hand is one such kind and not the measure (SZ 136, 265). The unit is a Mediation, and SZ's own treatment of certainty is not used.

Henry asks how ontological truth, identical with representation, is understood when identified with certainty: certainty is "l'être-certain de ce qui est certain, le fait d'apparaître de ce qui nous apparaît" (n. 48), and "*La certitude est la certitude de la représentation*"; it "ne désigne rien d'autre [...] que *la vérité de l'étant*", "l'essence de la manifestation et de la vérité *de l'étant*". The paragraph develops Henry's own equation of certainty, representation and appearing. "Le fait d'apparaître" again stands for what SZ separates as *Erscheinen* and *Sichzeigen* (SZ 29–30, 36), and the equation of certainty with appearing is the sort of identification of showing and appearing that SZ 30 calls "alles auf den Kopf gestellt" (everything turned upside down). No SZ locus bears on the rest.

Henry says that with certainty the idea of a subjectivity appears: certainty is subjective, representation is opposed to the represented as the subjective to the objective, and "La subjectivité est l'essence du sujet. Le sujet apparaît comme la condition de la phénoménalité des phénomènes": the object becomes a phenomenon for us only when related to the subject, which "opère cette position « devant »". The paragraph is Henry's reading of the classical subject-object scheme. It uses the phrase of SZ 24 ("Subjektivität des Subjekts") for what a subject is, and SZ's verdict on the scheme is that every idea of "Subjekt" "macht noch [...] den Ansatz des *subjectum* [...] *ontologisch* mit" (SZ 46). Henry takes the subject as the power of positing the object; SZ asks what that scheme assumes about the being of the one who posits. No SZ claim is quoted, and the two parts of the paragraph (what the subject is, whether it is real) stand to be taken up again in ¶25.

Henry says that "*La conscience cependant n'est pas le sujet*": the classical opposition is not between something conscious and something not conscious, since "*Ce qui caractérise l'objet, c'est justement le fait qu'il est conscient*"; an *ob-jet* is situated in the phenomenal dimension and belongs to consciousness; consciousness is "le rapport du sujet et de l'objet, elle est ce rapport comme tel"; "Il n'y a séparation entre eux qu'aussitôt qu'il y a conscience" (n. 49, IT 213); Renouvier (n. 50); Schelling: "Le moi de la conscience [...] n'est pas sujet pur, il est en même temps sujet et objet" (n. 51, IT 65). The paragraph is Schelling, Renouvier and Henry's own redefinition, and SZ is independent of it. SZ's counterpart claim, that the "subject-object" scheme presupposes an unasked ontology of both terms, is the premise, not the conclusion, of Henry's identification.

Henry says that consciousness "est le rapport comme tel. *Le rapport est le terme concret*"; for Hegel "le subjectif pur tout autant que l'objectif pur [...] est une abstraction" (n. 52); Merleau-Ponty: subject and object are "deux moments abstraits d'une structure unique qui est la présence" (n. 53, PhP 492); Sartre calls consciousness an abstraction (nn. 54–57); and "*Au point de vue ontologique il n'y a pas deux régions d'être, mais une seule région, et la conscience [...] est justement cette région ontologique fondamentale. Sur le plan ontologique, la philosophie de la conscience ne fonde et n'autorise aucun dualisme.*" The paragraph is Henry's argument that the relation, not the terms, is concrete, built on Hegel, Merleau-Ponty and Sartre. SZ has a counterpart in its own critique of subject and object as "Vorhandene" (SZ 59–62, 132), which treats the relation as an in-being of Dasein and not as a region. Henry's step, that the relation is "la région ontologique fondamentale", is made in Sartre's and Merleau-Ponty's terms; SZ's term for the unitary phenomenon is *In-der-Welt-sein*, a "einheitliches Phänomen" (SZ 53), not a region.

Henry says that consciousness is "le commerce qui s'institue entre ces termes, elle est l'être de cet « entre »" (the commerce established between these terms, the being of this "between"; n. 58: "Cette définition de la conscience est acceptée par HEIDEGGER, cf. *SZ*, 132"); the "totalité concrète" is not "conscience + rapport + objet"; the being of the subject is the relation as such; "*Le sujet désigne l'événement ontologique qui fait accéder l'étant à la condition d'objet*"; Sartre: the knower "n'est rien d'autre que ce qui fait qu'il y a un *être-là* du connu, une présence" (n. 59, EN 225), knowledge is "la pure solitude du connu" (n. 61); "*Le sujet est l'être de l'objet*"; the dualism of subject and object is "le dualisme de l'essence et de la détermination ontique". Note 58 misreports SZ 132. SZ says: "Diese Auslegung käme dem phänomenalen Bestand schon näher, wenn sie sagte: das *Dasein ist das Sein* dieses »Zwischen«. Irreführend bliebe die Orientierung an dem »Zwischen« trotzdem" (this interpretation would come closer to the phenomenal facts if it said that Dasein is the being of this "between"; the orientation toward the "between" would nevertheless remain misleading). The reason follows: the "Zwischen" "ist schon als Resultat der convenientia zweier Vorhandenen begriffen", the prior positing of the two "*sprengt* immer schon das Phänomen" (is already conceived as the result of the *convenientia* of two present-at-hands; the prior positing bursts the phenomenon apart; SZ 132). SZ concedes that the formula is nearer to the truth and rejects it as misleading; Henry reports acceptance. It is Selection and silence of a kind that reverses the verdict: the sentence Henry needs is half of SZ's, and the other half contains the objection. The long paragraph then builds on a Sartrean formula ("le sujet est l'être de l'objet") that has no SZ basis, and the note gives it SZ's authority.

Henry says that, conceived as the relation, the subject is the establishment of a distance, the ontological power that unfolds the horizon, and "En tant qu'il est le Rapport, le sujet est l'Être-dans. L'Être-dans est un Être-dans-le-monde. Mais le monde n'est lui-même rien d'autre que l'Être-dans"; Hegel: "La conscience [...] est elle-même l'espace privé de déterminabilité" (n. 62, PhE I 331); "La spatialité de l'Être-dans est un titre pour le processus ontologique de l'aliénation", "*La lumière qui surgit dans cet intervalle est tout à la fois celle du monde et de la conscience*". This reverses SZ's order of founding. SZ distinguishes being-in from "die vorhandene Inwendigkeit eines Vorhandenen »in« einem anderen" (the present-at-hand inwardness of one present-at-hand in another), calls it "wesenhafte Seinsart dieses Seienden selbst", and says of spatiality that "die existenziale Räumlichkeit des Daseins [...] gründet selbst auf dem In-der-Welt-sein" (the existential spatiality of Dasein itself rests on being-in-the-world, SZ 132; likewise SZ 105: spatiality "nur möglich auf dem Grunde dieses In-Seins"). Henry's "Être-dans" is a pure spatiality, "le règne d'une présence qui s'accomplit sur le mode d'une spatialité pure", and being-in in SZ is not spatial at base. The capitalized "Être-dans" and "Rapport" carry the re-inscription.

Henry says that Husserl's statement that consciousness is nothing of the world (n. 63, MC 21) concerns only the totality of beings, so that consciousness is "le monde lui-même dans sa mondanité pure"; and "« Le phénomène du monde… appartient, comme moment structural essentiel de l'être-dans-le-monde, à la constitution fondamentale du *Dasein* »" (n. 64, SZ 209), whence the world belongs "à la structure de l'essence ontologique de la présence"; one may say the world is "subjectif", but this means "plus objectif que tout objet" (n. 65, SZ 366), its "transcendance comme telle"; "« La subjectivité du sujet, dit Heidegger, c'est l'*Erscheinen* lui-même »" (n. 66, H 134); "*La lumière qui nous traverse est celle du monde*" (n. 67, Beaufret); "*La subjectivité humaine est la transcendance du monde*." The three SZ-related quotations are accurate and each is cut. SZ 209 says that the world, as an essential structural moment of being-in-the-world, "zur Grundverfassung des Daseins gehört" (belongs to the basic constitution of Dasein), and that being-in-the-world is "ontologisch verklammert in der Strukturganzheit des Seins des Daseins, als welche die Sorge charakterisiert wurde" (ontologically clamped into the structural whole of Dasein's being, which has been characterized as care, SZ 209). Henry's inference that the world belongs to "l'essence ontologique de la présence" replaces Dasein by presence. SZ 366 is conditional: "Wenn das »Subjekt« ontologisch als existierendes Dasein begriffen wird, dessen Sein in der Zeitlichkeit gründet, dann muß gesagt werden: Welt ist »subjektiv«. Diese »subjektive« Welt aber ist dann als zeitlich-transzendente »objektiver« als jedes mögliche »Objekt«" (if the "subject" is understood ontologically as existing Dasein whose being is grounded in temporality, it must be said that the world is "subjective"; this "subjective" world is then, as temporally transcendent, "more objective" than any possible "object"). Henry drops the condition, the subject as Dasein grounded in temporality, and keeps the transcendence; the world's transcendence is "in der horizontalen Einheit der ekstatischen Zeitlichkeit gründend" (SZ 366), and SZ states that "Wenn kein Dasein existiert, ist auch keine Welt »da«" (if no Dasein exists, no world is "there" either, SZ 365). The H 134 quotation belongs to the GA 5 run (the essay's coinage *Subjektität* and the wording of the French). The Beaufret quotation is a Mediation: the identity of subjectivity and world is credited to commentators of Heidegger and to Henry's reading. The reading is a Selection and a Radicalization of SZ 366: transcendence of the world is kept and detached from the temporality of Dasein that makes it "subjective".

Henry says that the identification of the essence of consciousness with the pure exteriority of transcendence arises when the problematic of consciousness understands itself ontologically; the philosophy of consciousness "ne peut être qu'une répétition des présuppositions fondamentales du monisme"; the determination of consciousness runs from a subject of knowledge, through being as relation to the object (intentionality), to the claim that consciousness is "ce dépassement" itself, "l'Être-dans comme tel"; Sartre: "Toute conscience est positionnelle et *elle s'épuise dans cette position même*" (n. 69, EN 18); the essence of man "en direction de l'être est cette direction"; Sartre says Heidegger rejects "l'isolement mégarique et antidialectique des essences" (n. 70); vision "n'est préparée *intérieurement que par mon ouverture*… à un champ de transcendances" (n. 71, PhP 432); "« L'existence est *spatiale*, c'est-à-dire que par une nécessité *intérieure* elle s'ouvre sur un *dehors* »" (n. 72, PhP 339); "*La transcendance est l'existence universelle*. *L'existence de l'homme est l'existence des choses*. *La subjectivité du sujet n'est que l'objectivité de l'objet*." The paragraph is Henry's thesis built from Sartre and Merleau-Ponty. SZ stands against the last sentence as to order of founding. In SZ the thematizing that makes beings objects is a derived mode: "das objektivierende Entdecken dieses Seienden setzen schon Welt voraus" (objectifying discovery of this being presupposes world already, SZ 366), so that objectivity rests on the transcendence of Dasein and cannot be identical with its subjectivity. Henry's "existence" here is the existence of things, i.e. presence-at-hand's *existentia*, which SZ 42 distinguishes from Dasein's *Existenz*. The unit is a Radicalization and a Re-inscription, and SZ's text excludes its closing identification.

Henry says that the identity of the subjectivity of the subject and the objectivity of the object remained hidden because the objectivity of the object had not been raised to a problem; in Kant, where the possibility of the object is the theme, the being of the subject proves identical with the a priori form of the object, "l'être universel de tout objet possible", so that the agreement of the laws of thought and things "se fonde [...] dans l'identité essentielle de l'être du sujet et de celui de l'objet"; criticism of Kant as intellectualism misses "la signification ontologique profonde de l'Esthétique et de l'Analytique transcendantales", and the identity is established "au détriment de la conception d'un être propre et spécifique de la subjectivité du sujet", which is "réduite à l'objectivité". The result agrees with SZ's verdict on Kant in effect: the subject has no being of its own in Kant's analysis (SZ 24: the "ontologische Analytik der Subjektivität des Subjekts" is lacking, and the connection of time and the "I think" stays "in völliges Dunkel gehüllt"). SZ gives a historical reason (the taking over of Descartes's position and of the vulgar concept of time, SZ 24); Henry gives a structural one (the subject reduced to the objectivity of the object). The unit reconstructs SZ's finding by another argument. The text does not engage Kant's schematism, which SZ treats as the decisive place (SZ 23).

Henry says that the identity persists in transcendental philosophy as long as it keeps its first ontological design: Lachelier's necessity "constitue et définit solidairement l'existence même des choses, *et cela en un sens ontologique*"; existence "en tant que distincte de cette chose" (n. 73, Lachelier 54) is the ontological milieu, "sa vérité"; "La nécessité est l'être de l'étant"; the idea of necessity "est et [...] seule peut être le sujet de la connaissance" (n. 74). The paragraph is Lachelier, and independent of SZ.

Henry says that the identification of the human being's innermost being with the essence of things contradicts the moral aims of those who saw in the "subject" a way to assert man's supremacy over nature; Lagneau's recoil of the mind from its own being is "le mouvement même par lequel l'esprit se sépare de soi pour se manifester", and necessity belongs to the structure of the act "comme *le principe transcendantal qui en assure l'unité*": transcendence carries necessity in it "comme la condition interne de l'unité de son ekstase, c'est-à-dire comme *une structure éidétique de sa liberté ontologique*", and is "l'essence du sujet et celle des choses, [...] l'existence universelle". The paragraph is Lagneau's and independent of SZ except for one word. "Ekstase" is SZ's term for the temporal modes: "Zeitlichkeit ist das ursprüngliche »Außer-sich« an und für sich selbst. [...] Ekstasen der Zeitlichkeit" (temporality is the originary "outside-itself" in and for itself; the ecstases of temporality, SZ 329). Henry uses it for transcendence in general, without SZ's tripartite temporal sense.

Henry says that the transcendental philosophy's ontological significance can be lost in neo-Kantianism and in a classical philosophy that treats the subject as a free being, a privileged existent whose privilege is nevertheless grounded in "l'essence pure de la transcendance"; the conception of the subject has evolved toward an identity of the subjectivity of the subject and the objectivity of the object, and "La substitution finale d'un « sujet temporel » au sujet classique ne fait qu'affirmer l'interprétation ontologique implicite ou explicite de l'être de ce sujet comme transcendance. *La temporalité est l'essence de la transcendance. Elle est solidairement la subjectivité du sujet et « l'horizon de l'être ».*" This is a faithful reconstruction of SZ's architecture. Temporality is "der Sinn des Seins" of Dasein (SZ 17); it is the "ursprüngliche »Außer-sich«" (SZ 329); and the "Horizont [...] der ganzen Zeitlichkeit" (SZ 365) is the horizon of the understanding of being (SZ 17). The sentence also names the unity SZ's end-question asks about (SZ 437: "Offenbart sich die Zeit selbst als Horizont des Seins?"). The departures lie in the surrounding sentences. Henry reads the "temporal subject" as a late avatar of the classical subject's ontological interpretation as transcendence, whereas SZ presents the existential analytic as the first ontology of the subject (SZ 24, 46), and "transcendence" in SZ has three levels (being, Dasein's being, world; SZ 38, 366) that the sentence collapses. The verdict is that the sentence is Reconstruction and the frame is Henry's.

Henry says that the conception of a common being of the subjectivity of the subject and the objectivity of the object is the ontological ground of Hegel's demonstration of the identity of matter and spirit and of the traditional identity of thought and being. The paragraph is a statement of Henry's thesis in its Hegelian form and makes no SZ claim. SZ cites the identity of thought and being only through the Parmenidean *noein* (SZ 26), whose temporal structure it analyzes.

Henry says that grasping the essential identity of subject and object exposes the vanity of the debates between objectivity and subjective philosophy and the naivety of the behaviorists' critique (Watson, nn. 75–77); and on the other side, when Sartre reproaches Heidegger for approaching the existential analytic directly without passing through the cogito and asks: "Mais que serait une compréhension qui, en soi-même, ne serait pas conscience (d')être compréhension ?" (but what would an understanding be that was not itself consciousness of being understanding?; nn. 78–79, EN 115, with Henry's note: "Sans doute faut-il lire « existentiale »"), the reply is that for Sartre consciousness is the position of the object, is exhausted in it, "n'est ainsi rien d'autre que l'ekstase de la transcendance, c'est-à-dire très exactement ce que Heidegger entend par la compréhension *(Verstehen)* ontologique de l'être, sa critique n'a *aucun contenu*" (is nothing other than the ecstasis of transcendence, which is exactly what Heidegger means by ontological understanding; his criticism has no content). Henry defends Heidegger against Sartre by identification, and the identification is his. SZ answers the question Sartre puts in its own terms: understanding is self-transparent as "Durchsichtigkeit", "die Sicht, die sich primär und im ganzen auf die Existenz bezieht", which SZ chooses as the name of "Selbsterkenntnis" to say that it is not "das wahrnehmende Aufspüren und Beschauen eines Selbstpunktes", but "ein verstehendes Ergreifen der vollen Erschlossenheit des In-der-Welt-seins" (the sight primarily directed at existence as a whole; not a perceiving tracking and beholding of a self-point but an understanding seizing of the full disclosedness of being-in-the-world, SZ 146). It does not say that understanding is the position of an object or the ecstasis of transcendence. The "ekstatisch" belongs to temporality (SZ 329) and to the projection of understanding only through temporality, which SZ argues in its second division. The reply is therefore a Re-inscription of *Verstehen* into the vocabulary of the philosophy of consciousness, with the consequence Henry draws: that no difference separates the two.

Henry quotes Heidegger on the classical philosophy: "« La subjectivité [...] *est* la présence sur le mode de la représentation »" (n. 80, H 134), and says that representation rests on alienation, and the world is "ma représentation"; that "Le renversement de la doctrine qui se fait jour dans les dernières œuvres de Heidegger où le principe de la phénoménalité est cherché dans l'extériorité radicale de l'être, trouve ses prémisses dans *Sein und Zeit* et dans la pensée philosophique traditionnelle où il est en fait déjà inclus"; whether access to the transcendental goes through a philosophy of being or of consciousness matters little if the essence of manifestation is the same; and "*Il n'y a pas de différence entre la philosophie de la conscience et la philosophie de l'être.*" The H 134 quotation belongs to the GA 5 run (the essay's *Subjektität*; Henry's rendering "subjectivité"). The sentence on the reversal of Heidegger's doctrine is a claim about the whole development, with a premise in SZ that the text partly supports and partly excludes. For: SZ calls being the "transcendens schlechthin" (SZ 38), makes being and truth equiprimordial (SZ 230), and ends by asking whether time is the horizon of being (SZ 437), so that the direction of the question points beyond Dasein. Against: SZ says the world is "da" only while Dasein exists (SZ 365), and being "gibt es nur [...] solange Dasein ist" (SZ 230). The reversal itself is not in SZ. Heidegger described it later as a turn that his thought failed to say in the withheld third division of Part One; that text (the *Letter on Humanism*, 1947) is not in the corpus and is noted as retrospective. The verdict on Henry's claim is a defensible extrapolation on the premises it names and unsupported on the identification of SZ's temporality with "l'extériorité radicale de l'être".

### § 12. The critique of the philosophy of consciousness

Henry asks why Heidegger directed a radical and constantly renewed critique against the philosophy of consciousness if there is no difference between it and the philosophy of being; the motive is the will not to let the essence fall to the rank of the determination that finds its ground in it. The rejection of the concepts of "sujet, [...] subjectivité, conscience, raison, voire même [...] personne" (subject, subjectivity, consciousness, reason, even person), whose reality "demeure toujours en fait non questionnée dans son être" (always stays unquestioned in its being), "signifient la transcendance de l'être par rapport aux éléments qui sont pensés comme le principe de la phénoménalité" (signify the transcendence of being over the elements thought as the principle of phenomenality). The objection is SZ's, nearly in its list: Subject, soul, consciousness, spirit and person are "bestimmte, »ausformbare« Phänomenbezirke" used "mit einer merkwürdigen Bedürfnislosigkeit, nach dem Sein des so bezeichneten Seienden zu fragen" (determinate, "formable" phenomenal domains, used with a strange lack of need to ask about the being of the beings so named, SZ 46; cf. SZ 22). The reconstruction is faithful. What Henry adds is the conclusion: SZ's complaint is that the being of these titles goes unasked, and Henry reads it as an assertion that being lies beyond the determination posited as principle. The second statement follows from SZ 38 ("Sein ist das transcendens schlechthin") and not from SZ 46.

Henry asks why the essence cannot be kept in its original farness and why Dasein cannot be taken as the essence of transcendence, and answers: because it is understood from what happens in it. "L'Être-dans [...] « est dénaturé » parce que le *Dasein* le comprend à la lumière de l'étant qui se produit grâce à cet Être-dans" (n. 81, "Cf. *SZ*, 58"), and Dasein then takes itself as a being, subject and object are understood from the intraworldly being, and their relation as one more given reality. Heidegger rejects this "dégradation de l'essence de la manifestation en une réalité d'ordre ontique". Natural consciousness "pourrait s'appeler une conscience ontique" (n. 82, H 161) and "pose immédiatement devant elle son représenté *et son acte de se représenter* comme étant, sans considérer l'être que, ce faisant, elle représente aussi déjà" (n. 83, H 173; both treated in the GA 5 run); "La philosophie de la conscience est récusée en tant qu'elle est une thèse de la conscience naturelle"; "Penser l'existence dans sa vérité, c'est comprendre [...] l'existence comme la vérité. « La vérité de l'existence » [n. 84, SZ 221]"; "« Le *Dasein*, dit Heidegger, peut se comprendre… à partir du « monde » ou des autres ou de son pouvoir-être le plus propre »" (n. 85, SZ 221); understanding Dasein from its ownmost potentiality "est *la compréhension de soi de l'essence comme essence*". The first half reconstructs SZ 58–59. SZ says that Dasein's being-in is known "immer schon irgendwie" (always already somehow), that the knowing of the world stands in for the whole of being-in as its exemplary mode, and that being is understood "im ontologischen Anhalt am Seienden als innerweltlichem Seienden" (in an ontological hold on the being as intraworldly being), so that the relation is conceived "als Vorhandensein" (as presence-at-hand, SZ 59). The quotation marks around "dénaturé" do not mark an SZ word that I could find; SZ's words are "mißleitet", "mißdeutet" and "verdeckt" (SZ 58–59). The second half is where Henry departs. SZ 221 gives, as the third determination of the constitution of Dasein, the *projection*: "Dasein *kann sich* als verstehendes aus der »Welt« und den Anderen her verstehen oder aus seinem eigensten Seinkönnen. Die letztgenannte Möglichkeit [...] ist die *Wahrheit der Existenz*" (as understanding, Dasein can understand itself from the "world" and the others or from its ownmost potentiality-for-being; the last possibility is the truth of existence). It is an authentic mode of disclosure of an existing Dasein, the existentiell possibility that SZ works out as resoluteness in its second division. Henry turns it into "la compréhension de soi de l'essence comme essence", an ontological self-understanding of the essence. The step is a Re-inscription from the existentiell to the ontological, against the distinction SZ 12–13 draws, and the sentence "penser l'existence [...] comme la vérité" retains SZ's vocabulary for a doctrine in which existence is the truth of the essence, where for SZ it is the truth of the existing Dasein.

Henry says that the great critiques of "l'heideggerianisme", the critique of Cartesian ontology and of the traditional conception of truth, aim at the preservation of "la vérité de l'existence". Descartes retains of the being only what is accessible to mathematical knowledge, "l'être-donné-en-permanence *(ständige Vorhandenheit)*", the character of being of the mathematical being; substance has its origin in that determinate structure; Descartes interprets by it not only the mathematical being, but "indûment l'être de l'étant intramondain en général" and "*l'être du monde lui-même*", reduced to space and substance; "« Descartes, écrit Heidegger, comprend l'être du *Dasein* à la constitution fondamentale duquel appartient l'être-dans-le-monde de la même façon que l'être de la *res extensa*, comme substance »" (n. 87, SZ 98); "Descartes accomplit ainsi philosophiquement [...] l'inversion de l'effet de l'ontologie traditionnelle sur la physique mathématique moderne" (n. 86, SZ 96); "*La « res cogitans » et la « res extensa » sont deux titres équivalents où s'exprime la même déchéance [...] d'une même essence*"; "*Le dualisme cartésien est une altération du monisme ontologique*." The summary of SZ's critique of Descartes (§§19–21) is faithful: *ständige Vorhandenheit*, the mathematical being as exemplar, substance, and the identification of the world with the *res extensa* are SZ's. The two quotations are accurate in substance. SZ 98 reads that Descartes grasps "das Sein des »Daseins«, zu dessen Grundverfassung das In-der-Welt-sein gehört", in the same way as that of the *res extensa*, as substance, and SZ 96 says that he "vollzieht so philosophisch ausdrücklich die Umschaltung der Auswirkung der traditionellen Ontologie auf die neuzeitliche mathematische Physik und deren transzendentale Fundamente" (carries out philosophically and explicitly the switching-over of the effect of traditional ontology on modern mathematical physics and its transcendental foundations); Henry's "inversion" renders *Umschaltung* (a switching-over) with a stronger word. Two sentences are Henry's alone. "Le *Dasein* [...] n'est rien d'autre que le monde dans sa mondanité pure" is the identification of ¶16 of §11, which SZ 209 and 365 exclude. And "deux titres [...] d'une même essence (l'essence ontologique de la présence)" makes the two *res* equivalent in mode of being, which is SZ's point, and attributes both to one essence, which is Henry's.

Henry says that the critique of the traditional conception of truth as agreement between representation and object has the same sense: the proposition is a given reality that keeps a relation to the being it speaks of; when the relation is adequate there is truth; so "la représentation qui, en tant qu'ouverture et découverte de l'étant, est l'essence, déchoit au rang de la proposition"; the agreement that defines "truth" in classical philosophy "n'est que la déchéance de la vérité ontologique originaire de l'existence en tant que cette vérité réside dans l'ouverture et la découverte de l'étant" (n. 88, "Sur tout ceci, cf. *SZ*, 224 sq"). This reconstructs SZ §44 b, with a minor shift. SZ shows that the assertion becomes "ein innerweltlich Zuhandenes" (something ready-to-hand within the world) and that truth then "wird [...] zu einer vorhandenen Beziehung zwischen Vorhandenem (intellectus und res)" (becomes a present-at-hand relation between present-at-hands, SZ 224–225), so that "Wahrheit als Erschlossenheit und entdeckendes Sein zu entdecktem Seienden ist zur Wahrheit als Übereinstimmung zwischen innerweltlich Vorhandenem geworden" (SZ 225): "die ontologische Abkünftigkeit des traditionellen Wahrheitsbegriffes". The shape is the same. The shift is in the first term: SZ derives the agreement from uncoveredness and disclosedness of Dasein; Henry derives it from "la représentation [...] l'essence", from the essence in its Henrian sense (representation as opening and discovery). The reconstruction holds for the structure of the derivation and changes its origin.

Henry says that the same fall shows in Kant's refutation of problematic idealism: the very idea of proving the reality of the external world "implique la méconnaissance de l'essence originaire du monde comme tel" (n. 89: "C'est l'objection générale que Heidegger dirige contre la position même des « problèmes de la réalité »"); the world is confused with the totality of intraworldly reality, subjective representations and external events become two juxtaposed realities, and the relation between them a given relation. The objection is SZ's, in §43, and Henry's note identifies it correctly. SZ's point is that the question of the reality of the external world presupposes a clarification of what the world is: "innerweltliches Seiendes ist ontologisch nur zu begreifen, wenn das Phänomen der Innerweltlichkeit geklärt ist" (intraworldly beings can be grasped ontologically only if the phenomenon of intraworldliness has been clarified, SZ 209). Henry's formulation of the world's "essence" is again his; the reconstruction of SZ's objection to Kant is faithful.

Henry says that the meaning of Heidegger's critique is to refuse the fall of the essence into ontic reality: the problem is not "comment le sujet va au-dehors jusqu'à un objet" but "qu'est-ce qui rend ontologiquement possible que l'étant puisse survenir intramondainement et être objectivé comme survenant", and the answer is "la « transcendance ekstatiquement, horizontalement fondée du monde »" (n. 90, SZ 366); "C'est la transcendance, ce n'est pas le sujet, qui est l'essence." The critique has an ontological significance in thinking the essence in its purity, and, "pour cette raison précisément", none, since it works at the frontier of the ontological plane; "Ce n'est pas la structure interne de l'essence de la manifestation qui est mise en cause, c'est une compréhension, impropre *parce que d'origine ontique*, de cette *seule et unique essence de la manifestation*"; the philosophy of being "élabore l'essence sous-jacente aux philosophies antérieures"; "Il s'agit de penser l'extériorité dans sa pureté, au lieu de l'insérer dans un « sujet »". The quotation of SZ 366 is accurate and cut. SZ 366 asks the same question ("was ermöglicht es ontologisch, daß Seiendes innerweltlich begegnen und als begegnendes objektiviert werden kann?") and answers: "Der Rückgang auf die ekstatisch-horizontal fundierte Transzendenz der Welt" (the return to the ecstatically-horizontally founded transcendence of the world). What Henry cuts is the sentence before the answer: the transcendence of the world is founded in "der horizontalen Einheit der ekstatischen Zeitlichkeit" of Dasein, and "wenn kein Dasein existiert, ist auch keine Welt »da«" (SZ 365–366). The inference "c'est la transcendance, ce n'est pas le sujet, qui est l'essence" and the translation of "ekstatisch" into "extériorité" are Henry's. The verdict that Heidegger's critique has "aucune signification ontologique" because it only purifies the essence is Henry's thesis of the identity of the two philosophies (¶25 of §11). It treats SZ's rejection of the subject as an act within monism, where SZ treats it as the discovery of Dasein's temporal being. The unit is Reconstruction of SZ's question, Selection on its answer, and Radicalization of what the answer means.

Henry says that on the ground of the common essence the exchange of themes between the philosophy of consciousness and that of being takes place; the identification of the nothing with consciousness or man (n. 91, Sartre, EN 230: "Ce *rien* est la réalité humaine elle-même") is "sans doute absurde" but shows the permanence of the presuppositions; "l'insertion illégitime du fond essentiel de l'être dans la conscience réalisée comme région « sui generis » de l'être ne saurait [...] s'appeler une « subjectivation »", but an "étantisation" (a being-ization, rendering into a being) of the essence, since to speak of subjectivation "il faudrait [...] que la subjectivité soit une essence". The paragraph is about Sartre's transposition of Heidegger's themes, and its coinage "étantisation" is Henry's. SZ is independent of it, except that its own warning is the same one in other terms: "Jede Idee von »Subjekt« macht noch [...] den Ansatz des *subjectum* [...] *ontologisch* mit" (SZ 46). Henry's claim that subjectivity is not an essence anticipates his reversal in Section IV.

Henry closes the section: it is not nothing to maintain the essence in its radical transcendence over every being "si c'est justement dans cette transcendance que réside l'essence", and the more rigorous the understanding of the essence "comme fondement néantisant de l'être", the more incomprehensible "l'assimilation explicite de l'essence à une réalité ontique dans l'affirmation paradoxale mais sans cesse formulée selon laquelle le « Dasein » est un étant". The sentence attacks SZ's own wording at SZ 12, where Dasein is the being "das wir selbst je sind" and also "ein Seiendes, das nicht nur unter anderem Seienden vorkommt" (a being that does not merely occur among other beings). It pretends that SZ asserts that Dasein is a being as the objects are, whereas SZ says the contrary in the same sentence: Dasein's difference is the ontic priority of being at issue for it. "Fondement néantisant de l'être" is the vocabulary of *Was ist Metaphysik?*, not SZ. The paragraph is the question to which §13 answers.

### § 13. The ambiguity of "Dasein": essence and determination

Henry asks whether the fundamental ambiguity of Heidegger's *Dasein* means that the philosophy of being has not been faithful to the rigor of its ontological design, whether an inevitable fall bars access "au royaume de la présence compris dans sa pureté", or whether "le caractère inéluctable de cette chute" is "inscrit dans l'essence même" and must be understood "comme un caractère éidétique, une propriété de l'essence elle-même, et qui s'historialise en elle conformément à sa volonté propre". The question borrows SZ's structure of falling. In SZ falling is a structural determination of Dasein, not a moral or historical accident: Dasein "verfällt" to its world and to the tradition, which takes over its questioning and choosing (SZ 21–22), and the "Entfremdung" of falling is a mode of its being (SZ 178). Henry's move is to relocate the necessity of the fall from Dasein to the essence ("conformément à sa volonté propre"). That the essence has a will and a self-historicizing is Henry's. SZ's falling is a falling of Dasein into the world it is in, and "Verfallen" is not the essence's alienation.

Henry says that transcendence goes beyond every being, but that in transgressing it "l'essence obtient aussi le séjour qui lui est propre": "*Le séjour de l'essence est justement celui de l'existant. L'être est l'être de l'étant*"; to the question whether the transgression of beings is without return, the answer is given if it leads not beyond the existent but to "*l'endroit même*" where it stands; the "au-delà" is "*l'être même de tout existant comme tel*". This answers the question posed at §8 ¶14 and agrees in direction with SZ's return: universal ontology rests on the hermeneutic of Dasein "woraus es *entspringt* und wohin es *zurückschlägt*" (SZ 38), and "Sein ist jeweils das Sein eines Seienden" (SZ 9). The return differs in destination. In SZ it strikes back to Dasein's existence, the questioner (SZ 38, 13). In Henry it returns to the existent in general, the place of beings. The step from "being is always the being of a being" to "the abode of the essence is the existent's" is Henry's, and SZ 6 holds against it that "Das Sein des Seienden »ist« nicht selbst ein Seiendes".

Henry says that "l'être est l'être de l'étant" states "l'unité essentielle de l'être et de l'étant"; the thought that opposes them "ne pénètre pas encore dans le contenu"; the opposition arises from the immanence in the being of being, "c'est-à-dire du néant", and "Le pouvoir ontologique du néant est immanent à l'étant comme son fond le plus essentiel"; on the ground of the nothing in it the being is negated, held at a distance, "il apparaît, il est"; Hegel: "*la vie concrète de la déterminabilité* est… l'opération de se dissoudre" (n. 92, PhE I 48); "l'être est identique au néant. Mais le néant est l'être. Il est l'être de la détermination." The thesis is Hegel's with Heidegger's 1929 vocabulary, and it is the opposite of SZ's emphasis. SZ's ontological difference stresses that being is not a being (SZ 6), and asks the relation of being and beings openly ("Was es bedeutet: Sein »ist«, wo es doch von allem Seienden unterschieden sein soll, kann erst konkret gefragt werden", SZ 230). Henry settles the question in a Hegelian direction: the difference is a unity whose ground is the Nothing in the being. It is a Contestation of the primacy of the difference, made with Hegel and the Nothing of *Was ist Metaphysik?* and not argued from SZ.

Henry says that transcendence, in its radical overcoming of every being, is bound to it, since it constitutes the essence of the ontic determination and is therefore "essentiellement finie", a finitude "plus originelle que la finitude de la détermination qui en résulte" (n. 93); the "élan vers l'au-delà" is a "retour sur" the being, which it opens a place for; "*Toute transcendance est comme telle essentiellement réceptrice. Dans la réceptivité de la transcendance réside sa finitude la plus essentielle*"; "La transcendance est le besoin de l'étant"; being is "toujours cet acte d'« aller au-delà » et de « revenir sur »". Thesis and terms belong to the Kantbuch (finitude, receptivity, the "élan"/"retour", the companion run on GA 3); SZ is not cited. SZ's counterpart is the third moment of care: the structure of care as "*Sichvorweg* – schon sein in einer Welt – als Sein bei innerweltlichem Seienden" (ahead-of-itself, already being in a world, as being-with beings within the world, SZ 221) belongs to Dasein's being, so that Dasein is by its constitution tied to the beings it transcends, and SZ locates its finitude in originary time: "Die ursprüngliche Zeit ist endlich" (SZ 331). The agreement is in the claim that transcendence is bound to beings. The differences are in the subject of the claim (Dasein, in SZ; "la transcendance" as such, in Henry) and in the ground (temporality in SZ; the receptivity of Kant's pure reason in Henry). The unit takes up the Kantbuch's finite transcendence under the title of Heidegger's "Dasein" and does not distinguish the two.

Henry asks whether it is by chance that finitude is asserted for the first time, with the greatest force, in Kant: "« Kant, dit Heidegger, avait à chercher la finitude dans l'être rationnel lui-même »" (n. 94, K); the rational being is finite as having to find outside itself the being it must receive; "c'est la rationalité pure elle-même, en fait, qui est finie"; transcendence as receptive "constitue ainsi *en elle-même* la sensibilité comme telle et dans sa possibilité". The paragraph reports the Kantbuch (the GA 3 run) and is independent of SZ. SZ's remark on Kant is that insight into the problematic of time was denied him by his dependence on Descartes and on the vulgar concept of time (SZ 24). Henry's source for Kant's finitude is the Kantbuch, not SZ.

Henry says that the overcoming of the being is the act which maintains it in being, so the world constituted in that overcoming "n'est transcendant aux « phénomènes » que pour autant qu'il se trouve, en fait, rapporté à eux"; the transcendence of the world is finitude itself; the task of ontology is to understand the tie between transcendence and the phenomena; "« Dans le renversement, dit Heidegger, la conscience ne doit pas… abandonner le séjour au milieu de l'étant, elle doit l'assumer expressément dans sa vérité »" (n. 95, H 190; the GA 5 run treats the quotation). This is supported by SZ without citation. "Mit der faktischen Existenz des Daseins begegnet auch schon innerweltliches Seiendes. Daß dergleichen Seiendes mit dem eigenen Da der Existenz entdeckt ist, steht nicht im Belieben des Daseins" (with the factical existence of Dasein, intraworldly beings are already encountered; that they are uncovered with the Da of existence is not at Dasein's discretion, SZ 366), and the world is "weiter draußen, als es je ein Objekt sein kann" (further out than any object can be). The thesis that the world's transcendence is tied to what it discloses is thus a Reconstruction of SZ 366. The claim "la transcendance du monde est la finitude même" is again Henry's formula for SZ's "in den Grenzen seiner Geworfenheit" (SZ 366).

Henry asks whether the character of ontology's task does not clarify the necessity for it "de se donner un fondement ontique": because being is the being of beings, "l'interrogation sur l'être que promeut l'ontologie est toujours nécessairement et d'abord *une interrogation sur l'étant* qui se trouve questionné dans son être", and the finitude of the ontological procedure is one with that of being, so the necessity of an ontic foundation "n'est que l'expression sur le plan où la philosophie se constitue du lien indissoluble conformément auquel l'être est toujours l'être de l'étant". This is the answer to the problem raised in §6 ¶¶1 and 10, now argued from the finitude of transcendence. SZ states the necessity of the ontic rooting differently: "Die existenziale Analytik ihrerseits aber ist letztlich *existenziell*, d. h. *ontisch* verwurzelt" because philosophical questioning is itself a possibility of the existing Dasein that is existentielly seized (SZ 13). SZ's ground is that the question is a mode of being of the questioner; Henry's is that being is the being of beings. They reach the same consequence by different reasons, and Henry's reason lies in the Kantbuch and Hegel.

Henry asks what it means "au point de vue phénoménologique" that being is always the being of a being: "L'être désigne l'essence de la manifestation", so manifestation as such is always the manifestation of something that manifests itself; "l'essence de la manifestation ne l'accomplit pas vis-à-vis d'elle-même mais seulement à l'égard de la détermination qui survient par elle"; "*L'essence de la manifestation n'est pas la manifestation de soi. Ce n'est pas l'essence qui se manifeste, c'est l'étant*"; "Une manifestation, pensait déjà Bœhme, ne saurait manifester qu'autre chose"; the manifestation is "essentiellement finie". This is the place at which the argument turns against SZ's program. SZ asks what "fordert, Phänomen zu werden" and answers that it is the being of beings, which "zunächst und zumeist" does not show itself and which phenomenology must make show itself (SZ 35–36), and in the closing line ties truth and being as equiprimordial (SZ 230). Henry's thesis is that the manifestation as such cannot show itself, since it shows only the other. That is a Contestation of the possibility that SZ's phenomenology of being rests on. The ground is the finitude of transcendence (Kantbuch) and the structure of manifestation as alienation (§10); the paragraph does not meet SZ 35–36's claim that what is covered can be uncovered, except by denying that what is covered here (being as manifestation) has the form of something that can show itself. The next paragraphs complete the argument, and the verdict on it belongs at the end of the division.

Henry says that the essence is what makes being not only being-in-itself but being-for-itself: "L'essence est le pour-soi lui-même", yet in its act of appearing "ce n'est pas cet acte d'apparaître qui apparaît", so "le pour-soi n'est pas la manifestation de soi"; "C'est l'être-en-soi qui devient pour-soi [n. 96], c'est lui qui apparaît"; for Sartre the for-itself bears "une contingence insurmontable", being only the for-itself of the in-itself, "l'apparence de la détermination contingente"; "nous n'avons pas lieu de nous interroger sur la manière dont le pour-soi peut s'unir à l'en-soi" (n. 97, EN 712); Sartre's introduction, "commentant Heidegger": "L'être est simplement la condition de tout dévoilement : il est être-pour-dévoiler et non être dévoilé" (n. 98, EN 15), and "L'être du phénomène, quoique coextensif au phénomène, doit échapper à la condition phénoménale… et, par conséquent, il déborde et fonde la connaissance qu'on en prend" (n. 99, EN 16). The paragraph is a Mediation by Sartre, who reads Heidegger as saying that being is not revealed but is the condition of revealing. SZ says otherwise: the phenomenon in the phenomenological sense is the being of beings, "das Sichzeigende" (SZ 35–36), and being is what is to be made to show itself. Sartre's gloss ("être-pour-dévoiler non être dévoilé") is therefore a reading of SZ that SZ's text does not bear as stated. Henry uses it as an authority for his own claim that the essence does not appear.

Henry says that the conception of the for-itself as an autonomous essence arises only when its significance of being the essence is lost, as when it is taken as a being opposed to the in-itself; once the sense of the essence is not travestied and the for-itself is pure manifestation, "sa nécessaire référence à l'étant se fait jour aussi", the for-itself being nothing more than the appearance of the being; Sartre: "la connaissance n'est rien d'autre [...] que la présence de l'être au pour-soi et le pour-soi n'est que le *rien* qui réalise cette présence" (n. 100, EN 227); "C'est donc au moment même où l'essence est comprise dans sa pureté qu'est aussi compris le lien indissoluble qui l'attache à l'étant." The paragraph is a Sartrean argument and independent of SZ.

Henry says that since the being of the phenomenon withdraws from the phenomenal condition, "l'essence de la manifestation se dissimule dans le temps même où elle accomplit son œuvre", and this self-dissimulation is the self-manifestation of the being: "*La non-vérité de l'essence est la vérité de l'étant.*" The essence is only where the determination is; "la lumière de la manifestation ne brille pas ailleurs et pas autrement que sur l'étant qui se manifeste. Ce n'est pas, à vrai dire, la lumière de l'être qui brille sur l'étant, c'est l'étant lui-même en fait qui brille en elle, dans cette lumière qui n'est pas autre chose que son propre éclat"; Hegel: "La lumière [...] n'est vivifiante que si elle s'applique au différent d'elle-même" (n. 101); "*l'étant est ce qui manifeste l'essence de la manifestation.*" SZ's formal concept of phenomenon supports the second claim: *phainomena* are "was am Tage liegt oder ans Licht gebracht werden kann, was die Griechen zuweilen einfach mit τὰ ὄντα (das Seiende) identifizierten" (what lies in the day or can be brought to light, which the Greeks sometimes simply identified with *ta onta*, the beings, SZ 28–29): beings show themselves from themselves, and the light is what lets them become visible in themselves. It does not support the first. The "non-truth" in SZ belongs to Dasein, not to the essence: "Das Dasein ist, weil wesenhaft verfallend, seiner Seinsverfassung nach in der »Unwahrheit«" (SZ 222), and the covered beings "zeigt sich – aber im Modus des Scheins" (SZ 222). Henry makes the essence's self-concealment the condition of the being's manifestation, which is the 1943 Heidegger's structure of concealment and not SZ's falling. The unit combines a supported thesis with one that SZ puts elsewhere.

Henry says that the essence of manifestation can show itself only on and through the ontic determination, "L'essence pourtant n'est pas la détermination, elle n'est pas non plus le phénomène": the essence of pure phenomenality is "autre que son effectivité"; "En tant que la détermination manifeste l'essence, elle est sa vérité. En tant que l'essence se dissimule dans cette manifestation, [...] la vérité de ce contenu lui est étrangère, elle est [...] la non-vérité"; the truth of the essence is "la non-vérité de sa non-vérité". The paragraph is speculative-dialectical and independent of SZ. The relevant SZ text comes in the next unit. The closing formula has no SZ counterpart.

Henry says that to reach effectivity the essence had to alienate itself, "parce qu'elle se perd dans cette réalisation où elle se dissimule en se manifestant", so the determination is "l'énigme" (n. 103), "l'apparence *(Schein)* mais une apparence telle *(Erscheinung)* qu'en elle ce qui apparaît renvoie inévitablement à ce qui n'apparaît pas". The German words are SZ's, and the sense is split between SZ's. *Erscheinung* in SZ means exactly the structure Henry describes: "das Sichmelden von etwas, das sich nicht zeigt, durch etwas, was sich zeigt. Erscheinen ist ein *Sich-nicht-zeigen*" (the announcing of something that does not show itself through something that does; appearing is a not-showing, SZ 29). It is, in SZ, what holds in Kant's "bloße Erscheinung", where the reporting thing "als Ausstrahlung dessen, was es meldet, dieses gerade ständig an ihm selbst verhüllt" (as emanation of what it reports keeps this constantly veiled in itself, SZ 30). *Schein* in SZ is something else, a privative modification of the phenomenon: a being shows itself as what it is not (SZ 28–29). Henry's "Schein [...] telle (Erscheinung)" fuses the two and applies them to the determination. The essence then stands behind the appearing determination as what does not appear. SZ's own sentence excludes this structure for the phenomena of phenomenology: "»Hinter« den Phänomenen der Phänomenologie steht wesenhaft nichts anderes" (behind the phenomena of phenomenology there essentially stands nothing else, SZ 36). The unit is a Re-inscription of SZ's *Erscheinung* in the service of the two-tier structure SZ rejects for being, and it confirms that the essence of Section I stands where SZ's *Erscheinung* leaves its "Sich-nicht-zeigendes".

Henry says that, because the effective determination manifests the essence by hiding it, it can manifest the hiding only by disappearing: "La suppression de cette inégalité est la suppression de l'entité. La mort de la détermination est la manifestation de la manifestation pure"; the essence "ne parvient jusqu'à nous qu'à travers le temps des choses", and consciousness, "égarée en fait par elle", can only wander "d'expériences en expériences, d'objets en objets, sans trouver le repos" (n. 104). The paragraph is Hegel's *Phenomenology* (the way of despair) rewritten in the vocabulary of §§10–13, and it contains no SZ claim. SZ's death (the being-toward-death of Division Two, §§46–53) is Dasein's own and is not the death of a determination, a difference that matters for how Henry will later use "Nichtigkeit".

### § 14. The relation of essence and ontic determination in the philosophy of consciousness

Henry says that the two philosophies exchange problems as they exchange themes: the link of being and beings becomes the link of consciousness and thing, or subject and object; "*Le sujet se rapporte nécessairement à l'objet parce que l'essence de la conscience, identique à l'essence de la manifestation comprise selon les présuppositions fondamentales du monisme, se réfère inévitablement à la détermination comme à sa vérité*"; the classical saying that "le sujet ne se connaît que sur l'objet" perceives this; the object is "une réalité impliquée en fait dans cette essence comme ce qui lui permet seule de se réaliser", so the determination belongs to the inner structure of the essence. The paragraph carries no SZ claim and has one structural parallel. In SZ the third moment of care is being-with intraworldly beings ("als Sein bei innerweltlichem Seienden", SZ 221), so that Dasein's constitution includes the beings it is with; and self-knowledge, "Durchsichtigkeit", is not a perceiving of "eines Selbstpunktes" but a grasping of the disclosedness of being-in-the-world (SZ 146). Henry's "le sujet ne se connaît que sur l'objet" is nearer to the second than to Hegel's. What he adds is that the object is a condition for the essence of manifestation to realize itself, which has no counterpart in SZ's account of Dasein.

Henry says that Böhme shows the link: the opposition inside the absolute makes only "le pur espace pour une manifestation possible en général", an undifferentiated milieu with "aucune manifestation réelle"; the opposition signifies the rise of light only if it posits an element opposed to it, "la détermination ontique": "*L'opacité de la détermination ontique est dans l'opposition elle-même l'élément radicalement autre par lequel l'opposition se réalise*"; light is realized only in fire, "Dieu porte en lui un corps éternel" (n. 105, Koyré); "L'élément ontique est une structure éidétique de l'essence ontologique et pure de la manifestation." The paragraph is Böhme through Koyré and independent of SZ. Its last sentence states the thesis of the section: the ontic is an eidetic structure of the ontological essence. SZ's own distinction of ontic and ontological is a distinction between modes of inquiry and of being (SZ 11–13), which Henry's sentence treats as two elements in the essence.

Henry says that the belonging of the ontic element to the inner structure of phenomenality appears in Schelling's philosophy of consciousness: "la conscience est l'acte par lequel le sujet pensant se devient immédiatement objet" (n. 106, IT 34), "Arriver à la conscience et être limité, c'est une seule et même chose" (n. 107, IT 65), the pure essence appears only in a finite form; in the late philosophy the different element is "un être réel au sens de la détermination" (cf. n. 108, Jankélévitch 183), the creature's separation from God; "*la pure objectivation ne peut accomplir son œuvre que si ce qui survient en elle lui est étranger*." The paragraph is Schelling and independent of SZ.

Henry says that Hegel too understood alienation as requiring "le terme radicalement autre": "cette aliénation est encore imparfaite ; elle exprime le rapport de la certitude de soi-même avec l'objet qui justement parce qu'il est dans le rapport n'a pas encore gagné sa pleine liberté" (n. 109, PhE II 311), the "besoin nostalgique d'une réalité" (n. 110, CD 117) and "l'intrusion" of "un élément historique réel" (n. 111); the alienation constitutive of the Logos "ne devient effective que dans la nature". The paragraph is Hegel's texts, and independent of SZ.

Henry says that the essence of phenomenality is realized only through being-in-itself: "*La conscience est toujours la conscience de quelque chose*"; "*La conscience de soi est identique à la conscience*"; "L'être-là est l'apparence de la pensée" (n. 112, PhE II 267); "La détermination est la réalisation de l'essence en tant qu'elle en est le devenir-conscient" (n. 113). The paragraph is Hegel's. The vocabulary "l'être-là" (Hegel's *Dasein*) is not SZ's; the essay's different *Dasein* is the subject of guard 9 in the GA 5 run, and it stands apart here too.

Henry says that Fichte already posed the problem as that of the manifestation of "existence", and found the effective reality of pure existence in the determination: "Que lui advient-il lorsqu'elle se saisit ainsi ?" (n. 115, VB 158); "elle (l'existence) voit directement qu'elle est ceci et cela" (n. 116); and "Que renferme donc en cet état la conscience ?… Le monde, dit Fichte, et rien que le monde" (n. 117, VB 160), where "Par monde il convient d'entendre la somme de l'étant. *Rien d'autre ne se produit dans le devenir effectif de la phénoménalité que la détermination ontique et elle seule : la conscience effective est l'entité transcendante.*" The gloss on "monde" is SZ's first, ontic concept of world: "Welt wird als ontischer Begriff verwendet und bedeutet dann das All des Seienden, das innerhalb der Welt vorhanden sein kann" (world is used as an ontic concept and then means the totality of beings that can be present-at-hand within the world, SZ 64). Henry uses it correctly here and uses the other sense (worldhood, "mondanité pure") when he speaks in his own name (§§9, 11). The ontological sense in SZ is "ein Charakter des Daseins selbst" (a character of Dasein itself, SZ 64), which is what Henry's "Être-dans" claims to be. The two senses are SZ's and Henry keeps them apart in practice, which exposes the argument of §11 ¶17 (the world as pure Being-in, identified with the essence) to the point that SZ attaches the ontological sense to Dasein.

Henry says that the pure essence of phenomenality is not present as such in the real content of the appearance: "Ou bien, demande Fichte, la vie divine ne se trouve-t-elle pas immédiatement dans cette conscience ?… Non" (n. 118, VB 160); the pure essence is the objectivation itself, "la transformation comme telle", "La conscience absolue est justement par elle-même l'accomplissement immédiat et, pour cette raison, non plus conscient, de cette transformation" (n. 119); "*La conscience pure ne parvient pas dans la condition phénoménale*. *La conscience absolue est inconsciente*"; "toujours la forme nous voile l'essence" (n. 120, VB 177); Schelling: "l'activité qui limite n'arrive jamais à la conscience" (n. 121, IT 65). The paragraph is Fichte and Schelling. It states, as the philosophy of consciousness's result, what SZ states of the tradition's ontology on another ground: the unthematized guide stays covered "ohne jedes ausdrückliche Wissen um den dabei fungierenden Leitfaden" (SZ 26). Henry's formulation makes the covering a structure of the essence, and SZ's makes it a failure of the tradition's questioning.

Henry asks whether the manifestation of consciousness in its purity can be exhibited, and answers with Schelling: the philosopher knows the primordial act "par induction" (n. 122, IT 71), the transcendental point of view "ramène à la conscience et rend objectif ce qui [...] est absolument non objectif" (nn. 123–124, IT 9), so "le transcendantal ne peut s'apparaître que sous la forme de l'objet du sens interne" (n. 125), and "le devenir phénoménal de l'essence de la phénoménalité est l'autosuppression de cette essence pure"; "ballottée entre le psychologisme et la méthode réflexive", transcendental philosophy cannot give itself the reality of its essence. The paragraph is Schelling, and independent of SZ. It states the difficulty SZ faces at SZ 35–36 in a particular form: can what shows itself only as the condition of showing be brought to show itself? Henry answers no for the philosophy of consciousness; the answer for SZ is the object of the next sections.

Henry says that the essence of manifestation in classical philosophy carries a double exigency: consciousness must objectify itself to be effective ("une philosophie de l'effectivité"), and the essence must be preserved in its purity, so that its manifestation is inadequate ("l'essence dans sa réalisation phénoménologique est « autre chose encore »", n. 126, Koyré 243); the determination is "le chiffre ambigu, la simple apparence qui renvoie à autre chose"; "La finitude est la forme qui voile l'essence"; Fichte's freedom "déplace sans cesse la limite"; the philosophy of consciousness is "ballottée"; "Sa contradiction reste ce qu'elle était chez Kant lorsqu'il était dit que « nous ne connaissons que des phénomènes »"; transcendental freedom "n'est que la liberté de l'objet transcendantal = *x*"; "La philosophie de la conscience n'a pu sauver l'absolu qu'en le rejetant dans un arrière-monde." The structure Henry describes, appearing that refers to something that does not appear, is the one SZ assigns to Kant's "Erscheinung" in the sense of "bloße Erscheinung": "Ausstrahlung dessen, was es meldet, dieses gerade ständig an ihm selbst verhüllt" (emanation of what it reports, constantly veiling it in itself, SZ 30). SZ excludes this for the phenomena of phenomenology (SZ 36). Henry's description of the philosophy of consciousness's contradiction is in this respect SZ's own account of the Kantian structure, and the "arrière-monde" is its rejected form; the point to hold is that Henry's own essence of §13 had the same structure.

Henry asks whether the difficulties common to the two philosophies lose their significance as an obstacle if the essence is understood in its concrete character, in "l'effectivité de son être-réalisé": "ce n'est pas l'inévitable référence de l'essence à la détermination, c'est la prétention de saisir l'essence en dehors de cette référence et dans une prétendue pureté qui doit être mise en cause"; "L'essence pure est l'abstraction de l'être-présent"; "*C'est l'étant qui apparaît*"; "*l'unité indissoluble de l'élément ontique et de l'élément ontologique dans le devenir effectif de la phénoménalité*." "Si le *Dasein* ne désigne pas seulement l'abstraction d'une présence mais cette présence même dans son accomplissement réel, n'est-ce pas légitimement, alors, que la transcendance est qualifiée en lui, non pas sans doute comme « la propriété d'un sujet donné, » mais comme « la manière d'être essentielle de cet étant » (n. 127, SZ 132) *que le « Dasein » est aussi* ? Le droit de parler d'une « possibilité ontique de la compréhension de l'être » (n. 128, SZ 212) n'est-il pas fondé ? L'ambiguïté fondamentale du *Dasein* ne trouve-t-elle pas sa raison dans la structure interne de la phénoménalité effective ?" Both quotations are accurate and applied to a different subject. SZ 132 speaks of being-in, not of transcendence: "das In-Sein nicht als eine [...] Beschaffenheit eines vorhandenen Subjekts; das In-Sein vielmehr als wesenhafte Seinsart dieses Seienden selbst" (being-in not as a property of a present-at-hand subject but as the essential mode of being of this being itself). The identification of transcendence with being-in-the-world is Heidegger's of 1929 ("die Transzendenz als In-der-Welt-sein", GA 9 139), and it is a Mediation here. SZ 212 reads: "nur solange Dasein *ist*, das heißt die ontische Möglichkeit von Seinsverständnis, »gibt es« Sein" (only as long as Dasein is, that is, the ontic possibility of understanding of being, is there being), together with "die Abhängigkeit des Seins, nicht des Seienden, von Seinsverständnis" (the dependence of being, not of beings, on the understanding of being). The ambiguity Henry finds is SZ's thesis. SZ states that Dasein is "ontisch ausgezeichnet" in that it "ontisch *ist*" (SZ 12) and that the question of being has an ontic priority (SZ 13–14); the ontic possibility of the understanding of being is explicitly what Dasein is. Henry's reason for it (the inner structure of effective phenomenality, the essence's need of the determination) is his own. His rhetorical question treats SZ's double character as a defect that a theory of effective phenomenality justifies, where SZ treats it as the starting thesis. As a reading, it recovers SZ's doctrine and attributes to it a ground SZ does not give. As a verdict on SZ, the "ambiguity" is the item to be tested in §15.

### § 15. The ontological significance of the problematic aiming at the essence and the originary concept of finitude

Henry says that the ontological character of a problematic aiming at the essence means that the essence is by itself the condition of possibility of appearance; if the ontic element belongs to the inner structure of the act of appearing, it belongs to the essence and is ontological; so "C'est la définition même d'un tel élément comme ontique qui doit être mise en cause. C'est la distinction opérée dans le réel entre ce qui est ontique et ce qui est ontologique qu'il faut repenser"; "L'étant est-il oui ou non une condition de la manifestation ?" The ontic-ontological distinction is SZ's (SZ 11–13), and SZ does not draw it "dans le réel" as a division of the elements of a thing. It distinguishes inquiries and, in Dasein, two ways it is a being: ontic, as a being at issue for itself, and ontological, as understanding being (SZ 12–13). Henry's question to the distinction treats it as a split in the real that the structure of effective phenomenality abolishes. This is a Re-inscription of SZ's distinction as a distinction between two components of one phenomenon.

Henry calls "absurde" the thesis that the being is a condition of the effective being of the essence: "Comment l'étant pourrait-il être ce sur quoi l'essence pure de la manifestation devient visible à elle-même s'il n'était d'abord et déjà là ? Et comment l'étant pourrait-il précisément être là, c'est-à-dire apparaître, si l'acte d'apparaître considéré en et pour soi n'avait d'ores et déjà accompli son œuvre ?"; "Pour réaliser le devenir effectif de l'essence, l'étant vient toujours trop tard car il le présuppose"; the in-itself has "rien de commun avec le pour-soi" and its retreat into its night shows it is not "identique à l'être de la lumière"; "A la nature particulière de l'étant est indifférente la lumière qui l'éclaire"; "Le fait de se manifester est étranger au caractère déterminé de la détermination." In SZ, beings and the opening of the world are equiprimordial: "Mit der faktischen Existenz des Daseins begegnet auch schon innerweltliches Seiendes" (with the factical existence of Dasein, intraworldly beings already encounter it, SZ 366), and "Mit dem Dasein als In-der-Welt-sein ist innerweltliches Seiendes je schon erschlossen" (SZ 207). Henry's dilemma, that beings are either already there (and then the essence has no work) or only there through the essence's completed work (and then they come too late), treats the two as a sequence that SZ does not accept: SZ declines to ask which precedes, and SZ 212 holds that beings are in their being what they are independently of Dasein's existing, while being, not beings, depends on understanding. "Indifférente" is supported: SZ 366 says it is not at Dasein's discretion that beings are uncovered with the Da. The sequence in time ("d'ores et déjà", already) is Henry's. The unit rejects, with an argument from the structure of manifestation, the claim that SZ 13 and 366 make together, that the ontological analytic rests on a being that is among beings.

Henry asks whether the indifference of appearing to the content of what appears means the *Selbständigkeit* (independence) of the essence, and answers that the contingency of the content "n'exclut pas mais présuppose peut-être l'existence de celui-ci, quel qu'il soit", as Kant requires "un divers empirique *quelconque*" for any real knowledge, the universal category finding its use only through empirical intuition. The paragraph is Kant, and independent of SZ.

Henry says that the essence of manifestation is thought, by both philosophies, as opposition, in which the phenomenon arises as "posé devant"; "l'ambiguïté foncière du terme" must be denounced: "*Ce qui surgit par la médiation de l'essence de l'opposition dans un avant-plan de lumière et comme la condition du devenir effectif de la manifestation n'est pas l'étant, c'est cet avant-plan lui-même et comme tel, c'est l'horizon transcendantal de l'être*. *Dans le déploiement de l'horizon transcendantal de l'être s'épuise l'œuvre de l'essence*"; ontic knowledge presupposes ontological knowledge, "l'ouverture de l'horizon de l'être"; the "contrarium" of Böhme must be understood in "sa signification ontologique pure"; distance was first thought as "réelle", then understood: "l'être-éloigné comme tel [...] n'est pas l'étant", "Ce qui se tient à distance dans l'accomplissement originel de la transcendance est l'horizon du néant. C'est le néant, non l'étant, qui se trouve objecté dans l'objectivation"; "La transcendance ne s'objecte pas l'étant mais sa place." The pairing of ontic and ontological knowledge is GA 9 130–131. The horizon as the "whither" of an overstepping has an analogue in SZ: each ecstasis has "ein »Wohin« der Entrückung", "das horizontale Schema" (the horizonal schema, SZ 365). The agreement is in the structure (the horizon is what the transcending moves toward), and the differences are the three that §9 has already marked. In SZ the horizon belongs to Dasein's ecstatic temporality, not to an "opposition"; it is a schema of time ("Umwillen", "Wovor der Geworfenheit", the horizon of the present), not a place; and it is not a Nothing: "Der Horizont der ganzen Zeitlichkeit bestimmt das, woraufhin das faktisch existierende Seiende wesenhaft erschlossen ist" (SZ 365).
