"Avant-plan de lumière" echoes the *Holzwege* essay's "Vordergrund des Lichtes" (the GA 5 run, guard 11). The unit reconstructs the horizon as "whither" of transcending and replaces the temporal content by the Nothing and by opposition. It then repeats the claim that distance is "néant" (R §10), which §10 has shown rests on a reversal of SZ 105.

Henry says that the equivocity of the philosophy of consciousness lies in "objectivation": "*le départ n'est pas fait dans l'objet entre ce qui est ontique et ce qui est ontologique*"; the content is ontic, "sa condition d'objet dépend de l'essence"; "*Le devenir effectif de la phénoménalité dans l'être objectif de l'objet est le fait de l'essence et d'elle seule*. *A ce devenir effectif l'étant est totalement étranger : il n'y contribue pas plus qu'il n'en résulte*." SZ gives the direction of the derivation and not the exclusion. The objectifying discovery of beings presupposes the world ("Das faktische besorgende Sein bei Zuhandenem, die Thematisierung des Vorhandenen und das objektivierende Entdecken dieses Seienden setzen schon Welt voraus", SZ 366), so that objectivity is derivative of the opening of the world, as Henry says. SZ adds, in the same passage, that beings are encountered with the Da of existence and that what is discovered, in which direction and how far, "ist Sache seiner Freiheit, wenngleich immer in den Grenzen seiner Geworfenheit" (SZ 366). Beings thus contribute, in SZ, in the sense that the discovery is bound by thrownness to what is already there. Henry's "totalement étranger" goes beyond SZ.

Henry says that the confusion of the being with the objective conditions of phenomenality makes "la conscience crée l'objet" inevitable and paradoxical, because consciousness is identical in its being with the "être-posé-devant"; "*L'idéalisme absolu confond seulement l'étant avec les conditions effectives de la phénoménalité*"; the act of positing before is confused with the position of the being and thought believes it can deduce it; Marx: "la suppression idéale, qui laisse son objet exister dans la réalité, croit l'avoir vaincu réellement" (n. 129). The paragraph is Marx and Henry's reading of absolute idealism. SZ has a comparable verdict on idealism, and it is more favorable: the idealist thesis that being is "nur »im Bewußtsein«" expresses "das Verständnis davon, daß Sein nicht durch Seiendes erklärt werden kann", but it "baut [...] die Interpretation der Realität ins Leere" because it leaves unasked what the understanding of being is ontologically (SZ 208), and "nachträgliche phänomenologische Verbesserungen des Subjekts- und Bewußtseinsbegriffes" do not provide the ground (SZ 207). Henry's position is closer to the first part of SZ's judgement (the idealist insight) than to the second (the demand for an analytic of consciousness's own being). That demand returns in §28 of the book as the phenomenology of the ground.

Henry says that the non-belonging of the being to the effective becoming of phenomenality reopens the problem of finitude: "*C'est justement dans son rapport à l'être comme tel que la finitude doit être comprise*"; knowledge is finite because it is receptive, because its content "n'est pas ce qu'elle s'objecte"; but the being is received as object, so "le problème de la réceptivité de la connaissance à l'égard de l'étant est celui de sa réceptivité à l'égard de l'objet"; the finitude of ontic knowledge is that of ontological knowledge. The thesis and its terms (*Hinnahme*, receptivity, object) belong to the Kantbuch. SZ is independent of the paragraph and has a different treatment of finitude, which locates it in originary temporality: "Die ursprüngliche Zeit ist endlich" (SZ 331).

Henry says that what ontic knowledge needs for receiving the being, ontological knowledge creates: "C'est dans l'acte propre de la transcendance que surgit l'horizon transcendantal de l'être"; this knowledge is finite "parce que l'horizon de la transcendance est lui-même fini"; "*C'est la place où surgit la lumière, c'est la place elle-même comme telle qui est finie*. La lumière est cette finitude d'une place"; "La finitude est une structure éidétique de l'essence de la phénoménalité." The finitude of a place is not SZ's. SZ's finitude is that of originary time, tied to the end of Dasein, and it is explicitly not that of time that "weiter geht" (goes on, SZ 330–331). Henry's spatial finitude is the horizon-as-place of §9; he states a thesis about the finitude of the essence that SZ does not make.

Henry quotes Heidegger: "« Ce dont il s'agit au fond [...] c'est de mettre en lumière l'imbrication essentielle de l'être (non pas de l'étant) comme tel et de la finitude dans l'homme »" (n. 130, K 278), and concludes that "ce qui est fini, c'est l'être lui-même": "*Le lien qui unit dans l'origine la transcendance et la finitude n'est pas le lien de l'être et de l'étant*"; "Ce n'est pas l'étant, c'est l'objet qui est fini"; the being is the *Enstand* of infinite intuition and the *Gegenstand* of receptive knowledge; "*L'étant est fini sur le fond de la finitude de l'être en lui*." The quotation is from the Kantbuch (the GA 3 run). Its use reverses §13 ¶4, where finitude resulted from the being's tie to the existent ("l'être est l'être de l'étant"); here that tie is denied as the ground of finitude. SZ 212 is close to the new thesis in one respect: what depends on Dasein's understanding of being is being, not beings. It differs in what the dependence says. Dependence of being on understanding is not finitude of being, and the finitude of SZ belongs to temporality (SZ 331).

Henry says that the finitude of transcendence results from its being receptive, but "Ce que reçoit la transcendance n'est rien d'autre [...] que ce qu'elle forme elle-même"; Heidegger: freedom "ne peut se dérober à ce qui prend d'elle ainsi naissance" (n. 131, WG 109); what is born in ontological freedom is "l'horizon transcendantal de l'être, c'est le monde lui-même dans sa mondanité pure", to which freedom is delivered, "à un monde fini"; "La limite de la transcendance n'est donc pas l'étant. Ce n'est pas l'étant qui, comme « limite », « obstacle » et « arrêt » permet à la liberté de prendre conscience de soi"; "C'est l'existence elle-même qui est limitée [...] parce que la transcendance qui constitue son essence porte en elle cette limite comme ce qu'elle produit" (n. 132). The quotation is from *Vom Wesen des Grundes*. SZ states the limit of freedom the other way: what Dasein discovers and discloses, and how, is the business of its freedom, "wenngleich immer in den Grenzen seiner Geworfenheit" (SZ 366), and thrownness is "dem Faktum der Geworfenheit in das Da überantwortet" (SZ 148), a limit Dasein did not produce. Henry's limit is self-produced; SZ's is factical. The contrast is exact, and it decides how finitude is understood in each: for SZ finitude includes what is not in Dasein's power; for Henry it is what the transcendence produces. This is a Contestation made under the authority of a Heidegger of 1929.

Henry says that Husserl had already understood that finitude has its ultimate ground in the structure of representation: in *Erfahrung und Urteil* nature, the only absolute substrate, is never "le thème d'une saisie simple" (n. 133, EU 159); the finitude of intuition itself, "finie en elle-même en tant qu'elle se produit toujours à l'intérieur d'un horizon fini", is responsible; "Pourtant son horizon de déterminabilité indéterminée est toujours essentiellement donné avec lui" (n. 134, EU 259); "*Loin d'être le principe de la finitude de la connaissance, l'infinie diversité de l'étant en est la simple conséquence*"; "Comme l'horizon est au-delà de l'étant, l'étant est au-delà de l'horizon"; "L'horizon des déterminations ontiques trouve son fondement dans l'horizon transcendantal de l'être." The paragraph is Husserl, and independent of SZ. It reads Husserl's horizon of unfulfilled intentions (see Introduction §3 ¶5) as an instance of the horizon of being, the shift from one sense of "horizon" to the other that the Introduction had begun.

Henry concludes that the ontological character of finitude returns thought to the problematic aiming at the essence and confirms that the becoming of phenomenality "s'accomplit sans la médiation de l'étant", and that finitude does not find its principle in the being either, "cela confirme l'indépendance radicale de ce devenir, c'est-à-dire de l'essence dans sa réalité effective, à l'égard de toute détermination ontique." The conclusion sets against §14 ¶10 and §13 a thesis that the essence is independent of every ontic determination, after having argued there that the ontic belongs to its inner structure. The two are reconciled only by Henry's distinction between "l'étant" and "l'objet", the determination as a content and as an *ob-jet*: the object belongs to the structure, the being does not. The distinction is not SZ's. It is the point where SZ's thesis of Dasein's tie to beings (SZ 221, 366) and its dependence of being on understanding (SZ 212) are redescribed as a doctrine of the essence's independence, and the next section draws the formal consequence.

### § 16. The idea of the formal structure of the autonomy of the essence and the task of a repetition of the ontological elucidation of the concept of phenomenon

Henry says that the radical independence of the essence's effective reality from the ontic determination must now be thought: the essence is the essence of manifestation, its effective becoming is "la manifestation qui se manifeste, sa réalité"; this reality cannot find its condition in the being, because the being can manifest the manifestation only if it manifests itself; the effective becoming of phenomenality lies in the opening of the transcendental horizon of being, "*c'est que cet horizon se montre*. *L'horizon de l'être considéré dans sa pureté doit être perceptible en et pour soi*. *L'être doit pouvoir se montrer*"; the essence is autonomous because "le devenir phénoménal se réalise dans l'essence et par elle". The demand that the horizon show itself is an extension of SZ 35, where the phenomenon in the eminent sense is what "fordert, Phänomen zu werden", and of SZ 17, where time, the horizon of all understanding of being, "muß [...] ans Licht gebracht und genuin begriffen werden" (must be brought to light and genuinely grasped). In SZ the horizon is brought to light by the analytic, as a task, and is not claimed to show itself in the act that opens it: in the opening, Dasein's understanding of being stays "nicht ontologisch begriffen" (SZ 147–148: "Sein ist im Entwurf verstanden, nicht ontologisch begriffen"). Henry's inference from the opening to the self-showing of the horizon ("c'est que cet horizon se montre") converts SZ's task into an ontological necessity. The sentence "L'être doit pouvoir se montrer" is the one he gave at §7 ¶5 as a paraphrase of SZ's view; it is now the premise of the autonomy of the essence.

Henry says that the autonomy of the essence means the *Selbständigkeit* of ontological knowledge, which is the condition of possibility of ontic knowledge; that being is always the being of beings is not excluded, "du moins ne pouvons-nous pas le dire maintenant"; the manifestation of the being presupposes the becoming of manifestation in the pure work of the essence; "Si l'étant peut se manifester sur le fond du néant, c'est seulement et d'abord parce que le néant se manifeste. « *Parce que le néant est révélé*, dit Heidegger, la science peut faire de l'étant lui-même l'objet de sa recherche »" (n. 135, Corbin, *Qu'est-ce que la métaphysique ?*, 42); "« Pour que l'on puisse comprendre l'essentielle détermination de l'étant par l'être, il faudra, dit Heidegger, que l'élément déterminant lui-même se montre avec une certaine clarté »" (n. 136, K 279); the finitude of the being in its objective condition "présuppose comme sa condition *la manifestation effective de l'essence dans sa pureté*". Both quotations are from texts other than SZ (*Was ist Metaphysik?* and the Kantbuch), and the second concerns the Kantian determination of beings by being, not a demand for the self-showing of the horizon. SZ has an analogue to the first quotation in its analysis of anxiety, where the "Nichts und Nirgends" is the disclosure of the world as such (§40): anxiety is anxious about, and in the face of, "das In-der-Welt-sein" (being-in-the-world, SZ 188; the analysis of anxiety is §40, SZ 184–191). It is an affective disclosure of Dasein's being, and not a revelation of the Nothing as a phenomenon of being. Henry takes the Nothing as the horizon that shows itself, and the premise from which he derives "l'expérience du néant" is the Heidegger of 1929. The unit is a Mediation by *Was ist Metaphysik?* and the Kantbuch, and it states the consequence on which Section II builds, that the horizon is itself a phenomenon.

Henry says that understanding ontological knowledge as the condition of ontic knowledge does not injure the *Selbständigkeit* of the essence if the manifestation of beings presupposes that of the horizon; the manifestation of the horizon signifies "l'immanence du devenir phénoménal à l'essence de la phénoménalité", in which the *Selbständigkeit* lies and which founds the right to call it an essence; the origin of this right must be clarified. The paragraph is Henry's formal definition of the autonomy, and independent of SZ. The word "immanence" appears here as a formal predicate of the essence and nowhere in SZ in this sense.

Henry says that the meaning of the essence's autonomy is first "de rendre plus incompréhensible la désignation du *Dasein* comme un étant": "Si le *Dasein* désigne la réalité effective de la manifestation, l'appartenance de l'élément ontique à cette réalité [...] a été exclue. Le devenir effectif de la manifestation est l'être-là. A ce devenir effectif qui se produit sans lui et avant lui, l'étant n'a point part. L'étant est étranger à l'être-là comme tel. Le *Dasein* comme tel est ontologique." The conditional is the hinge. If *Dasein* means the effective reality of manifestation, as Hegel's *Dasein* does (determinate existence, presence), it cannot be a being. SZ's *Dasein* is the other thing: "dieses Seiende fassen wir terminologisch als *Dasein*" (SZ 7), the being that is in each case ours, whose being is at issue for it and which is ontologically distinguished by being ontic (SZ 12). The contradiction between §14 ¶10, where the ambiguity of *Dasein* was justified by the structure of effective phenomenality, and this paragraph, where it is made "plus incompréhensible", is Henry's, and the resolution is a change of the word's meaning from SZ's being among beings to the effective becoming of manifestation. The unit is a Re-inscription of *Dasein* that cancels SZ's definition, and the reading of "Dasein" as "l'être-là" is the GA 5 run's guard 9 applied to SZ.

Henry says that the prohibition against understanding *Dasein* as a being is only a negative consequence of the autonomy; the positive meaning lies in "l'impossibilité de séparer l'essence comprise dans sa pureté et, d'autre part, le devenir phénoménal où elle se réalise"; the phenomenal becoming is included in the essence, which "trouve en elle-même sa réalité". The paragraph is a statement of the thesis and independent of SZ.

Henry says that the positive meaning of *Selbständigkeit* refers to the problem of its possibility; it means that the phenomenal becoming is immanent to the essence, expressed in the claim that the horizon opened by the essence "se manifeste comme tel et dans sa pureté": "*Avec la manifestation de l'horizon, l'être se montre. Le problème est celui de la possibilité de la manifestation de l'horizon. Cette possibilité réside dans l'essence de la manifestation. [...] Ce fondement, c'est l'essence elle-même. Le problème du devenir phénoménal de l'essence de la phénoménalité est justement le problème de la structure interne de celle-ci.*" The question is SZ's, how being can become phenomenon, and the answer differs. SZ's ground of the possibility is Dasein's pre-ontological understanding of being (SZ 12–15), which the analytic radicalizes and which the circle presupposes (SZ 7–8, 152–153). Henry's ground is the essence itself, as self-grounding. The structure of the question and the mention of "structure interne" belong to the formal task SZ gives to the analytic; the move that the ground is the essence and not an understanding is Henry's, and it replaces SZ's circle with an immanence.

Henry says that the essence of phenomenality finds its reality in itself since the phenomenality is produced in it, and that this is the positive meaning of *Selbständigkeit*; that the effective phenomenality is produced in the essence is possible only by it, so the meaning of *Selbständigkeit* finds its possibility in the essence: "C'est en ce sens ultime que l'essence est autonome." The paragraph is a formal restatement and independent of SZ.

Henry says that only the elucidation of the ground of the immanence of the phenomenal becoming to the essence can tell whether the becoming coincides wholly with its ground, "*si l'essence originaire et pure est la vérité ou si elle est aussi la non-vérité*"; once the being is excluded as a power to manifest or hide the essence, the double possibility must be asked of the essence. The question echoes SZ 222–223 (Dasein "gleichursprünglich in der Wahrheit und Unwahrheit") and §7 ¶7 of the Introduction, transferred from Dasein to the essence, and it prepares the claim of §13 that the essence is non-truth. SZ's text puts the double possibility in Dasein's falling, and Henry asks it of the essence. The paragraph is a preparation, and independent of SZ except for that transfer.

Henry concludes that the task of phenomenology was defined as the ontological elucidation of the essence of the phenomenon; this elucidation finds its possibility in the essence, as does manifestation in general; "La première élucidation de l'essence du phénomène poursuivie selon les présuppositions ontologiques fondamentales du monisme a du moins montré que, pour accomplir son œuvre, l'essence de la manifestation devait pouvoir se manifester. « L'être doit pouvoir se montrer. » La compréhension de cette possibilité exige que soit répétée l'élucidation ontologique de l'essence du phénomène. La tâche de la répétition [...] est la mise en lumière de la possibilité de la manifestation de l'essence" and "met en cause les présuppositions ontologiques fondamentales du monisme et nous introduit à l'essence originaire de la révélation." The form is SZ's. A first, preparatory elucidation is repeated at a higher level, and the repetition determines the first: "Ist dieser erst gewonnen, dann verlangt die vorbereitende Analytik des Daseins ihre Wiederholung auf der höheren und eigentlichen ontologischen Basis" (once the horizon is won, the preparatory analytic of Dasein requires its repetition on the higher and genuine ontological basis, SZ 17), and SZ's own plan of repeating the preparatory analysis by temporality (SZ 17). The content differs. SZ's repetition goes from the preparatory analytic to temporality and remains a way within the question of being; Henry's goes from the first elucidation to a new essence of revelation that questions the monism, and so leaves the question of being as SZ posed it. The unit is a Reconstruction of SZ's method of repetition used to carry out the departure.

The division's closing verdict follows. What Henry's Section I does with SZ is to take from it its question, its concept of phenomenon as the thing to be elucidated, and its finding that Dasein is both a being and the place of the understanding of being, and to build on them an argument that no longer follows SZ. The reconstructions hold where he cites the text and are faithful: Descartes's substance-ontology and the world as *res extensa* (SZ 96–98), the derivation of truth as agreement from uncoveredness (SZ 224–225), the priority of space in the world (SZ 111), the objectification as derived (SZ 366), and above all the "ambiguity" of *Dasein*, which SZ itself states as the ontic-ontological priority (SZ 12–14, 212) and which Henry rightly makes the pivot. The readings fail where they matter most. He reverses the direction of *Ent-fernung*, which SZ 105 defines as a nearing, and builds "phénoménologique distance" on the reversal. He merges *Erscheinung*, *Schein* and *Phänomen* in "apparaître", against SZ 29–31 and 36, and then gives the essence the structure SZ assigns to Kant's "bloße Erscheinung". He cites SZ 132 as accepting consciousness as the being of the "between" where SZ rejects the formula as misleading, SZ 366 without its condition (the subject as Dasein grounded in temporality) and SZ 209 with "essence de la présence" in place of Dasein, and SZ 230's *gleichursprünglich* as "contemporains". He takes presence for the name of being, the determination SZ 25–26 traces to the Greek understanding. The larger displacement is of the subject of transcendence: from Dasein, whose finitude is temporal and whose freedom is limited by thrownness (SZ 331, 366, 148), to an essence whose finitude is that of a place and whose limit it produces, and from the horizon as time to the horizon as opposition, distance and the Nothing, with Hegel, Fichte, Schelling, Sartre and the Heidegger of the Kantbuch and of 1929 as the sources of the vocabulary. The resulting section does not misread SZ throughout. It contests it, and the contestation is stated openly only in the final question, whether the essence can show itself, which SZ answers with its phenomenology and Henry denies for the monism. The verdict remains local: the thesis of the autonomy of the essence (§§15–16) conflicts with Henry's own claim that the ontic belongs to the essence's structure (§§13–14), and SZ's text (212, 221, 366) supports the second against the first.

## Section II. Repetition of the elucidation of the concept of phenomenon: transcendence and immanence

Henry's Section II cites the *Holzwege* essay (H) throughout §§17–21 and the Kantbuch (K) throughout §§22–36; the first is the subject of the GA 5 comparative run and the second of the GA 3 run. This run treats the SZ side of each unit: what SZ says at the loci the unit relies on, silently or by name, and where Henry's use of the other two texts bears on a claim about SZ. Henry's own SZ citations in Section II are few (nn. 62, 82, 90, 104), and he also cites two post-SZ texts of Heidegger that are not in the corpus: *Über den Humanismus* (1949, n. 107) and the 1949 introduction to *Was ist Metaphysik?* in Rovan's translation (n. 91).

### § 17. The originary character of the manifestation of being and the problem of natural consciousness

Henry says that the claim "l'être doit pouvoir se montrer" is ambiguous when tied to the methodological work of phenomenology as elucidation, which makes the possibility of being's showing depend on that work ("Ce qui doit être élucidé est ce qui tout d'abord se cache"); the first result of the elucidation was the need to dissociate the work from "le surgissement de l'essence dans l'effectivité de sa condition phénoménale": "*La manifestation de l'être, loin de pouvoir être une simple conséquence du travail méthodologique d'élucidation de la phénoménologie en est au contraire la condition*" (n. 1: §8); it is the "déjà" of its primitive condition, not the "enfin" of the accomplished work, "l'absolu". The "déjà" is SZ's. Understanding of being is "je schon" at work in every comportment ("Wir bewegen uns immer schon in einem Seinsverständnis", SZ 5), and phenomenology presupposes it as the "vorgängige Hinblicknahme" it radicalizes (SZ 8, 15). To that extent Henry restates SZ's thesis that the work of the analytic is conditioned by an understanding it does not produce. The two departures are those already marked. "Tout d'abord se cache" renders SZ 35's "zunächst und zumeist" as a time-order, which fits the opposition "déjà / enfin" Henry builds on it, and the condition is named "l'absolu", where SZ's is Dasein's pre-ontological understanding.

Henry says that the proposition "pour que l'on puisse comprendre l'essentielle détermination de l'étant par l'être il faudra que l'élément déterminant lui-même se montre avec une certaine clarté" (n. 2: §16; K 279) is equally ambiguous: it places the manifestation of being in the future, whereas "*La détermination de l'étant par l'être se réalise pourtant antérieurement à la compréhension de cette détermination par le philosophe*" and presupposes the manifestation of being; "la manifestation de l'être est originaire." The sentence is the Kantbuch's, about Kant, and the tense Henry corrects is his reading of "il faudra". SZ has the structure that Henry restores: the determination of beings by being is lived before it is conceptualized, in the pre-ontological understanding of the beings one deals with (SZ 12–13, 15). The passage adds nothing to the comparison with SZ beyond the fact that the "originary manifestation of being" is a name for that understanding.

Henry says that the determination of the being by being expresses the dependence of what appears on the act of appearing; "*L'être se manifeste à la conscience naturelle*" as what lets it be a consciousness aimed at beings; "La réalité de la relation est sa manifestation"; the manifestation of being is the manifestation of the absolute, whose "absoluité [...] est la Parousie"; natural consciousness "est déjà la connaissance absolue, la science qui « à son premier pas parvient dans la Parousie de l'absolu, c'est-à-dire est près de son absoluité »" (n. 3, H 126; GA 5 run). The SZ side is that *parousia* is for SZ the Greek name of being as presence: "die Bestimmung des Sinnes von Sein als παρουσία, bzw. οὐσία, was ontologisch-temporal »Anwesenheit« bedeutet" (SZ 25). Henry's Parousia names the absoluteness of the absolute, the same determination of being as presence that SZ treats as the tradition's unthought temporal sense. He takes the word from the essay, which reads Hegel's "près de nous", and thereby turns the object of SZ's critique into the name of the absolute. The claim that the relation of consciousness to beings is itself its manifestation ("La réalité de la relation est sa manifestation") is Henry's.

Henry says that natural consciousness lives in the Parousia from its first step; "*La conscience est elle-même comme telle la manifestation de l'être*"; it need not be led to the place where the manifestation occurs, it is that place; "« La conscience naturelle ne peut être introduite là où elle est déjà »" (n. 4, H 190), "Voilà pourquoi il n'y a pas d'introduction à la phénoménologie. La phénoménologie est la phénoménologie de l'esprit. *La phénoménologie de l'esprit est la conscience elle-même.*" "Comment sommes-nous dans la Parousie de l'absolu ? Nous y sommes selon l'habitude de la conscience naturelle" (n. 5, H 189). SZ's method begins where Dasein already is: the analytic is to show the being "wie es *zunächst und zumeist* ist, in seiner durchschnittlichen *Alltäglichkeit*" (as it is proximally and for the most part, in its average everydayness, SZ 16), and Dasein is ontically nearest to itself and "vorontologisch doch nicht fremd" (not alien pre-ontologically, SZ 16). On that Henry and SZ agree: no leading in from outside. SZ adds what Henry's "il n'y a pas d'introduction" drops: Dasein is "ontologisch am fernsten" (ontologically farthest), so that a thematic access to its structures needs an analytic with its own method (SZ 15–16). The equation of phenomenology with consciousness itself ("la phénoménologie de l'esprit est la conscience") is Hegel's, through the essay, and SZ makes phenomenology a method of access, not a property of the being it treats (SZ 27, 35).

Henry says that the essence of consciousness is the Parousia, "la présence dans sa présence, l'être-présent comme tel en tant qu'il est lui-même présent"; immediate manifestation of beings presupposes this "présence immédiate de la présence"; Hegel's statement that "la manifestation immédiate de la vérité est l'abstraction de son être-présent" (n. 6, PhE I 198) must be rejected, because the concept is present in the manifestation of beings and cannot be abstracted. The unit stakes the point on presence, the concept SZ 25–26 criticizes. Henry's "présence de la présence" is not SZ's. SZ's pre-ontological understanding is of the being of beings, in a world, and no "present presence" appears in its place. Hegel's remark and its rejection are matters for the GA 5 run.

Henry says that "l'être doit pouvoir se montrer" means that it shows itself, "non pas au terme d'un processus ou d'une histoire, mais originairement"; "ce qui est d'abord présent, ce n'est pas l'étant, mais l'être-présent lui-même et comme tel", which attests "le caractère ontologique de l'origine", and that it is present attests "le caractère phénoménologique de l'origine"; Hegel on the religious community's return to origin confuses "l'origine, comme l'être-là immédiat de la première manifestation, avec la simplicité du concept" (n. 7, PhE II 271–272). The reading contradicts SZ 35. SZ's sentence is that what must become phenomenon "sich zunächst und zumeist gerade *nicht* zeigt" and may be covered "so weitgehend [...], daß es vergessen wird und die Frage nach ihm und seinem Sinn ausbleibt" (so far that it is forgotten and the question of it and its meaning is left out, SZ 35). Where Henry says that being is what is first present, SZ says that what is first present is beings and that being is covered. The reconciliation Henry offers (in ¶7) is that the covering is the natural consciousness's attention to beings. The claim is a Contestation of SZ 35 under a reading of SZ 35's own words: the "must be able to show itself" is a task for SZ, a fact for Henry.

Henry says that, because the manifestation of being is originary, natural consciousness cannot turn away from the truth (n. 8, H 163), "*La conscience ne peut oublier l'être qui constitue son essence même*"; when it is said to live in forgetting of being, what is meant is that it holds to beings and takes for nothing "l'être, l'apparaître de l'apparaissant" (n. 9, H 144); "*Pas un instant l'être n'a cessé de se montrer à la conscience naturelle au moment même où elle est dite l'« oublier »*." This denies the forgetting of being that SZ asserts at its beginning: the question of being "ist heute in Vergessenheit gekommen" (SZ 2), and being "kann so weitgehend verdeckt sein, daß es vergessen wird" (SZ 35). Henry's distinction, that consciousness holds to beings and takes being for nothing, is a way of stating the forgetting SZ describes, without the claim that something has been withdrawn. SZ's Dasein understands being "vorontologisch" and forgets the question; the structure is the same, and the stress is opposite. Henry insists that the showing never ceases, SZ that it is covered. The two accounts differ in what they call forgetting, not in the facts of average attention to beings.

Henry says that the manifestation of being to natural consciousness requires no radical modification of its life; the modification that does occur is the *Umkehrung* (reversal) in which consciousness, ceasing to aim at the being, takes up "l'acte d'apparaître en vertu duquel l'étant apparaît" and represents "« l'apparaissant *comme* apparaissant »" (n. 10, H 178; cf. n. 11, H 175); "*La représentation de la manifestation dans le renversement de la conscience est la manifestation de la manifestation pure comme telle*." The reversal is the essay's, from Hegel, and is discussed in the GA 5 run (guard 5). SZ's counterpart, the thematic taking up of being, is conceptual and not representational, a "Begreifen" of what the understanding of being projects: "Sein ist im Entwurf verstanden, nicht ontologisch begriffen" (SZ 147–148). Henry's "se représenter l'apparaître" gives the thematization the character of representing, which the essay attributes to modern metaphysics and not to the philosopher's own thought.

Henry says that the reversal realizes "l'apparaître de l'apparaître": appearing is the essence of consciousness; the reversal is consciousness's own act; it is "le s'apparaître de l'apparaître", "la présence à soi-même de l'absolu, la Parousie", "par lequel la conscience se représente dans son apparaître" (n. 12, H 181). "S'apparaître de l'apparaître" renders the essay's *Sicherscheinen des Erscheinens*, a rendering the GA 5 run records (guard 8). In SZ's terms it is *Erscheinen*, which SZ distinguishes from *Sichzeigen* (SZ 29–30). The phrase is Henry's formula for the thesis that the essence manifests itself, and its tilt in SZ's vocabulary is the one noted in §8.

Henry says that representing the act of appearing introduces consciousness into philosophical, "vrai" knowledge, which is knowledge of truth, not of beings; the presence of the absolute to itself, its Parousia. The paragraph is the essay's and Hegel's, and independent of SZ except for the use of "savoir de la vérité" for philosophical knowledge, where SZ defines the knowledge of phenomenology as "Zugangsart" to being (SZ 35).

Henry says that the true knowledge represents the condition of the knowledge of beings, or rather that knowledge itself: "Le savoir de soi du savoir de l'étant est, comme le comprenaient déjà Fichte et Schelling, le savoir transcendantal. Le savoir transcendantal est le savoir vrai." The identification is the Fichte-Schelling tradition's. SZ's own use is different: "Jede Erschließung von Sein als des transcendens ist *transzendentale* Erkenntnis. *Phänomenologische Wahrheit (Erschlossenheit von Sein) ist veritas transcendentalis*" (SZ 38). The two agree that transcendental knowledge is knowledge of the condition of the knowledge of beings. SZ places it in the disclosure of being by Dasein, Henry in the self-knowledge of knowing.

Henry says that "Que le savoir transcendantal ne soit pas le savoir vrai" results from its being the knowledge of natural consciousness, because the manifestation of being is originary and the transcendental knowledge is nothing other than that manifestation: "*La conscience naturelle est la conscience transcendantale.*" The sentence collapses the distinction that SZ keeps between the pre-ontological understanding that Dasein is (SZ 12–13) and the transcendental, ontological knowledge that the analytic wins from it (SZ 38). The identification of the natural and the transcendental consciousness is the essay's reading of Hegel, in Henry's French, and SZ states the contrary difference: the pre-ontological understanding is not yet conceptual, and the analytic "radicalizes" it (SZ 15). The unit is a Selection that removes the difference SZ's method rests on.

Henry says that being is pure manifestation, the manifestation of being is the self-manifestation of pure manifestation, which "est si peu le fait du renversement qu'elle appartient au contraire à l'essence pure de la manifestation elle-même"; "Que l'être doive pouvoir se manifester [...] signifie que l'essence de l'être est la manifestation de soi. *La manifestation de soi est l'essence de la manifestation*"; this is originary, "le fait de l'essence elle-même", and philosophical knowledge presupposes it constantly. The thesis is the section's: self-manifestation is the essence of manifestation. It is where Henry's own doctrine begins, and it is stated against SZ 35–36, where being does not show itself and is made to show by phenomenology, and against the argument of §13, where manifestation is never self-manifestation. SZ is silent on the pure self-manifestation of manifestation. Its nearest text is the formal concept of phenomenon, "das Sich-an-ihm-selbst-zeigende" (SZ 28), which Henry's later definition of the phenomenon will turn into a self-showing without horizon. The statement is the first thesis of Section II that is neither a reading of SZ nor a reading of the essay.

### § 18. The concept of representation: ontological structure and existential understanding

Henry says that the manifestation of being belongs to the essence of consciousness, and so to natural consciousness, but this does not mean that natural consciousness "se la représente"; "représentation" has two senses. In the first it names the thematic object and the particular aiming at it, a "mode déterminé de la vie de la conscience"; natural consciousness is the mode that aims at beings and takes for nothing what is not they; "Le savoir philosophique ou savoir vrai est lui aussi un mode déterminé de la vie de la conscience" and "il n'a rien à voir avec le savoir absolu", which is "l'existence de la conscience dans son essence universelle", the Parousia, "non une détermination de cette existence"; the Parousia is the presupposition of both true and untrue knowledge and "elle est la vie consciente elle-même comme telle"; "Le s'apparaître de l'apparaître [...] ne se produisent pas dans la « représentation »". The distinction between a thematic act and the condition of every act is the one SZ draws between ontic comportments and the understanding of being that underlies them: Dasein's understanding of being "gehört [...] zu seiner Seinsverfassung" and precedes every comportment toward beings (SZ 12, 13), including the thematic, scientific ones (SZ 13). Henry's assignment of philosophical knowledge to the determinate modes of life, with absolute knowledge as the underlying condition, parallels SZ's placing of the ontological inquiry as an existentiell possibility of Dasein (SZ 13–14). Parousia and "savoir absolu" are the essay's vocabulary, where SZ's is "Seinsverständnis".

Henry says that "représentation" can also mean the essence of manifestation itself, "la manifestation de soi de l'être", "la conscience elle-même dans son essence universelle", whose object is "l'être lui-même"; natural consciousness represents both being and beings but not in the same way: it represents beings in a determinate act, and being without making it its theme; philosophical consciousness represents being "comme la conscience naturelle se représente l'étant, en faisant de lui le thème de sa visée", so its object is an object "au même sens". The two senses reappear in SZ as the difference between the pre-ontological understanding of being, which Dasein "has" without concept, and the ontological concept (SZ 12, 15; "Sein ist im Entwurf verstanden, nicht ontologisch begriffen", SZ 147–148). Henry's second sense of "représentation" (an essence of consciousness) has no SZ counterpart, since SZ does not call the understanding of being a "représentation", and the first sense is the one SZ's critique of the "nur Vorstellen" concerns (SZ 62). That philosophical thought turns being into an object "au même sens" than beings is a worry SZ shares in its own way (the danger of treating being as a being, SZ 6), but SZ proposes a different treatment, the "eigene Aufweisungsart" proper to being (SZ 6).

Henry says that to the division of "représentation" corresponds a division of "objet": the object of philosophical consciousness is "un objet au sens ordinaire du mot", determined by an act; the object of consciousness in its universal essence is "l'objectivité, l'être lui-même sous la forme d'un horizon", given in the absence of any particular aiming; "A l'essence de l'horizon il appartient de ne pas se laisser thématiser. L'objet de la représentation de la conscience universelle est indépendant de toute « représentation » particulière et, comme tel, il n'est jamais l'« objet » de celle-ci" (n. 13). The claim that the horizon of being cannot be thematized stands against SZ 17: the horizon of all understanding of being, time, "muß [...] ans Licht gebracht und genuin begriffen werden" (must be brought to light and genuinely grasped), which is the work of the second division, and the horizon-schemata of the ecstases are exhibited conceptually (SZ 365). The Introduction had already argued the opposite (R §3 ¶9: the horizon escapes intuition when thematized). For SZ the horizon is not an object, since it is a "Wohin der Entrückung" (SZ 365), yet it can be explicated. Henry's "jamais l'objet" is an extension that makes the horizon unavailable to philosophical knowledge, and the reconciliation lies in his distinction of the two senses of "objet", which SZ does not use.

Henry says that the ambiguity lets one resolve the formulas that define natural knowledge; its contradiction, according to Heidegger, is that "dans sa représentation de l'étant il ne prend pas garde à l'être et doit pourtant y prendre garde" (n. 14, H 136; GA 5 run); natural knowledge is only, in Hegel's phrase, "le concept du savoir"; but "prendre garde" does not mean the same in both cases: affirmatively it designates the representation constituting consciousness (the horizon whose "manifestation originaire [...] se trouve radicalement indépendante à l'égard de toute saisie dans un acte"), negatively it denotes the absence of a thematic grasp; so "il est faux de dire que le savoir naturel est seulement le « concept du savoir », il est le savoir lui-même en un sens absolu"; "L'opposition de la conscience naturelle et du savoir vrai est inessentielle." The quotation belongs to the essay. In SZ the same two-sidedness is asserted of Dasein without paradox: it "has" an understanding of being and does not conceptualize it; "ontisch [...] am nächsten, ontologisch am fernsten, aber vorontologisch doch nicht fremd" (SZ 16). The difference between natural and true knowledge is for SZ not inessential. It is the difference between an average, vague understanding and a conceptual one that "gewinnt [...] ihren notwendigen Leitfaden erst mit dem ausgebildeten Begriff des Seins" (SZ 6), and SZ requires the second for the question to be asked at all. Henry declares the opposition inessential for the ontological structure and keeps it as existentiell.

Henry says that an important distinction follows, between the ontological and the existentiel: "prendre garde à l'être" (true knowledge) and "ne pas prendre garde à l'être" (natural consciousness) "sont des modes de la vie de la conscience, des déterminations de son existence": "*déterminations existentielles*"; the "prendre garde" inherent in the essence of consciousness is "une *structure ontologique*", "à la fois indifférente et immanente aux déterminations existentielles"; indifferent because the structure does not prescribe that a given existentiel determination occur; "*Les déterminations existentielles sont inessentielles*, ce sont, par rapport à l'essence, des prédicats contingents et variables" yet the essence is immanent to them "comme leur essence même"; so natural knowledge "*est* ontologique"; the formula that "la conscience naturelle est et n'est pas… ontologique" (n. 15, H 163) is ambiguous. This is the book's adoption of SZ's distinction of existentiell and existential/ontological (SZ 12–13), and it is a re-inscription. SZ's *existenziell* is "das *hierbei* führende Verständnis seiner selbst" in the course of existing, "eine ontische »Angelegenheit« des Daseins" (SZ 12–13), and the existential is the ontological structure of existence. Henry's "déterminations existentielles" are modes of the life of consciousness and the signification it constitutes ("une détermination de sa signification transcendante", ¶6). The structural parallel holds, that the ontological structure is indifferent to and present in each existentiel determination, and SZ states it (SZ 16: the structures "die in jeder Seinsart des faktischen Daseins sich als seinsbestimmende durchhalten", which persist as determinative of being in every mode of factical Dasein). Two things differ. SZ's existentiell modes are not "prédicats contingents": Dasein's characters "sind [...] nicht vorhandene »Eigenschaften« [...], sondern je ihm mögliche Weisen zu sein und nur das. Alles *Sosein* dieses Seienden ist primär *Sein*" (not present-at-hand properties but possible ways for it to be, and only that; all being-so of this being is primarily being, SZ 42). And SZ's structure belongs to Dasein and is "je meines" (SZ 42), where Henry's belongs to the essence of consciousness, which Henry calls "immanent".

Henry says that what leads one to say improperly that natural consciousness is not ontological is that its aimed-at content "n'est pas l'être", that one qualifies a consciousness by its object, as one calls mathematical a consciousness whose object is mathematical (the triangle); the determination of the signification is identically that of the aiming, "La structure noético-noématique de la conscience est une structure unitaire"; "*Une détermination existentielle est donc une détermination de la vie de la conscience et, solidairement, une détermination de sa signification transcendante*" (n. 16). The paragraph is Husserl's noetic-noematic correlation, used to define the existentiel. It does not follow SZ, whose existentiell concerns one's understanding of one's own possibilities of being and not the correlate of an intention. The re-inscription widens SZ's term from the self-understanding of Dasein to any intentional life with its correlate, which is why the distance "existentielle" of §9 (tied to care) and this definition are not the same.

Henry says that to live in the presence of a signification is to understand, so every existentiel determination is an existentiel understanding, and one that "fait de l'existence elle-même son objet" accompanies it implicitly; "*La compréhension existentielle trouve son fondement dans la compréhension ontologique de l'être*", understanding "non plus d'une signification déterminée, mais de l'être lui-même comme fondement de toutes les significations possibles"; "*La compréhension ontologique de l'être est la manifestation originaire de l'être à lui-même dans la conscience en général*" and "la structure universelle de l'essence absolue". The two-level structure is SZ's. "In jedem Verstehen von Welt ist Existenz mitverstanden und umgekehrt" (in every understanding of world existence is co-understood, and conversely, SZ 152) states the implicit self-understanding that accompanies understanding of the object, and the existentiell self-understanding has its ground in the existential structure of Dasein, which includes the understanding of being (SZ 13). The paragraph reconstructs the structure and redescribes its ground. For SZ the understanding of being is a projection of Dasein that belongs to the "Seinsverfassung" of an existing being (SZ 147–148, 212); for Henry it is the "manifestation originaire de l'être à lui-même", the structure of the absolute essence. The step from understanding of being to the absolute's self-manifestation is what the monism supplies.

Henry says that absolute knowledge resides in ontological understanding; true or philosophical knowledge is "une compréhension existentielle ou, pour mieux dire, existentiale de la compréhension ontologique", though not every existentiel understanding of the ontological is true or philosophical; the implicit existentiel self-understanding of existence is always an existentiel understanding of the ontological understanding, "l'esquisse implicite et le plus souvent impropre d'une compréhension existentiale", and "Le savoir philosophique n'est qu'une thématisation, conduite avec le matériel conceptuel exigé et élaboré à cette fin, de cette compréhension existentielle implicite de soi de l'existence, c'est-à-dire de la compréhension ontologique elle-même." This is the closest reconstruction of SZ in the division. SZ states that the existential analytic is "letztlich *existenziell*, d. h. *ontisch* verwurzelt": only when philosophical questioning is itself seized existentielly as a possibility of the existing Dasein is the disclosure of existentiality possible (SZ 13–14), and that the analytic requires the right conceptual means and a method of access (SZ 15–17). Henry's "thématisation, conduite avec le matériel conceptuel exigé" corresponds to SZ's "Begrifflichkeit" and its demand that it be won from the matter itself. The use of "existentiale" for the thematized form is SZ's, and Henry's footnote at §11 (n. 78: "Sans doute faut-il lire « existentiale »") shows he takes SZ's pair seriously. The departure is the object. SZ's thematization yields the analytic of Dasein's existence; Henry's yields the structure of the "essence absolue".

Henry says that ontological understanding of being is radically independent of any existentiel understanding: "Que la compréhension existentielle de soi de l'existence soit vraie ou fausse, qu'elle s'accomplisse dans un mode d'existence « authentique » ou « inauthentique », cela ne change rien à la nature originaire de l'existence"; this yields an absolute opposition between "ce que l'existence est en soi" and "la façon dont cette existence se représente ou se comprend elle-même", as Marx's distinction of consciousness (the way an individual or an epoch understands itself) and real life (n. 17, Marx VI 156); the critique of "conscience" as ideology "ne peut se comprendre qu'en fonction de cette signification"; "Un changement de la « conscience » signifie donc une modification de la compréhension existentielle de soi de l'existence et n'affecte en rien la réalité de celle-ci" (n. 18, Marx VI 152); the investigation concerns only "cet élément ontologique pur dans sa réalité originaire antérieure à tout acte de compréhension". The structural thesis, that the ontological structures hold in every existentiel mode, is SZ's (SZ 16). "Authentique" and "inauthentique" are SZ's terms, and SZ places them among the existentiel modes grounded in mineness (SZ 42–43), adding that inauthenticity is no lesser being. Henry's strengthening, that the ontological understanding is "radicalement indépendante" of any existentiel understanding, is not SZ's: for SZ the ontological structures are those of an existing Dasein, so the existentiell modes are modes of the being whose structure the analytic exhibits (SZ 42–43), and the analytic itself rests on an existentiell grasp (SZ 13). The Marx quotations introduce the opposition between what existence is and how it understands itself, which reads in SZ's terms as the opposition between the existential and the existentiell, with a difference of aim: in Marx the first is the real conditions, in SZ the structure of Dasein.

### § 19. Being-for-itself from the ontological point of view and from the existential point of view: consciousness and truth

Henry says that the distinction between the universal ontological structure of existence and its existentiel self-understanding removes the equivocity of the "pour-soi": the term designates both the essence of existence, which consists in manifestation of self, and a particular determination in which existence understands itself "à l'intérieur d'un mode de compréhension « authentique » ou « inauthentique »"; the analysis of the for-itself has ontological significance only if its object is "*l'existence telle qu'elle est en soi et non telle qu'elle se comprend*"; the way existence understands itself is "variable", an "expérience", which concerns first philosophy not at all; Hegel's unhappy consciousness of Judaism understands itself as "une existence qui laisse l'essence en dehors d'elle", though "*L'essence [...] est immanente à l'existence comme constituant son essence même*"; "*L'existence est en soi l'essence, elle n'est pas l'essence « pour soi ». Mais l'essence est le pour-soi*." The opposition of existence as it is in itself to the way it understands itself is not SZ's, and SZ excludes it at its first characterization of Dasein. Dasein is the being that "versteht sich in irgendeiner Weise und Ausdrücklichkeit in seinem Sein" (understands itself in some way and explicitness in its being), and "Das Dasein versteht sich selbst immer aus seiner Existenz, einer Möglichkeit seiner selbst, es selbst oder nicht es selbst zu sein" (SZ 12–13). The self-understanding is not a representation added to an existence that would be what it is apart from it; it is the way the being exists, and "Die Frage der Existenz ist immer nur durch das Existieren selbst ins Reine zu bringen" (SZ 12). "Authentic" and "inauthentic" are modes of that being (SZ 42–43), not variations in a representation of it. Henry uses SZ's vocabulary for a distinction (existence in itself against self-representation) that SZ's definition of Dasein removes. The paragraph's Hegelian content (the unhappy consciousness) is for the GA 5 run.

Henry says that existence is the for-itself because its ontological structure is the self-manifestation of pure manifestation, the Parousia; it is not "pour-soi" in that it does not direct itself on its own essence to grasp it in an act; the ontological for-itself is radically independent of the existentiel; "La séparation de l'essence et de l'existence dans la représentation est une séparation irréelle", irreal not because false but because it occurs within representation (n. 19: later, §31, 66, 67); the unity of essence and existence as understood in true knowledge "n'est pas moins irréelle", being "une unité comprise par nous". The paragraph builds on Hegel's *für es / für sich / für uns* and Henry's thesis, and has no SZ locus. Its conclusion, that an understood unity is irreal, is the position SZ cannot take, since for SZ all understanding is of the being whose being it is and is real as a mode of existing (SZ 12–13, 152). The irreality Henry attributes to representation is the property he gives to the existentiel mode against SZ 12.

Henry says that the unhappy consciousness does not represent the unity of essence and existence, which is "pour nous *(für uns)*", while the natural consciousness, once raised to philosophical knowledge, will have it "pour soi *(für sich)*"; yet the unity is "pour nous" or "pour soi" no less irreal than the separation "pour elle *(für es)*" (n. 20); "Ce qui est pour la conscience [...] est toujours [...] quelque chose d'irréel. [...] *l'unité réelle de l'existence et de l'essence*"; "Peu importe finalement que l'existence se comprenne ou ne se comprenne pas comme étant l'essence, elle est l'essence, c'est-à-dire la réalité." The paragraph is Hegel's schema, and independent of SZ. The conclusion that it does not matter whether existence understands itself as the essence contradicts SZ 12 in the same way as the first paragraph.

Henry says that Hegel's distinction between what is for consciousness and what is for us, though it seems to introduce the problematic into the original dimension of for-itself, "lui demeure en fait radicalement étrangère, *elle ne concerne pas la structure ontologique de l'être-pour-soi*"; the *Phenomenology of Spirit* "n'a donc à aucun moment affaire avec la réalité", proceeding in abstraction, because "« l'abstraction est justement ce qui n'est pas authentiquement, ce qui seulement *est pour la conscience* »" (n. 21, PhE I 318; n. 22); the becoming-for-itself of the in-itself of consciousness "n'a [...] qu'une signification existentielle"; "*La phénoménologie de l'esprit est une structure ontologique*." The verdict on Hegel's book and the argument about *an sich* and *für sich* belong to the GA 5 run (guard 12). The SZ side is the analogous judgement SZ makes on Hegel's treatment of time, not of consciousness; that comes in §82 and Henry does not cite it here.

Henry says that the distinction of ontological and existentiel for-itself clarifies the relation of consciousness and truth: "La conscience est en soi la vérité"; the truth it takes for the being appears as constituted by its knowledge of the being; as long as the identity of truth and knowledge is only for the consciousness that aims at it, this "vérité" remains transcendent to it twice over (to the philosophical consciousness *für uns*, as a signification it reaches by surpassing itself, and to natural consciousness *für es*, as one it does not reach), it is a signification, "le strict corrélât d'un acte déterminé de compréhension", "une vérité existentielle", while "la vérité que la conscience est en soi-même" is "la vérité ontologique". Henry's truth-pair maps loosely onto SZ's. SZ's primordial truth is "die Erschlossenheit des Daseins" (SZ 220–221, 226) and is a way of being of Dasein, and SZ distinguishes a truth of existence in authentic disclosedness (SZ 221). Henry's "vérité existentielle" is a signification correlated with an act, which is closer to SZ's "Entdecktheit" of a being than to the "Wahrheit der Existenz". The alignment is therefore partial: Henry's ontological truth corresponds to SZ's disclosedness only if disclosedness is taken as what consciousness "is in itself", and SZ's source of disclosedness is Dasein's constitution (attunement, understanding, discourse, SZ 220–221), not a "pour-soi de l'être".

Henry says that the ontological truth is "la manifestation de soi de l'être, l'être-pour-soi lui-même"; "*La vérité est la vérité pour-soi. L'être-pour-soi de la vérité est justement la conscience*"; the life of consciousness is "une vie dans la vérité", "*La vérité n'est pas transcendante à la conscience*"; only existentiel truth can be separated from consciousness; this lifts the ambiguity of "la vérité de l'existence": "A « la vérité de l'existence » qui varie selon la manière dont l'existence comprend toute chose et soi-même, s'oppose radicalement « la vérité de l'existence » qui désigne l'essence même de celle-ci en tant que cette essence est la manifestation originaire de l'être." The phrase "la vérité de l'existence" is SZ 221's ("Wahrheit der Existenz"), already cited by Henry at §12 (n. 84). In SZ it is not ambiguous in the way Henry says: it names the most original and authentic disclosedness in which Dasein can be as potentiality-for-being (SZ 221), and the "Gewißsein" corresponding to it as resolute "Sich-halten in dem, was die Entschlossenheit erschließt" (SZ 308, the passage on resoluteness). Henry's split gives the phrase two senses (varying truth; essence as originary manifestation) and attaches the second to the "être-pour-soi" of consciousness. This is a Re-inscription of SZ's phrase, made in the passage where Henry rules that truth is never transcendent to consciousness. SZ's own thesis is nearer: "Dasein ist in der Wahrheit" (SZ 221), but as a constitution of an existing being that is also "in der Unwahrheit" (SZ 222).

### § 20. Critique of the Hegelian concept of experience

Henry says that the dissociation of ontological from existentiel truth challenges Hegel's concept of experience: experience is consciousness's experience of truth, which is first the being to which consciousness relates; "C'est de deux façons bien différentes [...] que la conscience est la vérité de l'étant": as the originary manifestation of being, and as a determinate representation that understands itself as the truth of the being, "ayant la mesure en elle"; "Comment une telle conscience peut-elle naître ?" The paragraph is Hegel's introduction read through Henry's distinction, and its Hegel-side is the GA 5 run's. The SZ side has no counterpart, since SZ does not treat Hegel's concept of experience.

Henry says that consciousness becomes the truth of the being "pour soi, dans sa représentation" only because it is that truth "en soi"; "la conscience qui est la vérité de l'étant en ce sens qu'elle se représente comme telle est donc le résultat, elle ne saurait être un principe de l'expérience. C'est pourtant ce qu'en fait Hegel": consciousness is for itself consciousness of the object and of itself, "Puisque tous les deux sont *pour elle*, elle est elle-même leur comparaison" (n. 23, PhE I 74), so reality is explained by the play of consciousness's representations: "Notre histoire est le produit de ce que nous pensons." The criticism of making representation the principle is SZ's criticism of subject-based ontology (SZ 22, 46, 59–62). The sentence "Notre histoire est le produit de ce que nous pensons" ascribes to Hegel a thesis that SZ finds in the tradition's treatment of consciousness and answers by the facticity of historicity (SZ 20–21: Dasein "ist seine Vergangenheit" and its past "geht ihm vorweg"). The GA 5 run decides Hegel's text. The passage here is for SZ an instance of the intellectualism SZ already criticized.

Henry says that Hegel assigns to the history of experience a goal, that consciousness understand itself as it is in itself, and attributes this self-understanding to the experiencing consciousness as a condition; it cannot both result from experience and precede it; "Ce n'est pas de la même façon [...] que la conscience est conscience de l'objet et conscience de soi"; to compare them Hegel must "confondre la signification ontologique de l'être-pour-soi avec sa signification existentielle". The charge of equivocation and its Hegel-side are for the GA 5 run. The structure of the charge uses SZ's pair existential/existentiell, and applies it to the Hegelian *Bewußtsein* as Husserl's categories of noesis and noema did in §18. The charge resembles SZ's objection to philosophies of consciousness that they leave unasked the being of consciousness (SZ 208), and the answer Henry gives (the ontological for-itself is not a representation) is not SZ's answer (the analytic of Dasein).

Henry says that, by confusing the for-itself with representation, Hegel gains the advantage of placing the consciousness's representation at the beginning of experience, but the for-itself "déchoit de sa condition ontologique primitive pour devenir l'un de ces objets"; there lies "le passage de la réalité à la représentation de cette réalité, le passage de la réalité à l'irréalité"; in the sphere of the irreal something may not be for consciousness; "Ici se fait jour le paradoxe de tout intellectualisme. Après avoir dit que tout est pour la conscience, Hegel doit dire [...] que quelque chose aussi pourtant n'est pas pour elle" (n. 24, PhE I 74). The "déchéance" of the for-itself into an object is SZ's falling in Henry's lexicon ("Verfallen" there is not a fall into representation but into the "world" and "Man", SZ 175–178). The paradox of "everything is for consciousness yet something is not" is the structure SZ attributes to the Cartesian-Kantian line (SZ 24, 207–208). The unit contains no SZ claim; it uses SZ's idiom of fall and of the unasked being of consciousness for Hegel.

Henry says that, for Hegel, experience is the movement by which consciousness perceives that what it took for the in-itself is only in-itself for it, so that the being-for-it of the in-itself is the truth; the second object is the truth of the first, but "cette considération de la chose est notre fait" (n. 25, PhE I 7); the consciousness that experiences sees only a new object and suffers the loss; the birth of the new object "se passe pour ainsi dire derrière son dos" (n. 26, PhE I 77); "*ce qui est pour nous est aussi irréel que ce qui est seulement pour elle*"; "*L'expérience réelle ne se produit ni devant la conscience, ni derrière elle, elle se produit dans la dimension ontologique originaire de l'être-pour-soi*"; "L'intellectualisme, que ce soit celui de Hegel ou de Freud, est une doctrine de l'inconscient." The paragraph is Hegel and Henry's own. SZ's analogue to "behind its back" is its handling of the unthematized horizon and of the average understanding (SZ 15–16, and the analysis of the "Man" in §27), where what governs Dasein's understanding is not before its eyes and is not "unconscious" but an understanding it has without concept ("vorontologisch"). The classification of Hegel and Freud as "doctrines of the unconscious" is Henry's, and SZ never uses the notion.

Henry says that what happens behind consciousness's back is what consciousness aspires to represent; when it does, it will have reached true knowledge; the motion of experience originates, according to Hegel, in the inequality between the consciousness of the object and of itself, "dans la différence, dit Heidegger, entre le savoir naturel et le savoir réel"; this difference is the difference between a modality of the life of consciousness and that consciousness "dans sa structure ontologique universelle", "Une telle différence n'est pas susceptible d'être supprimée : elle subsiste dans le savoir vrai"; what is suppressed at the end is the difference between natural and true knowledge, which is not the one at the origin; Hegel, by confusing the ontological and the existentiel meaning of for-itself, "faisait du savoir réel une représentation": "*La confusion de la signification ontologique et de la signification existentielle de l'être-pour-soi est identiquement celle du savoir réel et du savoir vrai.*" The attribution to Heidegger of "savoir naturel / savoir réel" is the essay's (guard 1 of the GA 5 run). The structural claim has an echo in SZ's account of the difference between pre-ontological and ontological understanding, which does not vanish when the analytic is complete: the analytic is to be "wiederholt" on the higher basis (SZ 17), and Dasein remains "ontologisch am fernsten" in its being (SZ 15–16). That the difference is "not susceptible of being suppressed" is therefore compatible with SZ, and the use Henry makes of it (to say that the end of experience abolishes only the existentiel difference) is the GA 5 run's matter.

### § 21. The reaffirmation of the originary character of the manifestation of being in the bringing-into-light of its non-historical character

Henry says that the confusion of true and real knowledge situates the latter at the end of the course of experience, "L'absolu, dit Hegel, est essentiellement Résultat" (n. 28, PhE I 19), whereas what results from the history of consciousness is only the absolute's representation in true knowledge; the homogeneity of natural and true knowledge lets Hegel start from the one to reach the other and oppose Schelling; "*Le problème de savoir comment la conscience parvient au savoir absolu ne peut cependant être résolu au terme de l'histoire de la conscience, il doit l'être à son début*"; consciousness neither reaches nor leaves absolute knowledge; "*Le problème du savoir absolu est le problème de la structure interne de la conscience en général.*" The unit is Hegel and Schelling, with the essay, and independent of SZ. Its statement that the problem of the absolute must be solved "à son début" has the form of SZ's thesis that the question of being is not solved by an outcome but presupposes the understanding of being already at work (SZ 5, 8), and the same difference in content as before: the beginning is a pre-ontological understanding in SZ, the absolute's self-manifestation in Henry.

Henry says that the absolute knowledge is not equivalent to the natural knowledge with which history begins, "il est son essence"; in what is at the beginning one must distinguish a modality of the life of consciousness and "ce qui rend possible un tel commencement, à savoir le commencement lui-même"; "Le savoir absolu est ce commencement absolu, il est l'origine, et cela en un sens ontologique et non pas seulement existentiel, *l'origine qui est le surgissement de la dimension effective de la phénoménalité*"; true knowledge "se représente l'acte même par lequel il surgit" but this manifestation of the origin "n'est pas l'origine elle-même"; since it is the common essence of natural and true knowledge, absolute knowledge "est étranger à leur histoire", as the essence of consciousness is to its existentiel determinations. Henry's "origine" in the ontological sense is not SZ's. SZ's origin is historical: the destruction goes back to "die ursprünglichen Erfahrungen, in denen die ersten und fortan leitenden Bestimmungen des Seins gewonnen wurden" (the original experiences in which the first and thenceforth leading determinations of being were won, SZ 22), and the question of being "ist selbst durch die Geschichtlichkeit charakterisiert" (SZ 20). The beginning of the ontological tradition is what the analytic retrieves; it is not a non-historical origin. Henry's distinction of ontological and existentiel senses of "origin" reproduces SZ's two levels, and puts the ontological one outside history.

Henry says that the dissociation of ontological from existentiel truth lets one fix the boundary between what is historical and what is not: "Ce qui est historique, c'est la vérité que l'existence se représente à son propre sujet, c'est la représentation de la vérité" (n. 29); "la vérité est présente avant l'accomplissement de cette histoire"; "Le concept est là avant son devenir dans le temps"; Hegel's text on the concept's "être-là [...] dans le temps et dans l'effectivité" (n. 30, PhE II 303–304) must be read with this restriction, since what comes about in consciousness's work is "la figure de son essence", not the essence; "*L'essence de la conscience est cependant l'être-là effectif du concept*": the concept is effectively there "autrement que par le travail de la conscience, ailleurs que dans l'histoire". The statement that the truth is present before history is the one SZ denies for Dasein. In SZ, historicity is not a feature of a representation of truth added to a non-historical being; it is the being of Dasein, "die Seinsverfassung des »Geschehens« des Daseins als solchen", and Dasein "ist" its past, which "geht ihm [...] schon vorweg" (precedes it, SZ 19–20), including the interpretation of being in which it has grown up. That which is "already there" is for SZ the thrown tradition and for Henry the non-historical concept. The two accounts agree that something is there before the work of thought, and differ on whether it is historical. The GA 5 run decides Hegel's text; the unit is a Contestation of SZ's thesis of the historicity of Dasein's understanding of being, not argued from SZ.

Henry says that it is improper to speak of an equality between "conscience" and its essence, since the difference between reality and its representation "ne peut être supprimée"; self-consciousness, which Hegel says against Fichte arises only after a preliminary process, "n'a [...] aucune signification ontologique"; the absolute, as the original for-itself of the essence, "ne se produit pas dans l'histoire"; it is the mere representation that can be refused to "cet enthousiasme qui, comme un coup de pistolet, commence immédiatement avec le savoir absolu" (n. 31, PhE I 25); but absolute knowledge is the act of this enthusiasm, as "ce premier acte d'apparaître de l'essence originaire" that has already done its work; "L'histoire n'est donc pas le « mouvement par lequel advient l'apparaître »" (n. 32, H 140; GA 5 run) because appearing "est d'ores et déjà venu près de nous"; "Le savoir transcendantal n'est pas à acquérir, il est à priori. Ce qui est au début n'est pas la modestie d'un *Grund* obscur, c'est le Verbe"; "*La Parousie se produit à l'origine parce qu'elle constitue l'essence de celle-ci*"; "Le destin de l'absolu ne se joue pas dans l'histoire." The unit contains no SZ claim, and its target is the essay's account of history as the coming of appearing (the GA 5 run, guard 7). "Parousie à l'origine" is again the name SZ gives to the Greek understanding of being as presence (SZ 25–26), and "le destin ne se joue pas dans l'histoire" is the contrary of SZ 19–22. "Le savoir transcendantal [...] est à priori" has a far echo in SZ 4–5 ("a priori ein Rätsel"), where the apriority is that of a vague understanding that always already operates. The statement "ce qui est au début [...] c'est le Verbe" belongs to Henry's later chapters (Fichte, §38) and not to SZ.

Henry says that, because the absolute finds its reality in the origin, the historical representation of truth "n'entre pas dans la définition structurale de la réalité absolue", is not its condition; to the question how an intemporal absolute knowledge can have temporal conditions in a humanity's existence and becoming (n. 33, Hyppolite, *Genèse et structure*, 575) the answer is that "un tel savoir, s'il est l'absolu, n'a en fait aucune condition historique"; the essence of manifestation is the condition of possibility of experience; so it is false to say, as Heidegger does after Hegel, "que « la représentation appartient à l'essence de l'expérience »" (n. 34, H 176) or that "« la représentation de l'expérience est voulue à partir de l'essence de l'expérience comme lui appartenant »" (n. 35, H 175; GA 5 run). The quotations are the essay's. The SZ locus is the position that the understanding of being has historical conditions: Dasein understands being "aus der »Welt«" and falls to tradition, which "verdeckt" its sources (SZ 21), and the destruction treats those conditions as constitutive of the question (SZ 19–22). Henry's answer to Hyppolite's question, that the absolute has no historical conditions, makes an a-historical absolute what SZ makes a historical understanding. The attribution of the essay's thesis to "Heidegger" is the guard 1/2 matter of the GA 5 run.

Henry asks why representation would belong to the essence of experience, and answers that representation signifies the reversal and history, so that its belonging would mean that the reversal and history are necessary for the essence to be realized, whereas effective manifestation is their condition; the character by which the essence is the presupposition "n'est rien d'autre [...] que *le caractère originaire de la révélation*"; the representation "se représente mal l'essence originaire de la révélation" and inserts it into its own history; "La tâche de saisir un tel fondement est pourtant celle de l'ontologie. A cette tâche [...] l'ontologie s'égale seulement dans le respect de l'origine"; Hegel: a pure thought is "« celle qui se tourne vers le commencement des choses »" (n. 36, L 163) and "« ce que l'esprit est, il le fut toujours en soi »" (n. 37, L 77); the distinction of ontological and existentiel truth forbids considering the essence as unfinished until representation grasps it, and rejects the claim that consciousness is "« quelque chose qu'elle n'est pas encore »" (n. 38, H 167); "L'identification de ce que la conscience n'est pas avec ce qu'elle est, est la tentation de la science". "Le respect de l'origine" is the task Henry assigns to ontology, and it is the reverse of SZ's relation of ontology to its origin: SZ's destruction "will die Vergangenheit nicht in Nichtigkeit begraben, sie hat *positive* Absicht" (does not want to bury the past in nothingness; it has a positive intent, SZ 23), and ontology "versteht sich" as the retrieval of the tradition's possibilities and limits (SZ 22–23). In SZ, to respect the origin is to retrieve a history. The remark that consciousness is not "quelque chose qu'elle n'est pas encore" takes aim at the essay's phrase (n. 38, H 167 / GA 5 182) and not at SZ's "Sich-vorweg" (ahead-of-itself, the first moment of care, SZ 221), which does say that Dasein is always ahead of itself in its potentiality. The two senses of "not yet" differ (Hegel's absolute that is to be achieved; Dasein's possibilities), and Henry's rejection leaves the second untouched, though a reader will want it considered, since the care-structure is SZ's central determination of the being of the existent.

Henry concludes: "Le savoir philosophique manque son but lorsqu'il s'interroge sur lui-même. Ou bien il faut qu'il pousse cette interrogation assez loin pour qu'elle soit identiquement une interrogation sur l'essence de tout savoir comme tel. Ce n'est pas le savoir philosophique qui importe. La philosophie vient toujours trop tard car ce qu'elle dit était au commencement." This is a version of SZ's thesis that the analytic starts from an understanding it does not produce (SZ 5, 8, 150) and of its statement that Dasein's past "geht ihm vorweg" (SZ 20). It differs in its consequence. SZ concludes that the question of being must be asked in view of its own history, with a method of retrieval; Henry concludes that philosophy is posterior and secondary, which is the judgement SZ's second division reverses in the anticipation ("Vorlaufen") that opens Dasein's potentiality-for-being-whole (SZ 265), and the analytic is not "too late" for a being whose being is ahead of itself. The final sentence of Section II's first part is therefore the point at which Henry's view of the relation of thought to its ground leaves SZ's: thought is late because the ground is non-historical.

### § 22. The interpretation of the essence of phenomenality within the fundamental presuppositions of monism and the problem of receptivity: ontological significance of this problem

Henry says that §§17–21 brought out the originary character of manifestation, which means that "l'essence de la manifestation se manifeste en elle-même et par elle-même"; but the essence was interpreted within monism, so the originary character means "la manifestation en et pour soi de l'horizon pur dans lequel l'essence s'objective"; the essence does its work by itself, "en cela réside sa *Selbständigkeit*", and "*Il s'agit de savoir comment est possible la manifestation de l'horizon pur de l'être*." The paragraph restates the problem set at §16. The comparison with SZ is that SZ's analogous problem, how being can be made phenomenon, has an answer in the method of access (SZ 35–38); Henry's formulation puts the possibility in the horizon's own manifestation.

Henry says that the horizon is what the essence, as transcendence, opposes to itself; the opposition is real only if what is opposed is "maintenu près d'elle" in the act, that is, received: "L'opposition *et la réception* de ce qui, dans l'opposition, se trouve opposé à l'essence constituent ensemble la possibilité de l'objectivation"; "Être reçu, cela veut dire, se donner à, apparaître, se manifester. *La réceptivité de l'horizon est identiquement sa manifestation*." The thesis of the unit is the Kantbuch's receptivity (*Hinnahme*) transposed to the horizon. SZ has the receptive aspect in another place. Dasein's projection is thrown ("geworfener Entwurf") and its disclosedness "ist wesenhaft faktische" (SZ 148, 221), so that what is opened is not wholly given by the opening: attunement brings Dasein "vor das Daß seines Da" (before the that of its Da, SZ 136). SZ's receptivity belongs to attunement and thrownness in an existing Dasein; Henry's belongs to the relation of an essence to the horizon it forms.

Henry says that the problem of receptivity has a pure ontological significance: what is to be received is "le milieu phénoménologique pur dans lequel l'étant peut se manifester", so that this milieu can be called "phénoménologique" only in so far as it is received; receptivity is ontological "comme constituant ce qu'il y a de plus essentiel dans l'essence elle-même", the act that maintains near the essence what is placed before it and assures its inner coherence. The assertion that receptivity is the most essential in the essence is Henry's. SZ locates the coherence of Dasein's being in care, the "Strukturganzes" that includes being-ahead, already-being-in and being-with (SZ 221), and the act that "maintient près" is not an element of it. The unit is independent of SZ.

Henry says that the ontological character of receptivity has been masked, since it is first thought in connection with the reception of beings and with finitude: "Si la connaissance finie de l'étant est possible, dit Heidegger, elle devra se fonder sur un connaître de l'être de l'étant *préalable à tout acte réceptif*.
