[...] *un connaître non réceptif*, [...] une sorte d'intuition « créatrice »" (n. 39, K 98); ontological knowledge, which enables the reception of beings, is first interpreted as creative, but it is receptive "à l'égard de l'être lui-même" insofar as it is the process by which the essence objectifies itself as a horizon that "ne se manifeste à elle qu'en tant précisément qu'elle le reçoit"; showing that the reception of beings refers to an ontological problem does not solve the problem of receptivity but poses it where it belongs, since "c'est seulement comme objet que l'étant est susceptible d'être reçu". The quotation is the Kantbuch's (the GA 3 run). SZ's treatment of intuition goes in another direction: it places "Anschauung" among the derivatives of understanding (SZ 147), and so denies that the primary question of finite knowledge is the contrast between receptive and creative intuition. Henry follows the Kantbuch in making that contrast the framework of the problem, and moves its solution to the ontological level.

Henry says that the ontological character of receptivity removes the ambiguity of the opposition between "créatrice" and "réceptrice" knowledge, valid only "relativement à l'étant"; ontological knowledge, which "forme l'horizon de l'être en même temps qu'elle le reçoit, est créatrice et réceptrice à la fois"; so Kant's distinction of *Entstand* and *Gegenstand* is "inessentielle", "foncièrement obscure"; "le vrai problème n'est pas de savoir si l'étant procède ou non du pouvoir de connaissance lui-même, il est de comprendre comment cet étant [...] peut cependant se manifester à elle"; "*la finitude est une catégorie ontologique*"; "Que l'étant soit créé ne change rien à la finitude de sa manifestation"; "L'intuition n'est pas finie parce qu'elle reçoit un étant qui n'émane pas d'elle. Elle est finie en elle-même, en tant qu'intuition." The criticism of Kant's distinction is Henry's own, made against the Kantbuch's account. SZ has no *Entstand/Gegenstand* distinction, and its closest thing is its refusal to base finitude on the receptive/creative contrast: finitude is the temporal finitude of Dasein (SZ 331), which depends on neither. The statement that finitude is "une catégorie ontologique" uses SZ's term "Kategorie" against SZ's use of it: for SZ *Kategorien* are determinations of beings not of Dasein's character (SZ 44–46), and finitude as a determination of Dasein's being would be an existential. Henry's phrase is a Re-inscription of the categorial/existential distinction, as in §9 ¶5.

Henry says that the being is finite as object, which is to say as intuited, because intuition is founded in the process by which the essence objectifies the pure field of phenomenality as a finite horizon; "*Il est clair alors que le concept antithétique d'une intuition originaire infinie surgit dans la problématique sans aucun contexte*"; either it makes no difference to the finite nature of intuition that it is called creative, "Ou alors, [...] c'est un autre mode de manifestation qui est pensé"; but by what right is it still called intuition?; Heidegger confuses ontic and ontological when he writes "« à la connaissance finie seule peut être donnée une réalité du type objet *(Gegenstand).* Elle seule doit s'exposer à l'étant qui est déjà »" (n. 40, K 92); the character by which the being precedes a power of knowledge that does not create it is ontic, the character by which it is an *ob-jet* ontological. The charge of confusion against "Heidegger" is directed at the Kantbuch (GA 3 run) and rests on a distinction that Henry draws between the "ontic" prior being and the "ontological" being-object. SZ is independent of it. The suggestion that "another mode of manifestation is thought" under the idea of infinite intuition is the first appearance in Section II of the possibility that Henry will use to introduce his own mode of revelation (§30). SZ has no counterpart.

Henry says that, by forbidding the tying of the receptive meaning of knowledge to a character of the being, the criticism of Kant's distinction makes the ontological character of receptivity clearer: "Ce n'est pas l'étant, toutefois, qui est reçu, mais son être. Plus exactement, *l'être de l'étant réside dans sa réceptivité*. *La réceptivité n'est pas une catégorie de l'étant, elle est ontologique en tant qu'elle constitue l'essence même de l'être*." The sentence assigns receptivity to being itself. In SZ, "Entdecktheit" of beings is grounded in the disclosedness of Dasein (SZ 221), and what is received in attunement is Dasein's own thrownness, not the being of the existent; Henry's "l'être de l'étant réside dans sa réceptivité" has no SZ basis. It is a restatement of the section's thesis in the grammar of the Kantbuch.

Henry says that the understanding of the ontological character of the problem of receptivity makes the solution more urgent: "qu'est-ce donc qui rend ontologiquement possible la réceptivité de l'être, c'est-à-dire la manifestation de l'horizon pur que s'objecte l'essence originaire de l'objectivation ?" The question parallels SZ's question at its close: "Wie ist erschließendes Verstehen von Sein daseinsmäßig überhaupt möglich?" (how is disclosing understanding of being possible for Dasein at all?, SZ 437). SZ's answer is the direction of the whole, a return to the constitution of Dasein's being in temporality. Henry's question is the same in form and asks for the possibility of the horizon's manifestation in the essence itself. The following sections give the answer in terms of the Kantbuch, then replace it.

### § 23. The internal possibility of the receptivity of being and the problematic of schematism

Henry says that the answer to the possibility of the receptivity of being begins with the nature of what is to be received, the pure horizon of being, which, being nothing ontic, "est aussi bien celui du néant", though "Le néant de l'horizon que s'objecte l'essence n'est pas rien"; it cannot be grasped thematically as a being, and "c'est à la condition de n'être pas l'objet d'une saisie thématique que l'horizon laisse libre la place pour une saisie de ce genre": "« L'horizon, dit Heidegger, sera non thématique, mais restera en même temps dans le champ du regard »" (n. 41, K 180); the critique of thematism is only negative, and "Si l'horizon de l'être est compris comme le néant, alors le problème est celui d'une *phénoménologie du néant*." The quotation is from the Kantbuch and carries the point on which §18 ¶3 and the Introduction §3 depended (the horizon escapes thematizing). The Nothing as horizon is not SZ's (see the Introduction §2 ¶7), and the "horizon de l'être" in SZ is thematized in the second division as the horizonal schemata of the ecstases (SZ 365), so that the claim "non thématique" is true of the horizon's functioning and not of what the analytic can say of it (SZ 17, 147–148). The call for a "phénoménologie du néant" has its nearest SZ counterpart in the analysis of anxiety, in which the "Nichts und Nirgends" is the disclosure of the world as such (SZ 186–188), an analysis of a mood of Dasein.

Henry says that the determination of the horizon cannot explain the possibility of receptivity, which it presupposes; since the horizon is the nothing, the power that posits it "est le pouvoir de poser autre chose que l'étant, il est, comme tel, *imagination*", transcendental imagination; but the imagination is not only what forms the horizon but what receives it, "« l'unité originelle et non composée de la réceptivité et de la spontanéité »" (n. 42, K 210), and "*le problème de la structure interne de l'imagination transcendantale est celui de comprendre comment celle-ci peut être réceptrice à l'égard de ce qu'elle forme.*" The passage rests on the Kantbuch (GA 3 run). SZ names the schematism and imagination only as the places where Kant "weicht zurück" before the problematic of temporality: "Dieser Schematismus [...] ist eine verborgene Kunst in den Tiefen der menschlichen Seele" (this schematism is a hidden art in the depths of the human soul; Kant, quoted at SZ 23), and SZ announces an interpretation of the schematism chapter in its unpublished second part (SZ 23–24). The Kantbuch executes what SZ announced, and Henry's reading goes through it. SZ itself says nothing of the transcendental imagination as the power that forms and receives the horizon.

Henry says that it would be a negative and illusory solution to give as ground of imagination's receptivity that the horizon is formed by itself: Heidegger says of pure intuitions that they receive the pure view of space and time but "« cette réception est *en elle-même* un acte formateur qui se donne à lui-même ce qui s'offre »" (n. 43, K 199); that the content is formed does not explain how it is also received, for "une telle formation [...] n'a sans doute un sens phénoménologique que pour autant que ce qu'elle forme se manifeste, c'est-à-dire est reçu par elle"; "*L'essence de la spontanéité réside dans la réceptivité*"; that pure intuition is imagination does not solve the problem of the ultimate possibility of the essence of objectification. The argument is Henry's against the Kantbuch's solution and independent of SZ. It states the objection that SZ's own account of thrownness would answer in another way: what is formed in projection is received as already thrown (SZ 148), so that spontaneity rests on receptivity in an existing being (attunement, SZ 136), not on the receptivity of an essence to its product.

Henry says that the solution to the problem of the inner structure of transcendental imagination consists in saying no longer that intuition is imagination but that imagination is intuition: "« L'imagination est aussi, et même surtout, une faculté d'intuition »" (n. 44, K 210), Kant's "constamment sensible" (n. 45, K 204); the reversal occurs "au moment où se pose [...] *le problème de la perceptibilité de l'horizon, c'est-à-dire en fait celui de sa réceptivité*"; "Pour que l'étant puisse s'offrir comme tel, l'horizon dans lequel sa rencontre pourra se faire devra se manifester, lui aussi, sous la forme d'une offre sollicitante" (n. 46, K 148); "cette forme d'offre a besoin d'une certaine perceptibilité" (n. 47); "« Nous appelons perceptible ce qui est susceptible d'être immédiatement reçu par l'intuition »" (n. 48); "nécessité de sa perceptibilité immédiate dans une intuition pure" (n. 49, K 178–179); "« L'imagination appartient à la faculté d'intuition »" (n. 50, K 187). The unit follows the Kantbuch closely, and the quotations are its. The relevant SZ locus is the inversion it states: "»Anschauung« und »Denken« sind beide schon entfernte Derivate des Verstehens" (SZ 147). The Kantbuch's route, via imagination to pure intuition, is for SZ the route that treats a derivative as the ground. Henry's reading makes that explicit as a circle at the next paragraph, and SZ 147 gives him the ground for it, though he does not cite it.

Henry says that the intuition's essence lies in objectification, that the critique of intuitionism (n. 51: Introduction §3) showed intuition to be possible only through the opening of the transcendental field, and that when reflection shows the horizon of being to function only if it manifests itself, "c'est à l'intuition qu'est demandée la manifestation de cet horizon pur"; so intuition "trouve sa condition dans un horizon qui ne devient réel que pour autant qu'il est intuitionné"; "la transcendance, dans l'objectivation, *doit rendre intuitif l'horizon formé par celle-ci*" (n. 52, K 149); "La pensée qui demande à l'intuition la réalisation de ce qu'elle conçoit comme la condition de cette intuition même se meut manifestement dans un cercle." Henry treats the circle as a defect to be left behind ("doit être dépassé", ¶16 below). This differs from his own use of the circle in §8 ¶10, where it was the sign of the absoluteness of phenomenology, and from SZ 153, where the circle of understanding is "nicht ein Kreis, in dem sich eine beliebige Erkenntnis bewegt, sondern [...] der Ausdruck der existenzialen *Vor-struktur* des Daseins selbst", and the point is "in ihn nach der rechten Weise hineinzukommen". The circle Henry finds in the Kantbuch is a vicious one (the conditioned is the condition of its condition), and SZ's is not. Whether the Kantbuch's circle is of the first or the second kind is a matter for the GA 3 run; for the SZ comparison it is notable that Henry classifies as a flaw a structure which SZ elsewhere defends.

Henry says that the intuition shown to be founded in the opening of the horizon was intuition of beings, whereas the intuition of the horizon is pure; that the intuition of beings finds its ground in the manifestation of the horizon understood as intuition means that pure intuition is the condition of empirical intuition, in the sense that it "constitue son essence même": "*L'intuition empirique est pure en tant qu'elle est une intuition*"; that it manifests the being is "étranger à ce pouvoir lui-même"; when intuition, interpreted as founded in objectification, intervenes to make it possible, "le conditionné se donne paradoxalement pour la condition de la condition." The argument is Henry's analysis of the Kantbuch and independent of SZ.

Henry says that the sense of Heidegger's problematic, a Kantian commentary, is to show the essential affinity of thought and intuition and so clarify the essence of schematism: they share a common essence, imagination; intuition is spontaneous (it gives itself pure space and time) and thought is receptive (rules are received by the mind); "La thèse selon laquelle *l'intuition est imagination* appartient donc au mouvement d'une dialectique qui veut montrer *en quoi* l'intuition est identique à la pensée." The unit reads the Kantbuch's strategy. SZ's relation to it is that of program to execution: SZ promises to clear the "Dunkel der Schematismuslehre" by the problematic of temporality (SZ 23), and the Kantbuch carries it out with the imagination and time as its guides. Henry's description of the problematic as "un commentaire kantien" omits SZ's reason for it, which is the destruction of the history of ontology (SZ 19–27), and the unit has therefore no SZ locus beyond the announcement.

Henry says that imagination is transcendence itself, "l'essence qui *forme* l'horizon pur de l'être en même temps qu'elle assure sa *réception*"; to do as imagination does is "représenter": "La représentation est le processus par lequel l'essence s'objecte l'horizon pur qu'elle reçoit"; to show the essence of imagination in thought and intuition is to define them as representation, "« Mais la pensée et l'intuition, quoique distinctes, ne sont point séparées l'une de l'autre comme deux choses de nature absolument différente. L'une et l'autre appartiennent [...] comme espèces de représentation, au genre commun de la représentation en général »" (n. 53, K 205); "« La connaissance *comme représentation* est intuition ou concept »" (n. 54, K 84, with Kant's parallel). The assimilation of thought to representation is the Kantbuch's, and Henry builds on it the identification of consciousness with representation of §11. SZ declines it for the logos: "Synthesis sagt hier nicht Verbinden und Verknüpfen von Vorstellungen, Hantieren mit psychischen Vorkommnissen" (synthesis here does not mean connecting representations, handling psychic occurrences, SZ 33), and the "Als" of assertion is grounded in the hermeneutic "Als" of understanding (SZ 223). The unit gives the SZ-side contrast: for SZ thought and intuition have no common genus "representation".

Henry says that, when the essence of imagination is brought out in them, intuition and thought are shown to be, each for itself, receptive and spontaneous at once; "*La réceptivité et la spontanéité n'appartiennent donc pas à l'intuition et à la pensée considérées dans leur spécificité, elles sont le fait de l'imagination*"; "*Loin de pouvoir fonder la réceptivité, l'intuition est rendue possible par elle*"; the imagination is the supreme essence that makes schematism possible; the true problem is its inner structure, "*Comment l'imagination reçoit-elle donc l'horizon qu'elle s'objecte ?* C'est, dit-on, en l'intuitionnant. *Ainsi l'intuition qui trouve son fondement dans la réceptivité est-elle chargée de rendre celle-ci possible*." The paragraph is the Kantbuch's argument in Henry's summary and has no SZ locus. The result, that the receptivity is ontologically prior to intuition, is a point SZ also holds in another form: the receptive moment of Dasein (thrownness, attunement) is prior to and independent of the "Sicht" (SZ 134–136, 147).

Henry says that the concept of intuition is ambivalent: it is one of the two specific powers of knowledge and subject to transcendence, and it presents itself as the essence of transcendence; "En fait, le double rôle dévolu à l'intuition ne peut plus cacher mais seulement mettre en lumière l'absence de toute solution véritable au problème de la possibilité interne de l'essence"; to appeal to intuition to found transcendence is "en sorte que l'intuition se donne paradoxalement comme la condition de l'intuition" and "s'en tenir à un mode de penser traditionnel qui [...] trouve en fait ses présuppositions dans la philosophie grecque. Connaître, pour celle-ci, c'est intuitionner"; so "le caractère illusoire du dépassement de l'intuitionnisme par la philosophie transcendantale de l'être": it carried the conditions of intuition to the absolute and resolved the ultimate problems only by recourse to intuition. The charge that the transcendental philosophy of being has not overcome intuitionism cannot be turned against SZ, which declares the primacy of seeing a derivative one and starts from circumspect concern: "Die Idee des intuitus leitet [...] alle Interpretation der Erkenntnis" (SZ 358) is its diagnosis, and its genetic account derives theoretical looking from the "Umschlag" of circumspection (SZ 357–358). The target is the Kantbuch's "reine Anschauung" and "Anblick" as the schematism's last word. Henry's formulation extends the target to "la philosophie transcendantale de l'être", and SZ's own critique of the primacy of intuiting is not seen by that formulation.

Henry says that the question of schematism covers two problems: the union of thought and intuition, solved by their common essence, and the true problem, the internal possibility of that common essence, the transcendence itself, whose possibility lies in the originary manifestation of the horizon; "*C'est au schématisme qu'il est demandé sans équivoque de fonder cette possibilité ultime*": "« L'accomplissement de la transcendance devra être foncièrement schématisme »", "« Kant touche donc nécessairement au schématisme transcendantal dès qu'il veut mettre au jour le fondement de la possibilité intrinsèque de la transcendance »" (n. 55, K 159); "« Cette vérité, dit encore Heidegger, est explicitée en son essence par le schématisme transcendantal »" (n. 56, K 180); schematism "consiste dans une sensibilisation de l'horizon", the "image-schème" being "une place ouverte" visible only through its intuitive character, "« provient du fait que l'image-schème surgit… d'une présentation possible »" (n. 57, K 157), so that "En tant que le schématisme consiste dans la *formation d'une image*, il trouve sa possibilité dans l'intuition." The claim that schematism founds transcendence is the Kantbuch's, and SZ's counterpart is its own statement that schematism is where Kant stops before temporality (SZ 23). Henry's last move, that schematism rests on intuition, is Henry's reading of the Kantbuch's "Anblick".

Henry says that because intuition is the ground of schematism, the two problems (the union of thought and intuition; the internal possibility of transcendence) coincide: "*ce n'est pas de la pensée mais de son essence qu'on montre qu'elle est identiquement intuition*"; "*l'essence de la pensée est la représentation*"; "« Le concept n'est rien en dehors de la représentation de l'unité régulatrice »" (n. 58, K 156); the concept becomes visible only by becoming an image, its "transposition sensible", "L'objet visé par le concept ne devient accessible que par ce caractère intuitif" (n. 59, K 160); "« toute représentation conceptuelle est essentiellement schématisme »" (n. 60, K 159); "*C'est en tant qu'elle est représentation que la pensée est intuition*"; "« Les concepts purs de l'entendement [...] ont donc besoin d'une intuitivité essentiellement pure, *si ce qui s'oppose dans la pure ob-jectivation doit être perceptible en tant qu'opposé* »" (n. 61, K 160). The unit is the Kantbuch's thesis restated; its SZ contrast is the one already marked: SZ 33 (synthesis not a connecting of representations) and SZ 147 (intuiting derives from understanding).

Henry says that the problematic of schematism reduces to showing that thought and intuition are identical in their essence, "*c'est-à-dire en tant quelles se manifestent*", and then as representation, which implies intuition as what secures the perceptibility of what is represented; the intuition appealed to "est un pouvoir absolument général et d'ailleurs indéterminé dans son fondement, celui de rendre perceptible l'horizon pur de l'être"; "Dans la mesure pourtant où l'intuition qui fonde la possibilité du schématisme ne demeure pas totalement indéterminée [...], elle se donne comme appartenant à la sensibilité"; "C'est une seule et même intuition, en réalité, qui apparaît tour à tour comme la condition et le conditionné." The paragraph is Henry's analysis of the Kantbuch's circle and independent of SZ.

Henry says that the circle must be passed beyond, but it has a sense: it brings the problematic back to the true problem, that of receptivity, "pour laquelle l'intuition qui est le dernier mot du schématisme n'est justement qu'un nom ou, quand elle est plus que cela, un pouvoir mystérieux et non éclairci, purement et simplement emprunté à une tradition philosophique dont on a suffisamment montré cependant qu'elle devait être fondée"; n. 62: "HEIDEGGER remarque lui-même dans *Sein und Zeit* (p. 358) que « l'idée de l'intuition conduit depuis le début de l'ontologie grecque jusqu'à aujourd'hui toute interprétation de la connaissance »", adding that Kant affirms the primacy of intuition "de façon aussi solennelle qu'explicite" at the start of the *Critique*. The note is SZ's single explicit appearance in the Kantbuch part of Section II. The reference is accurate. SZ 358 says: "Die Idee des intuitus leitet [von] den Anfängen der griechischen Ontologie bis heute alle Interpretation der Erkenntnis, mag er faktisch erreichbar sein oder nicht", and it cites in the same place the Kant sentence that Henry quotes in the second half of the note ("Auf welche Art und durch welche Mittel sich auch immer eine Erkenntnis auf Gegenstände beziehen mag, es ist doch diejenige, wodurch sie sich auf dieselben unmittelbar bezieht, *und worauf alles Denken als Mittel abzweckt*, [...] die *Anschauung*"). Henry's Kant quotation, which has the same clause "auquel tend toute pensée comme au but en vue duquel elle est le moyen", comes in all likelihood through SZ, a Mediation that the note does not state. What Henry omits is SZ's use of the remark. SZ states the primacy of the *intuitus* in order to derive its existential genesis from circumspection, so that "Gemäß dem Vorrang des »Sehens« wird der Aufweis der existenzialen Genesis der Wissenschaft bei der Charakteristik der *Umsicht* einsetzen müssen" (according to the primacy of "seeing", the demonstration of the existential genesis of science must begin with the characterization of circumspection, SZ 358). Henry cites SZ as having noticed the tradition's primacy of intuition, and uses the notice against the Kantbuch's recourse to intuition. The cut is the other half of SZ's sentence, the replacement of the primacy of intuiting by that of circumspect concern; and Henry's own replacement, "receptivity", is not SZ's.

Henry says that, for concerning thought and intuition in their essence, the problematic of schematism bears on the central problem of these investigations, the essence of manifestation; that the elucidation of its inner structure has shown receptivity to ground the structure "dans sa possibilité la plus ultime", so that the problem of the essence of manifestation concentrates in that of receptivity; before clarifying its essence, the ontologically central character of the problem of receptivity must be reaffirmed, "à propos du temps". The paragraph announces §24. The announcement itself recalls SZ's: time as the horizon on which the question of being turns (SZ 17). It is Henry's choice to take time as the occasion of the reaffirmation, with the Kantbuch's pure self-affection as the guide (the GA 3 run), and not SZ's analysis of ecstatic temporality.

### § 24. Reaffirming the central place of the problem of receptivity, and the ontological interpretation of time as auto-affection

Henry says that it is no accident that the reaffirmation is made about time: since Kant time is understood as what makes manifestation possible, "la condition universelle de tous les phénomènes en général", the essence of phenomenality; the process by which the essence opposes the horizon to itself is thought as time; the ego can be given the power of objectification only by understanding it as time: "« Le moi, en formant originellement le temps, c’est-à-dire *comme temps*…, forme la nature de l’objectivation et son horizon »" (n. 63, K 248); "« Le temps, dit Heidegger, est impliqué dans la possibilité intrinsèque de cet acte d’objectivation »" (n. 64, K 244); so the problematic of time should offer a solution to the central problem. The quotations are the Kantbuch's. SZ's connection of time with the question of being is of another kind: time is the horizon of the understanding of being (SZ 1, 17) and the "I think" and time are connected in a way Kant left "in völliges Dunkel gehüllt" (SZ 24). The Kantbuch fulfils that second part of SZ's announcement, and Henry's claim that time is the essence of manifestation reads the Kantbuch's result. SZ's statement of the relation is as horizon of the understanding of being and of the world's disclosure (SZ 365), which is narrower.

Henry says that time can be implied in objectification as its intrinsic possibility only if it lets what is formed in that act manifest itself: "« Le temps, dit Heidegger, … prête d’emblée à l’horizon de la transcendance le caractère d’une offre perceptible »" (n. 65, K 166) and "« il rend perceptible à l’être fini le caractère d’« opposition » de l’objectivité »" (n. 66); the perceptibility lies in the essence's capacity to receive what it has formed, to affect itself: "*C’est parce que le temps est compris par Heidegger, à la suite de Kant, comme auto-affection qu’il est présenté comme « la possibilité intrinsèque de l’acte d’objectivation »*". The paragraph is the Kantbuch's. It makes self-affection the character by which time is the ground of objectification. SZ has no counterpart, and does not call time self-affecting. The nearest SZ concept is self-temporalizing: temporality "zeitigt sich" (SZ 365) in the unity of the ecstases, and is "das ursprüngliche »Außer-sich« an und für sich selbst" (SZ 329).

Henry says that self-affection has a double meaning, first affection by oneself: time is not affected by another thing, not by the being, so it is "affecté en dehors de l’expérience" (n. 67, K 245), experience meaning determination by an ontic element; ontic affection presupposes an ontological and pure one, in which time affects itself "*sous la forme de l’horizon pur de l’être*": "Car le temps est essentiellement horizon." Without this conception, intuition of time is confused with that of the thing in time and the now is defined from the present being; but the being was awaited and will soon be past; a pure place was opened for it; the present is "un instant évanouissant" consisting in "*voir venir et voir passer*" the thing; "Le maintenant suppose donc [...] l’acte de pré-voir et celui de retenir dans le regard, c’est-à-dire l’horizon pur qui rend possible toute présence comme telle." Henry's critique of defining the now from the present being has the shape of SZ's critique of the vulgar concept of time, which takes time as a succession of nows modelled on what is present-at-hand (SZ 18, 24–26), and SZ's own account of the now is that the present "entspringt aus Zukunft und Gewesenheit" (SZ 365: "Wie die Gegenwart in der Einheit der Zeitigung der Zeitlichkeit aus Zukunft und Gewesenheit entspringt"). Henry's structure of foreseeing and retaining is Kant's synthesis (and Husserl's) and not SZ's. The point he makes ("le temps est essentiellement horizon") is SZ's thesis in terms of the Kantbuch, and the content he gives to the horizon is a three-dimensional succession of places.

Henry says that what affects time is not the being but the pure horizon of being; the affection by oneself is not the horizon's act, since it must first be formed, and "*Cette formation est l’œuvre du temps lui-même*"; "C’est le temps originaire qui s’affecte par la médiation du temps pur"; time "forme l’essence de toute auto-sollicitation" (n. 68, K 244); as the power capable of positing something other than the being, the original time reveals itself as imagination, "« l’imagination transcendantale est le temps originel »" (n. 69, K 242); as imagination it is an intuition that produces from itself the pure content it provides (n. 70, K 199 sq.), the three-dimensional horizon of pure time rooted in the "« acte originellement formateur »" that is "« à la fois acte de voir, de pré-voir et de re-voir »" (n. 71, K 230); inner sense "ne reçoit rien du dehors" (n. 72, K 246), it "tient tout de soi" (n. 73): "*C’est parce que le temps est imagination qu’il n’est affecté par rien d’autre que par lui-même, qu’il est affection par soi*." The paragraph is the Kantbuch's thesis of time as imagination, restated, and independent of SZ. Its distinction between "temps pur" and "temps originaire" is taken up at ¶9.

Henry says that auto-affection is not identical with affection by oneself but is also, more fundamentally, "affection de soi": time is not only what affects; as what affects it presupposes what is affected, which is time itself: "*L’affection par soi trouve son fondement dans l’affection de soi*." The paragraph states Henry's point and is independent of SZ.

Henry asks whether the power by which time affects does not also posit what is affected; whether the affection of oneself results from a self-positing by which affecting time posits itself as the reality to be affected. The question is Henry's and independent of SZ.

Henry says that such questions remain speculative unless answered phenomenologically; what does the affirmation mean, from that point of view, that affection by oneself is founded in affection of oneself? The paragraph poses the phenomenological question. Its framing, "au point de vue phénoménologique", is SZ's method claim (SZ 27–35) and Henry's own.

Henry says that the time that affects is the pure imaginative power that unfolds the transcendental horizon of being, the pure succession where the being can be present, but the horizon can make the being manifest only if it first manifests itself, if the pure succession is visible in its purity; "Un temps pur, dit Kant, ne peut être perçu" (n. 74, CPR 178); this means only that the horizon cannot be grasped thematically like a being, so that it must be apprehended "*dans son contenu ontologique pur*": "*La tâche d’appréhender le contenu ontologique du temps pur est, toutefois, celle du temps lui-même*. *Tel est le sens de l’affection de soi*"; "Affecter [...] l’acte d’objectivation, en tant qu’il est orientation pure vers… veut dire qu’on lui suscite quelque opposition" (n. 75, K 244); the opposition's rise "appartient à la réceptivité de l’essence"; "*C’est le temps qui est affecté qui fonde la manifestation de l’horizon du temps pur en assurant sa réception*"; "Le temps, dit Heidegger, est, par nature, pure affection de lui-même" (n. 76, K 244); "« Le sens interne pur est l’affection pure de soi, c’est-à-dire le temps originel »" (n. 77, K 253); "« L’affection pure de soi [...] détermine l’essence profonde de la transcendance »" (n. 78, K 245). The unit is the Kantbuch's argument in Henry's summary. SZ's own relation to the "horizon of pure time" is that its horizon is not an object of reception but the "Wohin der Entrückung" of the ecstases (SZ 365), and the thematic demand ("Appréhender le contenu ontologique du temps pur") is met in SZ by the conceptual exhibition of the horizonal schemata (Umwillen, Wovor der Geworfenheit, Um-zu of the present, SZ 365). Henry's solution, that time performs the task by itself as self-affection, is the Kantbuch's.

Henry says that understanding time as self-affection imposes a radical distinction between pure time and original time: "Le temps pur est l’horizon de la succession originellement formée par les places pures du futur, du passé et du présent", the pure object of transcendence; "*Le temps pur, toutefois, n’est pas le temps réel.*" "Le temps originaire" forms the horizon of succession by receiving it, "pour autant que [...] le temps originaire qui la détermine [...] est dans son essence affection de soi"; this self-affection is the condition of all representation in general and of space; "« L’espace, dit Heidegger, est toujours… en un certain sens identique au temps »", "« *Ce n’est pas sous cette forme que le temps est le fondement originel de la transcendance, mais c’est en tant qu’il est affection pure de soi* »" (n. 79, K 254). The pair "pure time / original time" resembles in sound SZ's pair of vulgar and original time, and differs in content. SZ's "pure succession of nows" is what it calls the vulgar concept of time: the "Zeit [...] als ein Seiendes unter anderem Seienden" in Aristotle (SZ 26), which Kant's analysis keeps (SZ 24: "bleibt seine Analyse der Zeit trotz der Rücknahme dieses Phänomens in das Subjekt am überlieferten vulgären Zeitverständnis orientiert"). Henry's "temps pur" is therefore SZ's vulgar time, and his "temps originaire" (self-affection) is the Kantbuch's replacement for SZ's ecstatic temporality. Henry does not note the identity, and he never examines SZ's original time. The claim that space is founded in original time is the Kantbuch's, and SZ's counterpart is that spatiality of Dasein is grounded in ecstatic temporality (SZ §70), which is not at stake in Henry's text.

Henry takes up the objection of self-positing and calls its abstractness evident: if the time that affects posited the time to be affected, the latter would have to be received by another time, and so on; but the time posited by affecting time is the milieu of exteriority itself, "*Le temps autoposé est le temps pur*", which presupposes "le temps qui est affecté" as the condition of its manifestation; "*Le temps qui est affecté est le même que le temps qui affecte*": "Le temps originaire est le temps de l’affection". The argument is Henry's against a reading of the Kantbuch and independent of SZ. The regress it blocks (an infinite series of times) is the sort of vicious circle that SZ treats by returning to the structure of the being that understands (SZ 153), and Henry stops it by an identity.

Henry asks how time can ensure the reception of the horizon: "Le temps assure la réception de l’horizon de la transcendance *en tant qu’il intuitionne* le contenu ontologique pur que la transcendance s’objecte"; "*C’est dans la mesure où le temps est en sa nature intuition qu’il est possible comme affection de soi*. *Ce qui importe dans le temps qui rend ultimement possible l’essence de la manifestation, ce n’est pas son caractère temporel, c’est son caractère intuitif.*" The time of succession is "la pure succession de la série des *maintenant*" (n. 80, K 229); neither it nor space can be called an intuition, for they are only the content of one; "L’intuition n’est ni le temps ni l’espace pur, mais le pouvoir qui les reçoit." The unit says that what matters in time is not that it is temporal. SZ says the reverse. The horizon of the understanding of being is time because of its temporal character: "die Zeit [...] als Horizont des Seinsverständnisses aus der Zeitlichkeit als Sein des seinverstehenden Daseins" is to be explicated (SZ 17), and what is called "intuiting" is derivative of understanding: "»Anschauung« und »Denken« sind beide schon entfernte Derivate des Verstehens" (SZ 147), which is itself to be explicated from the temporality that SZ 17–19 announces and its second division works out. Henry grounds time in intuition; SZ grounds intuition in understanding and understanding in temporality. The inversion is complete and Henry does not mark it. The identification of the pure time with the succession of nows is Kant's, and SZ's critique of it (SZ 18, 24, 26) applies.

Henry says that the reason for the central role of time is visible in schematism: it forms a pure view before all objects, whose possibility is in the reception of the pure place; "C’est parce que le temps est intuition et assure comme tel la réception de cette place pure, qu’il constitue l’essence du schématisme"; if, as Kant says (n. 81, cited by Heidegger, K 161), "« l’image pure… de tous les objets des sens en général est le temps »", that is not because time, "comme forme pure de la succession, [est] l’horizon universel de l’être, « l’horizon de toute compréhension et de toute explication de l’être » (n. 82, SZ 17), *c’est parce qu’il est, comme intuition, ce qui assure la possibilité de la réception de cet horizon*"; the pure image "se donne nécessairement sous une forme intuitive dans l’image pure du temps" (n. 83, K 166), "l’élément décisif est qu’elle forme, c’est-à-dire procure une image *par un acte d’intuition*" (n. 84, K 188), and "l’imagination a une fonction originellement présentative qui s’exerce par la forme pure du temps" (n. 85, K 190); "*Le schématisme transcendantal trouve ainsi sa condition dans le temps en tant que celui-ci est une intuition.*" The quotation of SZ is exact: SZ 17 calls time "der Horizont alles Seinsverständnisses und jeder Seinsauslegung" (the horizon of all understanding of being and all interpretation of being). The use is a Re-inscription at the moment of the quotation. SZ 17 says that the horizon is time to be explicated "aus der Zeitlichkeit als Sein des seinverstehenden Daseins", and SZ 18 adds that the concept of time so gained must be set off against the vulgar concept; Henry denies that time is the horizon as "forme pure de la succession" and gives its role to intuition, which in SZ is not the ground (SZ 147). Henry's denial is directed at the Kantbuch's pure succession, and SZ 17's horizon is not that succession. The quotation is therefore accurate in text, used against the sense it has in its context, and the reading it denies is not SZ's.

Henry asks what intuition is, and quotes Heidegger on the pure succession of nows: "« l’intuition pure intuitionne cette succession sans l’objectiver »" (n. 86, K 229), which only negatively determines the act; the positive determination: "« *intuitionner signifie : recevoir ce qui s’offre* »" (n. 87, ibid.): "*L’intuition n’est qu’un nom pour la réceptivité qui assure la manifestation de l’horizon pur de l’être*. *Loin de résoudre le problème de la réceptivité de l’essence, la détermination de l’essence du temps comme intuition le pose seulement avec plus d’urgence*." The definition of intuition as receiving what offers itself is the Kantbuch's and is the notion SZ treats as the tradition's guide (SZ 358, discussed at §23 ¶14). Henry's remark that "intuition" is only a name for the unsolved problem of receptivity repeats his criticism of the Kantbuch and agrees with SZ's view that the primacy of intuiting is a derivative one (SZ 147).

Henry concludes that the intervention of time leads, despite its claim to constitute "la possibilité intrinsèque de l’acte d’objectivation", to "une simple répétition" of the problematic: since time plays its role as intuition, itself conceived as the condition of the transcendence in which it is founded, "le caractère circulaire de la problématique du schématisme n’est pas levé par l’intervention du temps mais seulement rendu plus évident par elle"; the repetition confirms the task: "l’élucidation de l’essence de la réceptivité." SZ offers a solution to the problem of how the horizon is "there" for the transcending being that does not pass through intuition: the world, with its horizon, "zeitigt sich in der Zeitlichkeit. Sie »ist« mit dem Außer-sich der Ekstasen »da«" (it temporalizes itself in temporality; it "is there" with the outside-itself of the ecstases, SZ 365), and the unity of the horizonal schemata "gründet in der ekstatischen Einheit der Zeitlichkeit" (SZ 365). Section II never examines this, though it is SZ's answer to the question of the horizon's givenness. The unit is the place where Henry's exclusive reliance on the Kantbuch for the question of time becomes visible, since a reader who takes the Kantbuch as the continuation of SZ would expect Henry's conclusion to bear on the analytic of temporality, and it does not.

### § 25. Clarifying the essence of receptivity and the problem of determining phenomenologically the original reality of transcendence

Henry says that, according to monism's ultimate presuppositions, the essence manifests itself only by objectifying itself as the horizon it opposes to itself; the manifestation of the horizon is "identiquement l'acte dans lequel l'essence le reçoit"; "*La réalité de l'acte qui reçoit réside dans sa manifestation*. *La détermination de l'essence de la réceptivité est celle de son statut phénoménologique*"; and since the act that receives the horizon is the essence itself, its manifestation is that of the essence. The paragraph restates the problem and is independent of SZ. SZ's analogue of "the act that receives and is the essence" is Dasein's being, for which the manifestation of its being is part of its constitution: "Diesem Seienden eignet, daß mit und durch sein Sein dieses ihm selbst erschlossen ist" (it is proper to this being that with and through its being this being is disclosed to it, SZ 12). Henry's problem, how the act that opens the horizon can itself be manifest, is therefore one that SZ answers with Dasein's self-disclosedness (understanding, attunement, "Durchsichtigkeit", SZ 12, 134–136, 146), and Henry's text asks it as if the philosophy of transcendence had no answer.

Henry says that the manifestation of the receiving act resides in the process by which the essence objectifies itself, so its phenomenological reality lies in what it produces; "La transcendance s’élance en avant parce que [...] elle crée, avec ce dehors, l’avant-plan de lumière qui constitue la dimension effective de la phénoménalité"; but "*Transférer la réalité de l’acte qui ouvre le milieu de l’être à ce milieu ouvert, c’est, en l’absence de toute problématique explicitement dirigée sur le mode originaire de manifestation de cet acte, attribuer à celui-ci le statut phénoménologique de ce qui se trouve, en fait, produit par lui*"; the perceptibility of the horizon belongs to transcendence, not to the horizon; "L’horizon pur de l’être considéré en lui-même est abstrait"; "Du temps pur il a été montré qu’il n’est pas le temps réel"; the pretension to circumscribe the act's reality within the horizon "est celle de fonder la réalité du temps originaire dans le temps pur". The charge of transferring the being of the opening act to what it opens is aimed at the Kantbuch's treatment of the horizon as "perceptible". SZ would accept the distinction in its own terms, since Dasein's being is not read off from the world it opens, and the world is not an item that Dasein produces: "Wenn kein Dasein existiert, ist auch keine Welt »da«" (SZ 365), but world "zeitigt sich in der Zeitlichkeit" without being a product of an act. "Avant-plan de lumière" is the essay's "Vordergrund des Lichtes" (GA 5 run, guard 11). The argument that the real time is the original, not the pure, is built on §24 ¶9 and carries the identification of SZ's vulgar time with "pure time" discussed there.

Henry says that, once the reality of transcendence is no longer defined from the horizon, thought attaches to the act in itself, whose essence is receptivity, the power of "retenir près de soi en le recevant l’horizon que l’essence a elle-même formé"; is retaining not identically "celui de l’opposer et de le maintenir devant soi" and so the originary act by which transcendence unfolds the horizon? The paragraph is Henry's identification of receiving and opposing, and independent of SZ. The act of "maintaining before oneself" is the Kantbuch's Vor-halten and has no SZ counterpart.

Henry says that the thesis that the horizon's receptivity lies in its being formed by the essence (criticized at n. 88: §23) does not coincide with the thesis now set aside, which places the reality of the receiving act in the horizon; the unity of affecting and affected time cannot be taken as overcoming the duality of powers "si cette dualité se découvre en fait à l’analyse"; the grasp of this unity is the understanding of the identity of the act that forms the horizon and the act that receives it; "*l’horizon devient perceptible dans l’acte même par lequel l’essence le pose devant elle*"; "Être vu, toutefois, cela veut dire être reçu. *Parce que l’acte de poser devant est identiquement l’acte de voir, la possibilité de la vision, c’est-à-dire de la réception de ce qu’il s’agit de recevoir, se trouve immédiatement incluse en lui*"; so "la thèse en vertu de laquelle la formation effective de l’horizon réside dans la réceptivité n’apparaît-elle pas dès lors comme devant être renversée ?"; "l’affirmation selon laquelle l’essence de la spontanéité réside dans la réceptivité n’est-elle pas un peu hâtive ?" Henry reverses his §23 ¶3 and calls it hasty. The identification of positing-before with seeing is the Kantbuch's, and the reversal is Henry's own dialectic. SZ's position on the sight of the horizon is that "Sicht" is the clearing of the Da, grounded in understanding (SZ 147), so that the act of positing before is not an act of vision but a projection (Entwurf) that is thrown (SZ 148). The unit gives the debate within the Kantbuch's terms, and has no SZ locus beyond that.

Henry says that the reception of the horizon lies in the act by which the essence forms it by positing it before itself; "*A l’acte de poser devant de la transcendance il manque toutefois, pour être réel, la manifestation de soi*"; what manifests itself is the original farness it shapes, "La phénoménalité luit là-bas dans l’espace libre qu’a ouvert la transcendance"; "*La réalité de la transcendance n’est cependant pas définie par la phénoménalité de l’horizon transcendantal de l’être, elle est y bien plutôt y présupposée par celle-ci*"; the presupposition of transcendence "demeure toutefois totalement indéterminée [...] aussi longtemps que *ne se manifeste pas [...] la transcendance elle-même dans sa réalité propre*"; the thesis that reception lies in formation "lui emprunte pourtant toute sa force" from the thesis it denies. This is the objection that the philosophy of transcendence cannot make the act of transcendence itself manifest. SZ has a reply: transcendence is not an act that must be manifested by a further manifestation; the being of Dasein is disclosed in its own understanding, and "Erschlossenheit" is its basic mode of being (SZ 221). "Das Seiende, das wesenhaft durch das In-der-Welt-sein konstituiert wird, *ist* selbst je sein »Da«" (the being constituted by being-in-the-world is in each case its "Da", SZ 132). The self-manifestation that Henry demands of the act is Dasein's disclosedness of itself, not of an act apart from it. Henry does not consider it, and his own answer (§§30–31) will be the immanence of an absolute self-revelation. The comparison is sharp: both ask how the opening of the horizon can itself be open, and SZ locates the answer in the constitution of the existing being.

Henry asks whether, if the phenomenality of the horizon does not contain the reality of the act of transcendence it presupposes, the reality of this act must be sought "*en dehors du milieu ontologique de la vérité*", and whether the philosophy of transcendence can claim its inability to grasp it as a witness to its own fidelity to the real, if truth finds its condition "dans une non-vérité plus ancienne qu’elle"; if Kant's foundation of metaphysics "« ne mène pas à l’évidence absolue et claire d’une première thèse ou d’un premier principe mais… se dirige et nous renvoie consciemment vers l’inconnu »" (n. 89, K 97–98), it is because it focuses on what cannot be exhibited; with transcendental imagination thought faces "l’« abîme de la métaphysique »". The quotations are from the Kantbuch. The "non-vérité plus ancienne" is Heidegger's of the 1930s on concealment. SZ's own "Unwahrheit" is equiprimordial with truth in Dasein, whose falling covers beings: "Das Dasein ist gleichursprünglich in der Wahrheit und Unwahrheit" (SZ 223), and its word for the abyss is that "»Grund« nur als Sinn zugänglich wird, und sei er selbst der Abgrund der Sinnlosigkeit" (SZ 152). So SZ already allows that the ground can be the abyss of meaninglessness, but only as a sense that is accessible, which is the denial of an inaccessible, older non-truth. The unit therefore joins Heidegger's two late themes (non-truth, abyss) that go beyond SZ, and takes them as the horizon within which the Kantbuch's "unknown" is read.

Henry says that the pretension to assign to the accomplished being of phenomenality a non-phenomenal ground was challenged when the paradox of the philosophy of consciousness, forced to seek the principle of consciousness in the non-conscious, was denounced; the difficulty concerned the philosophy of being as well, so long as it found in the being a condition of manifestation; the evidence then was that the effective rise of phenomenality is the work of the essence alone; what was thought as realizing phenomenality was "l’objet" and not the being, so it rests on "le processus ontologique de l’objectivation considéré en et pour lui-même"; the true problem is the relation of effective manifestation to the power that grounds it; but this relation becomes unintelligible "quand l’horizon de l’être et l’acte originaire de la transcendance retombent en fait chacun de leur côté, l’un dans la lumière [...], l’autre dans la nuit"; "La vérité ontologique et la non-vérité qui la fonde sont deux essences juxtaposées"; defining non-truth as an essence is "la simple réalisation ontologique de ce qui demeure en fait en lui-même dans une indétermination totale"; "Que signifie enfin le non de la non-essence de la non-vérité ?"; the philosophy of being cannot avoid the recourse to the reflective method. The objection of juxtaposed essences is aimed at the late Heidegger's doctrine of truth and untruth as two essences, which is not SZ's. SZ's untruth is a moment of Dasein's disclosedness, "das Seiende ist nicht völlig verborgen, sondern gerade entdeckt, aber zugleich verstellt; es zeigt sich – aber im Modus des Scheins" (SZ 222), and is not an essence juxtaposed to truth. The objection therefore does not touch SZ's account, and Henry's formulation shows that the account he takes as the philosophy of being is the later one.

Henry says that the ontological indeterminacy of the non-essence of non-truth also concerns what is founded on it: "Comme la réalisation de la non-phénoménalité dans la non-essence de la non-vérité est arbitraire, arbitraire est aussi la réalisation de la phénoménalité dans le milieu ouvert de l’extériorité pure"; the conditions "existent, elles appartiennent au milieu ontologiquement indéterminé de la non-essence"; defined as non-truth they leave the essences isolated; "Car la réalité du fondement est dans le pouvoir qu’il a de produire ce qu’il fonde, comme la réalité de ce qu’il fonde est en lui"; the division in reality between what shows and what does not show "ne satisfait qu’en apparence aux exigences de la phénoménologie". The paragraph carries the criticism to the concept of ground. For SZ, ground is "nur als Sinn zugänglich" (SZ 152), and SZ does not divide the real between what shows itself and what does not, for it says that "»Hinter« den Phänomenen der Phänomenologie steht wesenhaft nichts anderes" (SZ 36). Henry's criticism of the partition agrees with SZ's refusal of a hidden ground behind the phenomena, applied to a Heideggerian doctrine of the later period. It brings Henry closer to SZ 36 than to the thinker whom he criticizes.

### § 26. The intrusion of man into the problematic of receptivity, and the fact that the original conditions of truth do not belong to the absolute milieu of exteriority

Henry says that realizing the essence of truth outside the essential being of the ground, in the abstraction of the horizon's separate being, is related to the dialectical reversal by which the philosophy of being forbids itself to seek the principle of intelligibility in a human subjectivity; the radical exteriority of the milieu is interpreted as "*une extériorité de cette vérité, c’est-à-dire de l’être lui-même, par rapport à l’homme*"; if phenomenality resides in the original spatiality of the open space, if this pure transcendental space "« ne se trouve pas dans le sujet »" (n. 90, "*SZ*, III") but constitutes its being, its subjectivity, then the man "ne la possède plus. C’est elle au contraire qui le possède"; "Au moment où l’homme est compris, non plus comme l’origine de la lumière, mais seulement comme ce qui est éclairé par elle, la philosophie de l’être peut se considérer à bon droit comme impliquant le rejet de l’idéalisme". The note's "III" is, by the sentence it supports, SZ 111: "Der Raum ist weder im Subjekt, noch ist die Welt im Raum" (SZ 111; the numeral is a misprint or conversion error for the page). The reference is accurate, and the use goes beyond it. SZ 111 says that space is "in" the world, as disclosed by being-in-the-world; it does not say that space is the subject's being, and it does not say that man is only lit. On the contrary: "Es ist »erleuchtet«, besagt: an ihm selbst als In-der-Welt-sein gelichtet, nicht durch ein anderes Seiendes, sondern so, daß es selbst die Lichtung *ist*" (it is "illuminated" means: cleared in itself as being-in-the-world, not through another being, but in such a way that it is itself the clearing, SZ 133). SZ makes Dasein the clearing, and not what is lit by it. Henry's account of "la philosophie de l'être" as making man what is lit is therefore not SZ's. He is describing the Heidegger of the post-1945 texts he cites (n. 91 here, the Humanism letter at n. 107 of this Section), and attributing it to the philosophy of being in general. The unit is a Mediation with a Selection of SZ 111.

Henry says that whether phenomenality has its principle in man's essence is hard to settle until it is known what man is, and "des rapports qui unissent la phénoménologie de l’ego à l’ontologie fondamentale"; for the present purpose it suffices that "Si l’insertion des conditions d’intelligibilité dans le milieu ontologique de l’extériorité amène à réaliser la vérité en dehors de l’homme, [...] la réception de cette vérité par l’homme lui-même" becomes a question; "*La réalisation de la vérité en dehors de l’homme ne signifie rien de moins que la non-appartenance de celui-ci au milieu ontologique de l’être*"; separated from it, man is "une réalité d’ordre ontique"; "Être éclairé par l’être, cela signifie pour l’homme recevoir sa lumière, de telle manière cependant qu’il devienne lui-même cette lumière"; "« Il appartient à la vérité de l’être, dit Heidegger, de relier à elle, *d’une manière privilégiée*, l’essence de l’homme »" (n. 91, *La Remontée au fondement de la métaphysique*, trad. Rovan, 893). The quotation is from Heidegger's 1949 introduction to *Was ist Metaphysik?*, which is not in the corpus, and the passage that "man belongs to the truth of being in a privileged way" is the later thought. SZ's claim about the relation of the "phenomenology of the ego" to fundamental ontology is the one Henry has stated at the Introduction §7: the existential analytic is fundamental ontology (SZ 13), and Dasein belongs to the milieu of being by constitution, since its understanding of being is a determination of its own being (SZ 12). Henry's statement that the realization of truth outside man means man's non-belonging to the milieu of being is correct for the later Heidegger as he reads it and false for SZ, where man (Dasein) is the being for whom the milieu is "da" (SZ 132–133, 365). The verdict on the sentence is a retrospective one: the 1949 text is later than SZ and Henry cites it as a witness to the philosophy of being, and it is not a witness to SZ.

Henry says that being lit by being means for man to receive its light "de telle manière cependant qu’il devienne lui-même cette lumière"; the being is incapable of that by itself, so "Comment donc la reçoit-il, sinon sur le fond en lui de l’essence originaire et pure de la manifestation ?"; the essence of truth cannot be realized outside man without posing "celui de la présence en lui de cette vérité"; "*Plus essentielle, toutefois, que la question de savoir comment l’homme reçoit la vérité de l’essence est celle de savoir comment cette essence se reçoit elle-même*." The paragraph is Henry's own step: man receives truth only through the pure ontological power in him, the essence's capacity to receive itself. SZ is independent of it, and the nearest SZ statement is the opposite. For SZ man's receiving the truth of being is nothing apart from the self-disclosure of Dasein, which is not a power of "the essence" (SZ 133, 221).

Henry says that the intervention of man in the problematic "a seulement pour effet de la détourner de son vrai problème": the possibility lies "tout entière dans l’essence de la manifestation, non dans l’homme"; the essence assures the reception of truth in man; that truth does not reside in man does not mean it coincides with the milieu of pure exteriority; the philosophy of being implicitly presupposes the reverse "en opposant la vérité à l’homme"; "A l’homme est subrepticement confié le pouvoir essentiel de l’essence, celui de se recevoir elle-même." This is a claim about the philosophy of being, with no SZ locus; the statement that man's reception of truth conceals the essence's self-reception is Henry's. SZ says that the clearing is Dasein's own, and the power of self-reception belongs on SZ's account to the existing being (SZ 133). The unit is thus a Contestation of what Henry takes the philosophy of being to hold, and in the passage the denial that the truth resides in man differs from SZ's claim that truth is "nur [...] solange Dasein ist" (SZ 226).

Henry says that the intervention of man has a double meaning: first, to mask the ultimate possibility by realizing the conditions of truth in pure exteriority, while setting against it "un homme dépouillé du pouvoir ontologique qu’on vient de situer hors de lui"; second, what must be returned to this man is not exactly what was placed outside him: "*Ce qui assure dans l’homme la possibilité pour lui de s’ouvrir au milieu de l’extériorité n’est pas ce milieu lui-même, c’est la possibilité justement de s’ouvrir à lui, c’est-à-dire la réceptivité comme telle*"; "*La réceptivité est la possibilité la plus ultime de la vérité*." The paragraph is Henry's argument, and independent of SZ. The result, that man's openness to the milieu is the receptivity and not the milieu, is a form of SZ's thesis that Dasein is its "Da" (SZ 132), expressed in the idiom of the Kantbuch.

Henry says that the second meaning is masked by the prestige of the themes that accompany the realization of truth in pure exteriority, first "La finitude de l’homme"; "Être fini, cela veut dire pour l’homme qui ne porte plus en lui le principe de la phénoménalité, être séparé de la vérité. Ce qui est séparé de la vérité [...] est en lui-même obscur. *L’obscurité intrinsèque de la nature humaine est le sens de sa finitude*"; in Malebranche the human soul is given over to the obscure and confused sphere of feeling, "L’obscurité de l’âme est liée au thème religieux du néant de l’homme fini et pécheur", while intelligibility of extension signifies "l’identification de la vérité [...] avec la spatialité transcendantale et pure de l’extériorité" (n. 92, Henry's note on Malebranche's intelligible extension). The finitude Henry criticizes here, an obscurity of man's nature, is not SZ's. SZ's finitude is originary time's (SZ 331) and its "Dunkel" is the thrownness of the Da, whose "Rätselhaftigkeit" is disclosed by attunement (SZ 136), not a feature of a nature cut off from truth. The unit's target is the religious-metaphysical theme of finitude in the philosophy of exteriority, and SZ is not in it.

Henry says that the human understanding's having no ideas in itself, intelligibility lying outside it, does not solve the problem of its internal possibility to receive the ideas, which "devient en fait incompréhensible quand ce dans quoi elle réside n’est pas défini autrement que par son hétérogénéité radicale"; Guéroult asks: "« Que devient cet entendement, si tout ce qui est clarté, raison, est plus haut que lui, hors de lui…, s’il est réduit… à une faculté passive… affective ? »" (n. 93, Guéroult, *Étendue et psychologie chez Malebranche*, 24–25); the passive character must be founded, and "les conditions qui fondent le caractère passif de l’entendement [...] c’est-à-dire les conditions qui constituent la réceptivité elle-même, ne sont pas contenues dans l’essence d’une humanité qui n’est rien d’autre que ce qui n’est pas la vérité". The paragraph is Malebranche and Guéroult, and independent of SZ.

Henry concludes that "ce n’est pas l’homme, à vrai dire, qui est ici en question, ce n’est pas lui qui est obscur": "*L’obscurité attribuée à l’homme par le thème existentiel et religieux de la finitude est en fait celle du pouvoir qui assure en lui la réception de la vérité*"; where it claims a positive sense, its non-phenomenality "n’est encore [...] que la simple réalisation de la négation de la phénoménalité propre au milieu pur de l’extériorité"; "*L’obscurité de l’âme humaine cache en elle l’essence ontologique de la non-vérité. L’impossibilité pour l’homme fini et pécheur de recevoir du moins la vérité recouvre l’impossibilité pour l’essence de la non-vérité de s’unir à l’essence de la vérité, c’est-à-dire de la fonder.*" The paragraph transfers the obscurity from man to the power of reception, and reads the religious theme as the form of the problem of §25. SZ's use of the word "existentiel" for this theme ("thème existentiel et religieux de la finitude") is Henry's, and in SZ the existentiell is a mode of Dasein's self-understanding (SZ 12–13), not a theme. The sentence on the obscurity of the soul as that of the non-truth repeats the juxtaposition of essences Henry criticized, and SZ's untruth is differently placed (SZ 222–223).

### § 27. Grasping the central place of the problematic of receptivity and putting in question the ultimate ontological presuppositions of monism

Henry says that the central character of the problem of receptivity emerges when its essence is no longer interpreted first and exclusively in relation to the horizon it receives; that relation showed receptivity to be the originary act by which transcendence forms the view of the horizon by positing it; if the perceptibility of the horizon rests on that act, "un tel acte est justement le fondement"; radically understood, the problem concerns "la possibilité de la réception de la transcendance elle-même"; "*Car ce qui doit être reçu n’est pas originairement ce qui se trouve formé dans l’acte imaginatif de l’essence, mais ce qui rend possible cette formation, le pouvoir imaginatif lui-même identique en son fond avec l’essence.*" The paragraph is Henry's own step, from the receptivity of the horizon to that of transcendence, and independent of SZ. It is the point at which his problem becomes SZ's, the question of how the opening act, a being's own constitution, is itself manifest to it (SZ 12, 132–133, 284).

Henry says that "*Être reçu [...] cela veut dire se manifester*"; the receptivity that is the possibility of receiving the originary act of transcendence "est identiquement la possibilité de la manifestation de cet acte"; what is put in question is "le statut phénoménologique de cet acte, c'est-à-dire en fait la réalité de ce qui dans l'essence constitue sa possibilité la plus ultime", whence the central role of the problem. The statement of the question is the one SZ answers for Dasein with a thesis about attunement: thrownness is not an event behind Dasein but its "Daß", which it is as care, and which attunement discloses: Dasein "ruht [...] in seiner Schwere, die ihm die Stimmung als Last offenbar macht" (it rests in its heaviness, which mood discloses to it as a burden, SZ 284). The answer to "how is the ground manifest?" is given there in affective terms. Henry arrives at the question without noting that SZ has already placed an answer in mood, and the next division of his book (Section IV, affectivity) will give an answer of that kind.

Henry says that, radically understood, receptivity is "encore que le titre d’un problème": it designates manifestation in general; the horizon's receptivity is its perceptibility; the question is how the horizon becomes perceptible: "Par et dans la transcendance. La transcendance est le comment de la perceptibilité de l’horizon"; the transcendence is the essence of manifestation as "le comment" of it; it makes the horizon's phenomenality arise "dans l’acte même par lequel elle le pose devant elle". The unit uses "le comment" for the mode of manifestation, which the Introduction §7 ¶6 and §8 ¶9 derived from SZ's *Wie* of the method (SZ 27) and moved to the ontological plane. It is the same re-inscription, and the use here confirms that Henry now takes "comment" as the name of the essence. SZ has no counterpart.

Henry says that, as the horizon's receptivity refers to the "comment" of this receptivity, transcendence, "*le problème de la réceptivité compris dans sa signification radicale [...] renvoie au comment de la possibilité de cette réceptivité dernière et fondamentale*"; the question rises: "*le comment qui rend possible la réceptivité de la transcendance elle-même est-il le même que le comment qui rend possible la réceptivité de l’horizon transcendantal de l’être ?* [...] *revient à demander si la transcendance assure elle-même la possibilité de sa propre réceptivité, revient à demander si la transcendance est l’essence.*" The question is the core of the section and SZ answers it in terms of the ground: "Das Dasein ist nicht insofern selbst der Grund seines Seins, als dieser aus eigenem Entwurf erst entspringt, wohl aber ist es als Selbstsein das *Sein* des Grundes" (Dasein is not the ground of its being in the sense that this springs from its own projection, but it is, as self, the being of the ground, SZ 285), and "Das Selbst, das als solches den Grund seiner selbst zu legen hat, kann dessen *nie* mächtig werden" (the self that has to lay the ground of itself can never become master of it, SZ 284). Whether transcendence secures the possibility of its own receptivity is, for SZ, a question to which the answer is that it does not, since Dasein is thrown, "*nicht* von ihm selbst in sein Da gebracht" (SZ 284). Henry's question and SZ's answer meet at the point where he concludes in ¶5 that transcendence cannot assure its own manifestation. The difference, discussed below, lies in what each draws from it.

Henry says that the phenomenological meaning of the question must be understood: if transcendence is the "comment" of the horizon's receptivity, the horizon's exteriority is the meaning of its manifestation, and "l’extériorité définit une dimension de la phénoménalité"; to the question whether what phenomenalizes itself originally in this dimension is transcendence, "la réponse a été donnée quand il a été montré que la réalité de la transcendance ne réside pas dans le milieu ouvert de l’extériorité pure"; so the originary manifestation of transcendence is not that of the horizon, and the "comment" that makes transcendence's receptivity possible is not the "comment" of the horizon's; "*la transcendance n’est pas ce qui assure la possibilité de sa propre réceptivité, [...] la manifestation originaire de la transcendance n’est pas l’œuvre de la transcendance elle-même.*" The claim that Dasein's being is not found in the world it opens is SZ's: world "ist" with the ecstases and Dasein is not an item of it (SZ 365). The result, that the originary manifestation of transcendence is not its own work, is SZ's thesis of thrownness: Dasein "kommt nie hinter seine Geworfenheit zurück" (never gets behind its thrownness, SZ 284). Henry presents the result as a discovery against monism and the philosophy of transcendence.

Henry concludes: "*La mise en évidence de l’incapacité de la transcendance à assurer elle-même la possibilité de sa propre manifestation est identiquement la mise en question des présuppositions ontologiques fondamentales du monisme*"; the impossibility for transcendence of founding itself, "de constituer ainsi l’essence d’un fondement", is the abstractness of the essence of manifestation within monism and explains the failure to determine the being of the ground. The inference is Henry's. It is not the inference SZ draws from the same structure. From the fact that Dasein is not its own ground (SZ 284–285), SZ concludes that Dasein's being is care in its unity of thrownness, projection and falling (SZ 284), a being of nullity in its ground (*Nichtigkeit*, SZ 285), and the nullity "bedeutet keineswegs Nichtvorhandensein" but constitutes the being of Dasein. SZ does not conclude that the philosophy of transcendence is abstract. The two views take the same premise (transcendence is not its own ground) and differ on the verdict. Henry's verdict is that a ground is needed beyond transcendence; SZ's is that Dasein is "das *Sein* des Grundes" without being its master. This is the clearest point in Section II at which a fair statement of what SZ says would have changed the argument, and it returns in Section III (§42, Nichtigkeit).

### § 28. The abstract character of the essence of manifestation within the ontological presuppositions of monism, and the problem of constructing a phenomenology of the ground

Henry says that the essence's autonomy was first thought as the non-belonging of the being to its inner structure, in the possibility of abstracting from ontic determination, its *Selbständigkeit*; if the "pour-soi" designates the essence of manifestation, it is absurd to say it is *unselbständig* (n. 94, EN 679) or "n’est aucunement une substance autonome" (n. 95, EN 712); the absurdity increases when the ontological insufficiency of consciousness is interpreted as lack of the in-itself: "« La conscience, dit Sartre, est un abstrait puisqu’elle recèle en elle-même une origine ontologique vers l’en-soi »" (n. 96, EN 37); the themes of man as lack, need and desire, the vanity of the ontological, the "descriptions « existentielles »" of the failure of the "projet" "ont contre elles la confusion des concepts sur lesquels elles s’appuient". The paragraph is directed at Sartre, with a reminiscence of SZ's *Selbständigkeit* in the vocabulary of the Kantbuch. Its relation to SZ is that SZ's *Nichtigkeit* is explicitly not a lack: "Nichtigkeit bedeutet keineswegs Nichtvorhandensein, Nichtbestehen" (SZ 285), and the "Nicht" of guilt excludes measurement against "ein mögliches bzw. gefordertes Vorhandenes", so that the one who is the ground of a nullity cannot be reckoned as "mangelhaft" (SZ 284). Henry's criticism of the interpretation of consciousness as lack therefore does not reach SZ's nullity, though he extends it to the "descriptions existentielles" in general.

Henry says that the confusion is clearer when Sartre writes that if the cogito leads necessarily outside itself, "c’est qu’elle n’a par elle-même aucune suffisance d’être comme subjectivité absolue, elle renvoie d’abord à la chose" (n. 97, EN 712); the reference is to alterity itself, "le mouvement vers le dehors comme tel, vers l’extériorité pure": "C’est parce que la phénoménalité est implicitement identifiée avec l’extériorité que la conscience est pensée comme ne se réalisant qu’en allant à l’extérieur de soi". The paragraph is Sartre, read as symptom of monism, and independent of SZ.

Henry says that the same confusion is seen in Sartre's definition of the concrete as "« la totalité synthétique dont la conscience comme le phénomène ne constituent que des articulations »" (n. 98, EN 219); behind "phénomène" what is thought is "l'objectivité de l'objet, l'extériorité"; "*La conscience est abstraite en tant qu’elle est séparée de la phénoménalité*", that is, "en tant qu’elle ne s’est pas encore réalisée dans l’extériorité, en tant qu’elle reste « en elle-même »". The paragraph is Sartre and Henry, and independent of SZ. Its use of "phénomène" for the objectivity of the object, as opposed to SZ's *Phänomen* as what shows itself of itself (SZ 28–31), is the same shift noted at §8.

Henry asks what the consciousness considered "en elle-même" is: "N’est-elle absolument rien ?" Or does the phenomenality that resides in exteriority not have as condition the becoming of exteriority? "*La conscience en elle-même est la transcendance*. *L’abstraction de la conscience considérée en elle-même est l’abstraction de la transcendance*." The unit is Henry's identification of consciousness in itself with transcendence, and independent of SZ. The identification is the one SZ 24 and 46 call an unanswered question about the subject's being.

Henry asks how transcendence can be abstract, when the power that unfolds the horizon founds its phenomenality; considered in itself, "la conscience est le terme concret"; Hegel's phrase "« la manifestation immédiate de la vérité »", "« l’abstraction de son être-présent dont l’essence et l’être-en-soi sont le concept absolu »" applies to the manifestation of the horizon; why then is the essence said *unselbständig*? "Parce que [...] elle est séparée de la phénoménalité effective, parce qu’en elle-même elle ne se manifeste pas. *L’abstraction de la transcendance signifie sa non-phénoménalité*." "*Singulièrement éclairant est le paradoxe qui retire au fondement sa réalité s’il intervient au moment de la détermination phénoménologique de cette réalité*"; "*Ainsi l’être est-il retiré au fondement au moment même où se pose le problème de l’être du fondement*"; "*La réalité se déplace comme se déplace la manifestation*." The structure described, the ground whose being is withdrawn when one asks for it, has its SZ form in the thesis that Dasein as ground can never become master of itself: "Grundseiend *ist* es selbst eine Nichtigkeit seiner selbst" (being ground, it is itself a nullity of itself, SZ 285). For SZ the withdrawal is part of Dasein's being, and the being of the ground is not elsewhere than in it ("es ist nie existent *vor* seinem Grunde, sondern je nur *aus ihm* und *als dieser*", SZ 285). Henry's "la réalité se déplace" into exteriority is the monism's version, and SZ's account has a ground that stays with Dasein as its burden. The paradox Henry describes belongs to the philosophy that looks for being in manifestation.

Henry says that the determination of the ground's being as "être en dehors de son être" (Fichte) conforms to monism, but leaves the ground itself indeterminate; "l’être-à-l’extérieur-de-soi du fondement n’est possible que sur le fond du fondement lui-même en tant qu’il fonde son propre être-à-l’extérieur-de-soi"; the abstract consciousness "qu’on ne peut que critiquer mais dont on ne peut non plus se passer" attests that one cannot forget what is implied in the idea of a ground. The paragraph is Fichte and Henry's argument, and independent of SZ. SZ's phrase for the structure is "Grundsein einer Nichtigkeit" (SZ 284), which has no relation to "être en dehors de son être".

Henry says that the problematic falls into a dialectical situation, returned endlessly from essence to its being-outside-itself; what legitimates this is perhaps the dialectic's idea that only the totality is concrete; but a ground cannot enter as an element in a synthesis; "*C’est précisément au moment où la problématique se montre incapable de déterminer la réalité du fondement qu’elle a recours au schéma dialectique*"; "comme si la *Selbständigkeit* pouvait résulter de la réunion de deux éléments *unselbständig*." The paragraph is a criticism of dialectic as a refuge, and independent of SZ. SZ also declines dialectic as an ontological resource: it is "aus einer echten philosophischen Verlegenheit" and appears where the ontological guide is unclear (SZ 25–26 on Plato's dialectic).

Henry says that the dialectical situation is raised to its highest tension when the perpetual return is ended only by identification of essence and exteriority, as when it is said that "« le champ où l’*Erscheinen* parvient à l’intuition de soi est fait de l’*Erscheinen* lui-même et par lui »" (n. 99, H 134; GA 5 run); the proposition is true in its indeterminate generality, "*En elle s’annonce le caractère décisif de l’essence, celui d’être le fondement de sa propre manifestation*", whose possibility "réside dans la manifestation du fondement lui-même, c'est-à-dire de la transcendance comme telle"; that the field is "fait par lui" invites reflection on the nature of *Erscheinen* "en tant qu’il n’est pas seulement ce champ lui-même mais encore ce qui le crée"; the determination of the field as made of the act of appearing and by it "repose sur une confusion, plus exactement sur l’ambiguïté de l’« apparaître » lui-même en tant que *celui-ci désigne à la fois la phénoménalité de l’horizon transcendantal de l’être et la transcendance elle-même*"; the identification of transcendence and horizon is the attempt to give the ground a phenomenological status, and it fails; "la manifestation de l’horizon n’est possible que par la transcendance, c’est-à-dire justement sur le fond de quelque chose qui lui échappe." Henry finds in the essay's formulation an ambiguity of "apparaître" that he resolves by distinguishing the phenomenality of the horizon from the transcendence that forms it. SZ's ambiguity of "Erscheinung" is a different one: appearing as announcing what does not show, as showing itself, and as mere appearance (SZ 29–30). The two ambiguities are not the same, and Henry's French "apparaître" cannot register SZ's; his resolution, in the same paragraph, is made within a vocabulary in which *Erscheinen* is what the essay says it is, so the SZ distinction has no place in it. The H 134 quotation and its French rendering are treated in the GA 5 run (guard 8).
