Henry says that with this understanding the problematic is referred back to a problem: "*elle dépend de la cohérence de la structure interne de l’acte d’objectivation considéré en lui-même*"; transcendence can retain the horizon only if it first retains itself in the originary unity of its structure; "*L’unité originairement cohérente du mouvement de la transcendance réside dans le maintien près de soi de ce mouvement dans son accomplissement*"; what lets the movement stay near itself is identical with its originary revelation to itself; "*L’unité originairement cohérente du mouvement de la transcendance considéré dans sa structure propre réside dans l’immanence.*" The question of what unifies the structure is SZ's too: "Läßt sich das Sein des Daseins einheitlich so herausheben, daß aus ihm die wesenhafte Gleichursprünglichkeit der aufgezeigten Strukturen verständlich wird?" (can the being of Dasein be brought out so unitarily that the essential equiprimordiality of the exhibited structures becomes understandable?, SZ 180–181, the opening of the chapter on care), and SZ answers with care as "Strukturganzheit" (SZ 209, 221) whose unity is temporality: the ecstases have a "ekstatische Einheit" (SZ 365). Henry's answer (immanence) and SZ's (ecstatic temporality) address the same question and contradict each other.

Henry says that the originary coherence of the act of objectification makes possible the coherence of the structure it engenders; the unity of the whole structure rests on the unity of transcendence itself; the results of the problematic of schematism must be rejected again, namely the claim to give the phenomenological unity of formation and reception as the sufficient ground of the unity of the whole structure; "*Avec l’unité de la spontanéité et de la réceptivité de la transcendance à l’égard de l’horizon, l’unité qui fonde la cohérence interne de l’acte de la transcendance considéré en lui-même n’a rien à voir*"; that unity is "*l’unité de l’essence et du milieu pur qu’elle imagine, non l’unité interne de l’essence elle-même*". The paragraph is a criticism of the Kantbuch's unity of spontaneity and receptivity, and independent of SZ.

Henry says that the expression "cohérence de la structure interne de l’essence de la manifestation" is equivocal: the internal structure includes only the essence's reality; the synthetic totality includes the imaginary product; "*La cohérence de cette structure d’ensemble n’est pas celle de l’essence dans sa structure interne, elle la présuppose*"; "*Le schématisme renverse les termes, donne pour l’unité de l’essence [...] l’unité de la structure d’ensemble que la transcendance développe*", so that the "« structure… unifiée de la transcendance »" (n. 112, K 176) is taken for the transcendence's inner unity, and "l’immanence comme telle [...] sombrent à la faveur de cette confusion dans l’oubli." The paragraph is a criticism of the Kantbuch and independent of SZ. The forgetting of immanence it describes, a forgetting of the inner unity in favor of the structure it develops, has the form of SZ's forgetting of the question of being in favor of beings (SZ 2, 35).

Henry says that the unity of spontaneity and receptivity, or of thought and intuition, cannot be the essential original unity of ontological knowledge, "« la possibilité interne de l’unité essentielle de la synthèse véritative pure »" (n. 113, K 99), as the schematism shows; the "unité « essentielle et originelle »" is found by defining thought and intuition as "représentations", "L’unité interne de la pensée est donc en elle celle de la représentation, *c’est, chaque fois, l’unité de la transcendance et de l’horizon*"; "*L’unité de l’intuition ne repose plus sur l’unité de la représentation : elle la fonde*"; but intuition is one only if transcendence is "une dans l’unité originairement cohérente de sa structure propre". The paragraph is the Kantbuch's, criticized, and independent of SZ.

Henry says that the concept of the unity of intuition refers by necessity to the reality; the internal unity of thought, of intuition and of the act of transcendence "ne sont en vérité qu’une seule et même unité, l’unité de la réalité"; thought and intuition are one by that unity and not by the mediation of the unity of representation, which presupposes it. The paragraph is Henry's own step and independent of SZ.

Henry says that representation opens and holds open the transcendental horizon, unifying it, and the systematic determination of unity requires three unities: "1° *l’unité de l’horizon*" (the being is reached "non pas « dans la dispersion et l’isolement »" (n. 114, K 141), unity belongs to the being only in so far as it manifests itself, "*Toute unité est par principe phénoménologique*" (n. 115, Henry's note on Husserl's time); imagination synthesizes the horizon, "« Toute synthèse est produite par l’imagination »" (n. 116, K 138); 2° "*l’unité de la représentation comme unité de l’essence et de ce qu’elle se représente*"; 3° "l’*unité interne de l’essence elle-même*" (n. 117). The classification of unities is Henry's and rests on the Kantbuch. SZ distinguishes unities of another kind (the unity of the referential whole of significance, §18, and the ecstatic unity, SZ 365), and the claim that all unity is phenomenological is the nearest to SZ's phenomenological method, in which the unity of the world is an uncovered structure and not a construction.

### § 36. The essential ontological significance of the concept of immanence: the immediate

Henry says that in its inner unity, as radically immanent, "réside la possibilité fondamentale de l’essence, la possibilité pour elle d’être elle-même. L’essence est l’action, elle est le devenir de l’extériorité, le pouvoir qui la produit"; what lets it act is "le pouvoir par lequel elle entre en possession de son propre pouvoir", its fundamental power; the answer: "*La structure ontologique où s’accomplit la réunion de l’essence avec soi est celle de la réceptivité originaire par laquelle l’essence se reçoit elle-même dans l’immanence.*" The paragraph is Henry's own and independent of SZ. Its image, a power that enters into possession of its own power, is the structure SZ describes as Dasein's "Seinkönnen" (§31; SZ 284), a potentiality-for-being that is its own and yet not given to itself by itself ("das sich selbst gehört und doch *nicht* als selbst sich zu eigen gegeben hat", SZ 284). Henry's formula resolves the paradox in the direction of self-possession; SZ's keeps the "doch nicht".

Henry says that the significance of immanence is lost when it is taken as the mere belonging of the essence to its determinations, modal inherence; "*La signification traditionnellement reconnue au concept de l’immanence de l’essence comme déterminant dans sa nature le rapport de l’essence et de ses modes recouvre l’impuissance de la pensée à saisir la signification essentielle*" of its reference to the internal structure of the essence. The paragraph criticizes the tradition's immanence (Spinoza, Hegel) and is independent of SZ.

Henry asks what can be the philosophical interpretation of the relation of essence to its modes without that determination; "Avec l’immanence de l’essence dans la conscience naturelle – quand ce n’est pas celle [...] de l’« Esprit » ou de la « Raison » dans les différents « individus » – la philosophie classique se satisfait à bon compte"; it names the possibility of knowledge: "la tautologie où la possibilité de la connaissance est simplement nommée." The paragraph is independent of SZ.

Henry asks whether the concept of immanence goes beyond tautology, since the idea behind it is that of the possibility of the ground; the immanence of the essence to experience's content, of real to natural knowledge, "atteste l'impossibilité pour la pensée d'oublier totalement ce qu'elle se cache sous un tel rapport, *la révélation immanente où l’essence se donne originellement à elle-même dans l’action par laquelle elle se rend présente*"; "L’obscurité qui est celle, chez Hegel aussi bien que chez Heidegger, du statut du savoir naturel et de l’immanence en lui du savoir réel, appartient en réalité au concept inélaboré de cette immanence"; "*L’essence immanente est celle de l’immanence.*" The Hegel-Heidegger obscurity is the essay's (GA 5 run, guard 1). The corresponding SZ structure, the pre-ontological understanding's immanence in Dasein's everyday existence (SZ 12–16), is not obscure in the way Henry says, since SZ gives its structure (understanding of being as a determination of Dasein's being, SZ 12–13, 212). Henry's description that immanence names the possibility of the essence "d'être là" returns to the vocabulary of presence (R §2 ¶6), the determination SZ 25–26 criticizes.

Henry asks whether the immanence of the essence to itself says simply that such a power exists, that the essence must be present to act, "la simple présupposition de celle-ci dans la tautologie où l’essence est reconnue et nommée sans plus, où on dit qu’elle « est »"; "Mais que signifie être ?"; the description of the fundamental possibility of the essence is "la structure originaire de l’être lui-même"; "l’être n’est pas quelque chose de mort ou de tout fait"; "*l’œuvre intérieure de l’être*". The question "Mais que signifie être ?" is SZ's opening question (SZ 1–4). Henry reaches it by a structure of self-reception, SZ by the analysis of Dasein's understanding of being; in Henry's answer being is the immanent work of the essence, and the answer is stated as "structure originaire de l'être" without the temporal sense SZ's question demands.

Henry says that the possibility of being's coming originally to itself determines it as "*l’immédiat*", not that of naive consciousness nor of the thought that takes the condition as simply given; "*L’immédiat est l’être lui-même comme originairement donné à lui-même dans l’immanence.*" The immediate is "le concept fondamental de l’ontologie", not a name for the tautology that being is. SZ's "unmittelbar" is used for the relation of intuition to its object and treated as derivative (SZ 358, quoting Kant), and being is for it not immediate but "verdeckt" (SZ 35). Henry's "l'immédiat" is the book's name for what SZ calls covered and requires to be made phenomenon, so that the unit states the book's reversal in one word.

Henry says that the determination of the immediate excludes the claim to grasp the condition of all presence in objectification as "*l’universelle médiation où tout ce qui est trouve son être*"; everything that is finds its being through the essence's mediation, which is the law; but a confusion arises between the being's necessary reference to the pure formal ontological element and the pure element's internal development by which it comes into its own reality; Schelling: "« Aucun opposé objectif n’arriverait jamais en elle s’il n’était posé en même temps par l’action par laquelle elle se pose elle-même »" (n. 118, IT 54); the self-positing is understood as objectification; "*La médiation désigne à la fois, sur le fond de l’identité de leur structure ontologique interne, l’action par laquelle l’essence rend l’étant manifeste et celle par laquelle elle se manifeste elle-même*"; "l’objectivation est la loi universelle de tout ce qui est et de l’être lui-même, l’universelle médiation." The paragraph is Schelling and Henry's diagnosis of the Kantbuch and the essay, and independent of SZ. In SZ the objectifying discovery of the present-at-hand is derived (SZ 366), so that "objectification as the universal law of all that is and of being itself" is no thesis of SZ's.

Henry says that what mediation lacks in claiming universality is its own possibility, since objectification is possible only if the essence "parvient originairement en soi comme dans cette objectivation même"; "*Et que ce parvenir originaire de l’essence en soi-même ne soit pas constitué par l’objectivation, qu’il ne puisse, comme le veulent Hegel et Heidegger, en être la conséquence, cela résulte justement de ce qu’il en est la condition*." The attribution to Heidegger of the view that the original coming-to-itself of the essence is a consequence of objectification belongs to the essay. SZ derives objectifying discovery from the world and the world from temporality (SZ 366), and so holds the reverse of what Henry ascribes to "Hegel et Heidegger": objectification is a consequence, not the origin. In this respect SZ is nearer to Henry's own claim than the Heidegger he criticizes.

Henry says that the original coming of the essence to itself, which determines it as the immediate, cannot be understood from an intellectual necessity, the logical requirement that the essence by which all presence is mediated be not mediated in turn: that is "une décision unilatérale de la pensée"; thought "« obéissant » à l’axiome selon lequel « il faut bien s’arrêter »" takes its desires for reality; "C’est dans celle-ci, au contraire, que réside l’immédiat, c’est en elle qu’il doit être exhibé, et cela comme la structure fondamentale qui la constitue." The methodological demand, that the ground be exhibited and not required, is SZ's (SZ 7–8: "aufweisende Grund-Freilegung" against deductive grounding). The paragraph applies it to the immediate.

Henry concludes: "La mise en évidence de l’immédiat comme constituant, non une simple exigence logique, mais la structure même de la réalité et son essence, est identiquement celle de la structure interne de l’immanence." The sentence closes Section II and sets the task of Section III, which is the exhibition of the immanence's internal structure.

The closing verdict for Section II follows. Henry's Section II takes from SZ little by name (nn. 62, 82, 90, 104) and relies on it throughout for a vocabulary and a problem. The vocabulary is the existentiell, existential and ontological tiers of §18, the "comment" of §§27 and 32, the ontic rooting of philosophy, and the circle; the problem is the question of how the act that opens the horizon is itself manifest, which is SZ's (SZ 12, 132, 284). Where Henry cites SZ the quotations are accurate (SZ 17, 55, 358) and the use is mostly sound in letter; the exceptions are n. 90, which supports with SZ 111 a thesis about man as only lit that SZ 133 contradicts, and n. 82, which cites time as the horizon of the understanding of being to deny that it is such as temporal. The argument runs through the essay (§§17–21), the Kantbuch (§§22–33) and Heidegger's other texts and French commentators, so that "Heidegger" and "la philosophie de l'être" in Section II do not name SZ. Four displacements are SZ-relevant. The subject of transcendence moves from Dasein to "the essence", so that the claim that "the transcendence is not its own work" (§27), which SZ 284–285 states of Dasein's thrown ground, becomes an argument against the philosophy of transcendence. The horizon of being is taken as pure succession and an object of intuition, though SZ calls this the vulgar concept of time (SZ 18, 24, 26) and derives intuition from understanding (SZ 147). Being-in-the-world is made a relation whose possibility is a worldless immanence (§§33–34), against SZ 13, 53 and 62. And the revelation that SZ places in attunement (SZ 136, 138, 284), Henry places in an immanent self-reception without world and without projection. Henry's strongest result is that a transcendence which does not ground its own manifestation requires another source of manifestation; it is a result SZ shares in its account of thrownness and the nullity of the ground (SZ 284–285), and it does not require the monism that Henry attributes to SZ. The verdict is local: Section II contests the Kantbuch and the late Heidegger more than SZ, and what it contests in SZ it does so by omission, never meeting SZ's own answers (self-disclosedness, attunement, thrownness, ecstatic temporality).


## Section III. The internal structure of immanence and the problem of its phenomenological determination: the invisible

Section III is where Henry cites SZ most (nn. 134–135, 137, 145, 148–158, 161–171, 175, 179, 183, 186, 201, 204 in the source's numbering; the notes of §§42–43 and §§49–51 carry them). The units below treat each paragraph against SZ's text at the loci it relies on, silently or by name, and refer to the GA 5 and GA 3 runs for Henry's quotations of the *Holzwege* essay and the Kantbuch.

### § 37. The internal structure of immanence

Henry says that immanence was defined by reference to transcendence and by the exclusion of the latter from its structure; before pushing the analysis of the structural determinations further, one must note what already results "d'une manière purement négative" from the exclusion. The paragraph introduces the section and is independent of SZ.

Henry says that "Là où il n’y a pas de transcendance, il n’y a ni horizon ni monde"; the horizon of the world is excluded from the essence considered in itself, and with it all intraworldly reality; the thought that would reach the essential follows a strange path, "non « se diriger vers » mais au contraire « se détourner de »", abandoning "la nature même du séjour qui était le sien auprès des choses", so that to the loss of all that is "s’ajoute [...] celle, plus essentielle, de l’être lui-même", and with it "sa propre possibilité, la possibilité de penser"; its "vraie détresse". The loss of the world as the condition of the path is the contrary of SZ's method, which begins in average everydayness (SZ 16–17). The nearest SZ phenomenon is anxiety, in which "alles Zuhandene und Vorhandene" says nothing to Dasein and the world as such is disclosed (SZ 186–188). Henry's thought turns away from the world and loses being; SZ's anxiety turns Dasein toward its being-in-the-world as such: "Die Angst vereinzelt und erschließt so das Dasein als »solus ipse«. Dieser existenziale »Solipsismus« versetzt aber so wenig ein isoliertes Subjektding in die harmlose Leere eines weltlosen Vorkommens, daß er das Dasein gerade in einem extremen Sinne vor seine Welt als Welt bringt" (anxiety individuates and discloses Dasein as "solus ipse"; this existential "solipsism" so little places an isolated subject-thing in the harmless emptiness of a worldless occurrence that it brings Dasein precisely, in an extreme sense, before its world as world, SZ 188). Henry's detour from the world is the "worldless occurrence" that SZ excludes.

Henry says that this abandonment is the essence's, which finds there its possibility; the essence is deprived of exteriority and alterity: "*L’indigence de l’essence réside dans le fait qu’elle ne renferme rien d’autre*"; approach to the essence takes the form of "l’épargne", of rejection; essential poverty lets go of "les multiples configurations de l’être, la forme même de son séjour auprès de la terre, la Maison et l’Année", and of "la parole même où toute chose est contenue, le nom et l’appellation"; "Mais que reste-t-il alors ?" The renunciation of "séjour auprès de la terre" and of the word is not SZ's; the nearest SZ structures (Dasein's "Aufenthalt", "Rede" as an existential equiprimordial with attunement and understanding, SZ §34) are what Henry's thought relinquishes. The language of poverty and sparing is that of the Heidegger of the 1940s and of mystical theology (Eckhart, §39), and has no SZ source.

Henry says that what remains is "l’essence elle-même", to which nothing is taken: "*rien n’est retiré parce que l’essence ne renferme rien d’autre et que la suppression de l’altérité est seulement la suppression de l’élément étranger par rapport à l’essence, de ce qui la recouvre et la dissimule à nos yeux*." The idea that what covers and dissimulates the essence is the other (the world) is a use of SZ's term of covering (*Verdeckung*, SZ 35–36), with the roles reversed. For SZ the covering is by the tradition and by falling into the "world" (SZ 21, 222), and what it covers is being, which is uncovered through the beings, not by their suppression. Henry's retreat from the world as removing a veil is thus the opposite procedure to SZ's destruction, which loosens the tradition's covers while keeping the beings (SZ 22–23).

Henry says that the negative determination shows its positive sense: thought's withdrawal obeys "une prescription qui est celle de l’essence elle-même"; "*C’est dans l’essence qu’il n’y a rien de transcendant*"; "*dans l’essence* il n’y a rien d’extérieur, rien d’étranger." The paragraph is Henry's own and independent of SZ.

Henry says that the structural determinations resulting from the exclusion reappear with their concrete positivity: in the essence nothing departs; "l’essence [...] ne se divise pas, elle ne se sépare pas de soi, aucune distance ne s’institue entre elle et elle. Il n’y a dans l’essence ni opposition ni représentation"; the concept of intuition is therefore "foncièrement impropre" since it implies the opposition of intuiting and intuited; interest implies opposition: "« il n’y a d’intérêt que là où il y a opposition »" (n. 1, Hegel); so in the essence there is "ni intérêt ni quoi que ce soit de semblable"; "l’essence ne veut rien, ne se propose rien. Elle est sans projet et sans désir"; it is calm, rest: "C’est en elle que l’essence se repose." The denial of interest, project and desire is the negation of SZ's care: care is being "Sich-vorweg-schon-sein-in-(der-Welt-)als Sein-bei" (SZ 221), a structure of projection, and "Interesse" belongs to the everydayness of the "Man" (SZ 43: "Interessiertheit" as a trait of inauthentic existence). The essence of Henry is defined as the exclusion of care. The rejection of intuition for implying opposition is SZ's too in another form (SZ 147).

Henry says that rest is solitude: "L’essence repose dans la solitude [...] Pas plus que le repos, la solitude de l’essence n’est simple privation. [...] l’essence reste seule *avec soi*. La solitude a un contenu"; "*C’est là ce qui demeure dans la solitude de l’essence, la relation de l’essence avec soi comme constitutive de cette essence même et de sa solitude*." The word is SZ's in a different use: anxiety "vereinzelt" Dasein and discloses it as "solus ipse" (SZ 188), a solitude of the individual in its world, with a "Wovor" that is being-in-the-world as such. Henry's solitude of the essence is the absence of any other and the relation of the essence to itself. The two meet in the formal point that solitude has a content (Dasein is brought before itself, SZ 188), and differ in that the individuated Dasein is brought before its world.

Henry says that the solitude of the essence is its unity, which is not the external identity of a thing with itself but "l’œuvre intérieure par laquelle l’essence parvient en elle avec l’acte même par lequel elle se donne à elle-même"; its content is "la relation de l’essence avec soi, *relation telle qu’en elle l’essence jouit de soi, a l’expérience de soi, se révèle à elle-même dans ce qu’elle est, telle qu’elle est*. *Ce qui a l’expérience de soi, ce qui jouit de soi et n’est rien d’autre que cette pure jouissance de soi-même, que cette pure expérience de soi, c’est la vie.* La solitude est l’essence de la vie." Here "la vie" enters as the name of the essence's self-experience. SZ 46 reproached the philosophies of "Leben" that "»Leben« selbst nicht als eine Seinsart ontologisch zum Problem wird", and Henry's step is the one that makes life an ontological determination, as the Introduction announced (R §7 ¶13). Whether the step meets SZ 46's demand (that life be asked as a mode of being) is for the later sections to decide; the present paragraph defines life by self-enjoyment without asking what mode of being that is.

Henry says that solitude is not a moment of life, a choice or a moral imperative: "La solitude n’est pas une catégorie de la psychologie ou de l’éthique, mais une catégorie ontologique fondamentale"; only the essence relates to itself originally, and this revelation to itself is the Parousia: "La Parousie est l’essence de la vie. La Parousie qui est l’essence de la vie, c’est donc là le contenu de la solitude. La solitude est la solitude de l’absolu." "Parousie" is the essay's word, and for SZ the Greek name of being as presence (SZ 25), here made the essence of life. "Catégorie ontologique fondamentale" again reverses SZ's use of "Kategorie" (SZ 44–46). SZ treats solitude as an existential, Vereinzelung in anxiety (SZ 188), not a category; Henry's use of "catégorie" is the term's meaning in the Kantbuch.

Henry says that solitude is unity in the sense of the "unité qui réunit", "la réunion de l’essence avec soi", so that "il n’y a pas à proprement parler un problème de l’unité de l’essence", posed only within monism; the unity designates the relation of the form and the content "telle qu’elle permet justement leur identité"; "la manière dont l’essence se donne à elle-même, c’est donc là ce qui est désigné sous le concept de l’unité, *et c’est cela aussi l’essence*"; so "le contenu de la révélation est la révélation elle-même" and "la Parousie elle-même qui se rend présente à elle-même dans la Parousie". The thesis that unity is not a problem for the essence is opposed to SZ's, for which the unity of the structural whole of Dasein is the problem on which §39 turns (SZ 180–181), solved by temporality (SZ 326–331). Henry replaces the problem by the definition of the essence as self-unity. The identity of the content and the revelation repeats the thesis of §7 ¶8 and §32.

Henry says that, since the content of the revelation is the revelation itself, the essence gives itself in the totality of its reality, in the unity; "*L’élément irréel est par principe exclu de l’essence*"; "*L’unité désigne le mode originaire de présence de l’essence à elle-même*", which is habitually named "proximité"; but proximity so understood is "présence sur le fond d’une certaine distance" (n. 2, §10), "*la proximité de l’essence est par principe impossible*", an insurmountable remoteness; "*Quand il s’agit de la réalité il n’y a plus de finitude*." The criticism of proximity as mediated by distance is directed at his own Section I account (§§9–10), which has now been replaced; SZ's *Nähe*, an effect of de-severance (SZ 105), is not an insurmountable remoteness. The sentence that "il n'y a plus de finitude" for reality contradicts SZ 331 ("Die ursprüngliche Zeit ist endlich") at the same place where finitude is Dasein's. The paragraph thus takes up the distance-presence analysis of §§9–10 as a position of the monism and rejects it.

Henry says that to an essence that gives itself in the totality of its reality nothing is lacking, "elle ne se projette vers rien"; it needs not become what it is: "à elle on ne peut dire « deviens ce que tu es »"; what lacks "le pouvoir-être au sens précisément de pouvoir être autre chose" lacks a power conditioned by the "ne pas", so "Le « ne pas » qui détermine essentiellement le pouvoir-être dans sa possibilité et dans sa finitude trouve son fondement ontologique dans l’irréalité"; "*Mais cette condition qui trouve précisément son explicitation dans l’interprétation ontologique de l’essence comme Projet et Souci, comme pouvoir-être, se trouve radicalement exclue au contraire de ce qui constitue à proprement parler la structure interne de celle-ci*"; "Dans le non-pouvoir l’essence trouve son pouvoir suprême, son impuissance est celle de la plénitude" (n. 3). The target is named. "Projet et Souci, [...] pouvoir-être" are SZ's: *Entwurf*, *Sorge*, *Seinkönnen*. The thesis that their "ne pas" has its ground in irreality is Henry's account of SZ's *Nichtigkeit*: "Grundseiend *ist* es selbst eine Nichtigkeit seiner selbst" (SZ 285), the nullity belonging to the existential sense of thrownness. For SZ the "Nicht" is not irreality: "Nichtigkeit bedeutet keineswegs Nichtvorhandensein, Nichtbestehen, sondern meint ein Nicht, das dieses *Sein* des Daseins, seine Geworfenheit, konstituiert" (SZ 285), and the potentiality-for-being is the projecting of possibilities into which Dasein is thrown (SZ 284). Henry's decision that the essence has no "pouvoir-être" (no possibility, no projection) states his thesis against SZ's definition of Dasein's being as care, and the argument that the "ne pas" has its ground in the irreal is an interpretation of SZ's *Nichtigkeit* that §42 develops with SZ 284–285 in hand. The unit is a Contestation with SZ named, and the reading of the "Nicht" is tested in §42. Henry's n. 3 to this paragraph cites SZ directly and concedes the point at which SZ is strongest: the Not-yet arising from self-anticipation, he quotes, is "no more than the structure of care in general an argument against the possibility and existence of a state of totality" (SZ 259: "Das aus dem Sich-vorweg entnommene Phänomen des Noch-nicht ist so wenig wie die Sorgestruktur überhaupt eine Instanz gegen ein mögliches existentes Ganzsein"; the quotation stops before SZ's clause that this *Sich-vorweg* makes being-toward-the-end possible in the first place), and that anticipation reveals "all the possibilities lying this side of" the last one (SZ 264, rendered from "die faktischen Möglichkeiten, die der unüberholbaren vorgelagert sind"). The note then sets SZ's totality on the plane of irreality: the totality disclosed by being-toward-death is a *comprise* totality, the horizon of transcendence, and finitude is "identical" with the ontological meaning of being-toward-death, whereas the totality of the essence's reality is "not even taken into consideration" by SZ. That is a Selection that is also a fair description of SZ's aim, which is the wholeness of an existing Dasein (SZ 259), not of a pure self-giving; it presupposes what the paragraph argues, that totality of reality and totality of understanding are different, and SZ has no term for the first.

Henry says that the plenitude of the essence, separated from all determinations of the world and lacking nothing, is what the young Hegel took as the content of religious consciousness, in the symbol of water, "aucune lacune, aucune limitation, aucune diversité ou détermination" (n. 4), whose feeling is "le moins dispersé, le plus simple" and which baptism realizes: "« en celui qui est immergé, il n’y a qu’un sentiment et l’oubli du monde, une solitude qui a tout rejeté de soi »" (n. 5); "*L’expérience de l’être dans sa simplicité et dans sa totalité se réalise autrement, toutefois, que dans la représentation de la conscience religieuse*", she is an ontologically universal structure; "le sentiment simple de sa plénitude." The paragraph is Hegel's youthful writings, and independent of SZ. It introduces "le sentiment" as the name of the essence's self-experience (preparing Section IV), a term for which SZ's *Befindlichkeit* is the existential counterpart, as Henry does not note.

Henry says that, in the essence, there is nothing but being itself, "rien de plus et rien de moins"; the absence of a beyond is not a limitation but "le caractère adéquat de la phénoménalité elle-même"; "l’absence de toute finitude"; the complete coincidence of reality and appearance, in which being finds "son privilège", Kafka: « chance, que le sol sur lequel tu te tiens ne peut être plus large que les deux pieds qui le couvrent » (n. 6), and the "flèches exactement ajustées aux plaies qu’elles ont faites" (n. 7); the truth is also tragic: "l’instauration d’un monde absolu duquel rien ne peut être soustrait, auquel rien ne peut être ajouté"; "Privilège ou [...] déchirement d’une blessure, joie ou souffrance, ou bien encore [...] l’un et l’autre en même temps". The coincidence of reality and appearance in the phenomenon is SZ 36's principle (nothing stands behind the phenomena). The tone and the Kafka images are Henry's, and the anticipation of "joie ou souffrance" the preparation for §53.

Henry says that the adequate experience of being expresses an impossibility inscribed in the essence: that of non-coincidence of being with itself; the power that is lacking is "le pouvoir de s’en aller hors de soi, de poser autre chose que sa propre réalité et de lui échapper, c’est-à-dire encore la « liberté »" (n. 8, Fichte); "*La structure sur laquelle repose l’impossibilité pour l’être de ne pas être tout entier présent à lui-même, [...] est la non-liberté.*" The assertion that freedom is the power of leaving oneself and positing something other is the Kantbuch's and GA 9's (*Überstieg*) account of freedom. SZ's freedom is different: "Freiheit" is Dasein's being-free for its ownmost potentiality (SZ 188, 191) and is limited by thrownness (SZ 366); it does not consist in positing something other than oneself. The negative statement that being is "non-libre" has an SZ trace in the thrown ground, which Dasein cannot become master of (SZ 284), but SZ calls that the burden of a free being, not non-freedom.

Henry says that "non-liberté" must be specified, since essence and laws imply prescription and impossibility; freedom itself is determined by a structure with which it merges: the product of freedom "revêt la forme d’un horizon", necessarily; "*La nécessité qui détermine la structure interne de l’être ne se ramène nullement [...] à une simple nécessité d’ordre éidétique, à celle par exemple qui appartient à l’eidos de la liberté, c’est la négation ou l’absence de celle-ci qu’elle exprime*"; it is "nécessité d’une structure déterminée, [...] déterminée [...] par l’absence en elle de toute liberté". The paragraph is Henry's own and independent of SZ.

Henry says that freedom has an ontological meaning: "Être libre, pour un pouvoir ontologique pur, [...] c’est être capable de poser autre chose que sa propre réalité"; "Le pouvoir de poser autre chose que soi est, comme tel, créateur"; what the freedom creates is the milieu of alterity, "le milieu de l’irréalité et de l’idéalité", not the being; its power is limited because the being remains foreign to the light; "la finitude ne limite pas à proprement parler le pouvoir de la liberté mais le qualifie proprement"; the freedom is the ontological power itself. The doctrine of freedom as creative of a horizon is the Kantbuch's and GA 9's (freedom as ground), and has no SZ counterpart.

Henry asks whether the freedom that creates the horizon should not be grasped in itself: considered in itself the power appears independent of the act that creates the horizon, "*Libre, celle-ci l’est dans sa relation à l’horizon, relation qui constitue précisément la liberté elle-même, non dans sa relation à soi, relation qui est comme telle, comme radicalement indépendante à l’égard de la liberté, l’essence de la non-liberté*"; so such a power "n’a plus à l’égard de lui-même et de sa propre réalité aucun pouvoir, aucune possibilité de se poser lui-même, de susciter son être ou de l’assumer"; with it ceases "toute maîtrise, toute faculté d’agir ou d’opérer, tout ce qui se donne habituellement comme le fondement d’une responsabilité ou d’une imputabilité, [...] toute possibilité d’assumer et de prendre attitude". This is the structure of SZ's thrown ground: the self "kann dessen *nie* mächtig werden" (SZ 284), "ist nicht insofern selbst der Grund seines Seins, als dieser aus eigenem Entwurf erst entspringt" (SZ 285). Henry's wording, no power to posit oneself or to produce one's being, restates it. The difference is stated in the same SZ sentence: "und hat doch existierend das Grundsein zu übernehmen" (and yet has, existing, to take over being ground, SZ 284). SZ's thrown ground is something Dasein must assume (*übernehmen*); Henry's inclusion of "assumer" and "responsabilité" among what ceases at the origin removes the half of SZ's formula that makes Dasein existentially guilty (SZ 285). In SZ Dasein's lack of mastery over its ground is the ground of its responsibility (guilt) and not its absence.

Henry says that what has no power over itself is "en sa nature la plus intime essentiellement passif. *La passivité est la détermination ontologique structurelle de l’essence originaire de la révélation*"; the passivity is not the action of a foreign reality as in Descartes, "C’est la relation de l’être avec lui-même [...] qui se trouve décrite et subsumée sous le concept de passivité"; "*une passivité dans l’unité*"; "Être soi-même ce qu’on reçoit et comme tel essentiellement passif à l’égard de soi, telle est cependant l’essence de la vie [...], l’essence de l’esprit"; the young Hegel: "« A l’esprit, à la vie [...] ce qu’ils reçoivent n’est pas donné, ils le deviennent par eux-mêmes »" (n. 9), which Henry corrects: "ils le sont, et cela depuis toujours". The determination of the structure as passivity toward oneself is the nearest approach to SZ's thrownness, whose "Nicht" is that of a being delivered to itself "nicht von ihm selbst in sein Da gebracht" (SZ 284) and which is disclosed in the burden of mood (SZ 284). SZ calls it *Geworfenheit* and *Überlassenheit* (SZ 365: "Überlassenheit an es selbst") and does not call it passivity, because for SZ the delivered-over being is also projecting. Henry's point that passivity toward oneself is not passivity toward a foreign reality repeats SZ's insistence that thrownness is Dasein's own and not an event behind it (SZ 284). The unit's reading of Descartes (passion as action of a foreign being) is the tradition's, from SZ 94 (*Das Sein selbst affiziert uns nicht*).

Henry says that in the life and the spirit the passivity "n’a rien à voir avec celle qui caractérise la relation à l’être étranger"; that freedom's submission to being as other "constitue la liberté elle-même"; against it "ce dont la soumission à l’égard de soi ne signifie plus une possibilité mais [...] l’impossibilité de toute possibilité en général"; this is the theme of the problematic as seeking the origin: "*Car l’origine est précisément ce qui n’a à l’égard de soi aucun pouvoir, aucune possibilité de se vouloir ou de se comprendre, de se dépasser ou de dominer son être de quelque manière que ce soit et d’abord en l’assumant*"; the origin is the "ce à partir de quoi" of a power, never its "ce sur quoi": "*c’est à partir de l’impossibilité que s’accomplit toute possibilité*"; "L’absolu n’est pas tel parce qu’il se tient au-delà de tout pouvoir, c’est parce qu’il est l’absolu [...] qu’il se tient dans cet au-delà"; "La nature interne de l’absolu est la passivité"; being is "la révélation originaire immanente de soi dans l’unité". The structure is that of SZ's thrown ground in vocabulary of the origin: the "ce à partir de quoi" which can never be gone back on is SZ's "Existierend kommt es nie hinter seine Geworfenheit zurück" (SZ 284), and "Dieser ist immer nur Grund eines Seienden, dessen Sein das Grundsein zu übernehmen hat" (SZ 285). The formula "c'est à partir de l'impossibilité que s'accomplit toute possibilité" has an SZ counterpart in the thesis that the *Nichtigkeit* is the ground of the potentiality-for-being: "Das Dasein ist nicht insofern selbst der Grund seines Seins [...], wohl aber ist es als Selbstsein das *Sein* des Grundes" (SZ 285), and in the sentence "Der eigene geworfene Grund zu sein, ist das Seinkönnen, darum es der Sorge geht" (SZ 284). Henry's "absolu" and "au-delà de tout pouvoir" have no SZ counterpart. The reconstruction of the structure is thus faithful in its core, and the name (the absolute) and the exclusion of assumption are his.

Henry says that the structure of being as originary passivity and non-freedom "se découvre à toute compréhension qui s’approche de l’essentiel", at least as an experience: it is discovered to freedom "au point le plus extrême de son exercice, dans la volonté la plus extrême de son pouvoir", when it says yes not to itself but to necessity: Nietzsche's "amor fati" — "le défi s’invertir et se comprendre dans l’amour du destin, c’est-à-dire aussi bien dans l’amour de soi, car le destin n’est pas une suite extérieure d’événements [...] mais la structure interne de l’être comme originellement et fondamentalement constitué par l’essence ultime de la non-liberté." The paragraph is Henry's reading of Nietzsche. Its SZ counterpart is resoluteness and the repetition of thrownness: Dasein "übernimmt" its thrown being in resolute anticipation (SZ 284 on assuming the ground; Division Two's resoluteness and repetition, SZ 305–308, 385). Henry's reading of the "oui" as the freedom's discovery of its essence resembles SZ's account of resolute assumption of one's thrownness, and the next paragraph denies that the yes reveals the structure.

Henry says that the understanding of such an experience must beware of mistaking itself: the "oui", the acquiescence or refusal are "divers modes d’un pouvoir qui est celui de la liberté", what they address is "ce qui se trouve soumis à celle-ci et à son pouvoir"; "*Ainsi la passivité ontologique qui constitue la structure interne de l’être comme structure du rapport originaire de l’être à soi, est-elle confondue avec la passivité qui n’est qu’un mode de la liberté et du pouvoir de prendre attitude*", with the passivity of which Kierkegaard says that "« il faut toujours qu’il y ait en elle assez d’activité pour qu’elle puisse garder sa passivité »" (n. 10). The distinction between ontological passivity and the passivity of an attitude is the analogue of SZ's distinction between thrownness (the "Daß" that Dasein is) and the existentiell taking-over of it (SZ 284–285). Henry's remark applies to SZ's resoluteness as much as to Nietzsche: resolute assumption is a mode of existing, and the thrown structure it assumes is prior to it. SZ states this priority: "Die Geworfenheit aber liegt nicht hinter ihm als ein tatsächlich vorgefallenes [...] Ereignis, sondern das Dasein ist ständig – solange es ist – als Sorge sein »Daß«" (SZ 284). Henry's objection to Nietzsche therefore does not touch SZ, whose distinction is the same.

Henry says that the acquiescence to what constitutes being's innermost nature cannot be confused with it, nor can the structure be understood as what discovers itself to such acquiescence; "*La structure la plus intérieure de l’être est la structure interne de la révélation en tant qu’elle se révèle intérieurement elle-même : loin de la révéler, l’expérience de l’acquiescement la présuppose au contraire comme ce qui le révèle originairement à lui-même en tant qu’elle se révèle originairement à soi*." In SZ the revelation of thrownness is by mood, which precedes and conditions projection and resoluteness (SZ 134–136, 148, 284), so that resoluteness does not reveal it but presupposes it. The structure of the claim is SZ's. What SZ lacks is the claim that the revelation is the being's own self-revelation, which Henry will expand into the "sentiment" (Section IV).

Henry concludes that the innermost structure of being, being as originally given to itself in the fundamental passivity of non-freedom, "c’est-à-dire encore la structure interne de l’immanence, constitue précisément la structure de la révélation elle-même et, comme telle, l’essence du Logos, c’est là du moins ce qu’il faut comprendre." "Logos" here is the term of §§38–40 (Fichte, Eckhart). SZ's *logos* is the letting-see of apophantic discourse, "ein Sehenlassen" (SZ 32–33), which Henry's immanent self-revelation, without sight, will replace; the paragraph announces the replacement.


### § 38. The internal structure of immanence and the problem of its understanding as revelation: Fichte

Henry says that it is doubtful whether the history of philosophy ever understood immanence's internal structure, and that where it glimpsed it, it read it not as constitutive of revelation but as excluding it and so as designating the being (*l'étant*): immanence "se trouve rejetée hors du domaine propre de l'ontologie pour recevoir [...] la signification d'être une catégorie ontique." Immanence arose in opposition to transcendence, and since transcendence was taken as the power in which phenomenality, identified with exteriority, finds its ground, the concept excluded from it was excluded from phenomenality as well. The paragraph is Henry's own history and does not cite SZ. It depends on SZ in one respect: that the inner/outer scheme belongs to an ontology of the present-at-hand (SZ 60–62) is a thesis Henry shares, and his account of how "immanence" came to denote the being is a version of it. Where he differs is the remedy; SZ drops the scheme, Henry keeps immanence and re-founds it.

Henry says that the idea of subsistence in oneself returns when the absolute is thought, and that Fichte, in the tenth lecture of the *Anweisung zum seligen Leben*, understands the absolute no longer as emergence into alterity but as maintenance of itself in love: "il est directement l'absolu se supportant et se maintenant lui-même"; love is opposed to reflection, which by essence divides; blessedness is not to be sought "en quelque chose d'autre que dans ce qui ici déjà les entoure de si près qu'il ne peut être rapproché davantage"; love is "ramassé totalement en soi", having reality in its totality; reflection transforms the content of love into "une essence solidifiée". The paragraph is Fichte (nn. 11–14) and independent of SZ. The proximity that cannot be nearer is the counter-image of *Ent-fernung* (SZ 105), where nearness is brought about by a de-severing that presupposes distance; Henry's §37 ¶9 has already turned this into the insurmountable remoteness of proximity, and Fichte's nearer-than-near is the immanent term.

Henry says that with the opposition of love to reflection "se fait jour chez Fichte une nouvelle philosophie de l'existence" in which existence names what cannot be surpassed and so cannot return to posit or deduce itself; Fichte's: "toute possibilité lui est coupée de dépasser cette dernière et de se comprendre", the existence rests on itself "antérieurement à toute notion qu'elle a d'elle-même et insoluble pour cette notion". The contrast with SZ is the point of the unit. For SZ, "existence" is Dasein's way of being, determined by projecting beyond itself: Dasein "ist" its possibilities and "geht um sein Sein" (SZ 12, 42–43). Henry's "philosophie de l'existence" gives the word the inverse sense of the unsurpassable. SZ has a structure that matches Fichte's at one point: the *Faktum* that Dasein can never get behind its thrownness, "Existierend kommt es nie hinter seine Geworfenheit zurück" (SZ 284), and "Dasein [...] ist nicht von ihm selbst in sein Da gebracht" (SZ 284). Henry's quotation is closer to this than to anything in the Fichte of the *Wissenschaftslehre* to which SZ's concept of Entwurf is sometimes linked (not argued in SZ). Henry does not mention thrownness here; he builds the philosophy of existence against the transcendence-existence of the tradition (which, for him, SZ continues).

Henry says that Fichte recognized that the doctrine of existence changes when the existence is the absolute's, and revised his doctrine of the *Verbum*: "au début était le Verbe" no longer means the absolute in conscious form, outside itself, but love, which stays within itself; "il n'y a plus de différence entre l'être et l'existence, et cela précisément parce que l'existence a cessé d'être la différence" (n. 19), the difference between being and existence existing "que pour nous"; the first theory made "en Dieu" mean "hors de soi". The paragraph is Fichte. The "différence entre être et existence" is, for SZ, not a difference of two terms but the ontological difference at work in the question of being (SZ 6: "Das Sein des Seienden »ist« nicht selbst ein Seiendes"); Henry's reading of Fichte abolishes the difference by identifying existence with immanent reality and so gives *Dasein's* existence no place. SZ's distinction between *Existenz* (Dasein's being) and *Vorhandenheit* is a different distinction again, and Henry's "existence" here belongs to neither.

Henry says that, with the alteration, philosophy of existence must not forget its fundamental ontological meaning, "existence veut dire manifestation", and Fichte presupposes that the existence without difference is an experience: love gives blessedness, "qu'est-ce qui donne la certitude de Dieu si ce n'est l'amour ?", love is "source de toute certitude, de toute vérité", and the existence "ne saurait être sans se trouver" has "de son essence de se saisir elle-même" (third lecture); but this is presupposed, not founded: "La mise en lumière d'un mode originaire de révélation comme trouvant précisément sa structure dans l'immanence est seule susceptible de fournir le fondement ontologique", and because Fichte does not do the work, the traditional concept of phenomenality "reprend inévitablement son pouvoir" and existence "retombe [...] dans l'indétermination et dans la nuit". The structure of the criticism, that a thesis about disclosure needs an ontological ground in the phenomenon itself, is SZ's phenomenological demand (SZ 34–36: what shows itself in itself, and the "Wie" of its showing); Henry applies it to Fichte, as he applied it to the tradition. The claim that "existence" in the traditional sense "veut dire manifestation" is the thesis of his own Introduction, and conflicts with SZ's use, where manifestation (*Erschlossenheit*) is a feature of existence, not a synonym of it (SZ 133, 220–221).

Henry says that thought moves here in uncertainty: an existence that does not divide in exteriority and does not produce itself in representation is "sans image"; Fichte writes that at the lower levels of spiritual life the divine being does not reveal itself to consciousness and at the central point it is discovered "comme une *image* et une *reproduction*"; "Il est remarquable qu'au moment où elle se trouve comprise de la sorte par Fichte, son caractère phénoménologique devienne brusquement incertain [...] et finalement nié"; the image, which is manifestation of the absolute and determines "le point central de la vie spirituelle", is required for the absolute to be discovered. The paragraph is Fichte and independent of SZ.

Henry says that the absolute's imageless existence "est de tout temps entré dans la vie réelle des hommes, simplement sans être reconnu"; its manifestation belongs to representation; "la manifestation de l'existence immanente est contingente par rapport à celle-ci, elle s'ajoute à elle d'une manière synthétique, comme la représentation, et cela précisément parce que la structure interne de l'immanence n'a pas été reconnue et comprise comme la structure même de la révélation"; the divine existence is hidden "à notre regard" and "ne se manifeste que sous forme de savoir". The contingency of manifestation in relation to being is the claim that SZ's identification of the phenomenon with being's showing (SZ 28–31, 35–36) rules out for beings that show themselves; Henry's complaint about Fichte is that he has a being that is not a phenomenon in its own right. SZ would add a sentence Henry omits: that being "is" only in the understanding of being (SZ 212), so that nothing like an unmanifest absolute can "be".

Henry asks whether the absolute is hidden only "pour nous", so that a knowing that is only ours must be added extrinsically as a consequence of our limitation; the opposition of knowing to the absolute is then not internal to it but an external addition of an "existence de seconde main"; because there is "pas de séparation entre l'absolu ou Dieu et le savoir dans sa racine la plus profonde" (n. 27), the difference is real "que pour nous, comme conséquence de notre limitation" (n. 28); the absolute "se dérobe" to a light not consubstantial with it; "L'œil de l'homme lui cache Dieu" (n. 29). The limitation of knowing by human finitude is the figure SZ refuses when it places finitude in the constitution of Dasein itself and not as a deficit of an infinite knower: "Die ursprüngliche Zeit ist endlich" (SZ 331) and the *intellectus infinitus* is a limit-concept that would presuppose the possibility of the finite one (SZ 227–229 on the "eternal truths", which SZ calls a residue of Christian theology that has not been purged). Henry's line of argument in this and the next unit is that finitude is not human but belongs to the structure of the horizon, which agrees with SZ's refusal of finitude as a mere limit, and then diverges: finitude belongs for SZ to Dasein's being as such, for Henry to the horizon only.

Henry says that the human intervention, meant to keep room for an ontological interpretation of immanence as revelation, is philosophically suspect: "l'homme n'a par lui-même aucun pouvoir, pas même celui de rendre une connaissance inadéquate et « finie »"; every manifestation rests on a determinate ontological structure, whose eidetic analysis is the task; the relation of the two modes of manifestation, the one hiding and the one revealing the absolute, is an ontological question, and its comprehension "a montré qu'il n'y a pas de connaissance finie qui ne soit en son essence révélatrice de l'absolu"; "Si la transcendance repose dans l'immanence, celle-ci est présente, de par son œuvre propre, partout où il existe un rapport, en l'homme"; to say otherwise is "une impossibilité éidétique et [...] une absurdité". Fichte: the absolute unity of human and divine existence is the deepest knowledge to which man can rise (n. 30); it holds whether man rises to it or not; the structure is "universelle et, par suite, indépassable", the basis of Fichte's Christology, of truth as accessible, of his horror of scepticism and relativism (nn. 31–32). Henry's insistence that "anthropological" considerations cannot explain manifestation corresponds to SZ's refusal to read Dasein as an anthropological subject (SZ 45–50); he differs in drawing the converse, that the manifestation of the absolute is the ontological ground of every finite knowing. SZ names the same kind of relation in the other direction: Dasein's disclosedness is the "light" of beings and not the effect of an absolute (SZ 133). The Christology is a Fichte paragraph and independent of SZ; Henry's closing questions (why does Fichte say we know nothing of this divine life, "au premier contact de la conscience elle se transforme déjà en un monde mort", n. 33?) have the answer that Fichte holds the same conception of manifestation as classical monism.

Henry says that the responsibility for the absolute's hiddenness is not man's: it lies in the absolute itself, in the fact that Fichte did not grasp the internal structure of immanence "comme originairement révélatrice de soi"; for Fichte as for others there is only one power of manifestation, monism's, to which immanent existence necessarily withdraws, "non pas en raison d'une déficience de ce pouvoir [...] précisément, elle ne déforme rien –, mais parce qu'elle est une connaissance de l'idéalité, non de la réalité". The absolute's phenomenological reality, without which all assertions about it are "pure hypothèse sur un être lui-même purement hypothétique", "n'est rien d'autre [...] que son appartenance originaire à soi dans l'immanence"; and so "la compréhension de la structure interne de l'immanence comme révélation ne saurait s'ajouter à la simple compréhension de cette structure, elle lui est identique." That the knowledge of the horizon "ne déforme rien" is also SZ's position on the everyday disclosedness of world, which is not deformation but covering-up in the mode of falling (SZ 167–170: *Gerede* and the closing-off that belongs to everyday disclosing); Henry's formulation puts the loss in the object (reality versus ideality), SZ's in the way of access (*Verdecktheit* as the mode of the disclosed). "Hypothèse" as the charge against a being without phenomenological reality matches SZ's phrase on being: "Sein ist nur im Verstehen" and "nur solange Dasein ist [...] ist Sein" (SZ 212), with the difference that for SZ the phenomenological reality in question is Dasein's.

Henry concludes that this understanding was missing in Fichte as it was later missing, "avec des conséquences infiniment plus graves", in all modern ontology, and had hardly ever been met in history "si ce n'est cependant chez un penseur d'exception qu'on appela autrefois, à juste titre, un maître : Eckhart." "Modern ontology" here includes SZ by Henry's own reckoning in §§19–36. The paragraph announces §39.

### § 39. Eckhart

Henry says that Eckhart does not aim at the ultimate ontological structures but at the edification of souls, and preaches the soul's union with God; but to relate to God is possible only by the work of the absolute itself, so that "la relation à l'absolu dépend de la nature de celui-ci et de sa structure interne ou plutôt leur est identique: l'union existentielle de l'homme avec Dieu n'est possible que sur le fond de leur unité ontologique." Eckhart's text: "l'âme ne fut rien d'autre que l'opération de Dieu" (n. 36). The *existentiell/existential* distinction in Henry's terms ("union existentielle", "unité ontologique") is SZ's (SZ 12–13). It is used here as SZ uses it, with the existentiell instance (preaching, the union) read as ontically indexed to an existential-ontological structure. The inference from the existentiell to the ontological is SZ's method (SZ 13: the existentiell understanding is the root of the analytic), applied to a medieval mystic who has no analytic. Henry asserts the transposition and does not argue it.

Henry says that the identity of the soul's essence with God's operation must be understood radically: the operation does not add to God's being as would a creation but "s'identifie [...] avec son propre fond", is "égale à lui-même" and "continûment opérante"; the soul's essence is God's essence ("elle est elle-même le Royaume de Dieu", n. 37; "le Fond de Dieu et le Fond de l'âme (n'est) qu'un seul et même Fond", n. 38). The paragraph is Eckhart. SZ has no counterpart; its "Grund" is the thrown ground of Dasein (SZ 284–285), the opposite of a ground that Dasein and God share.

Henry says that, because the ground of the soul is God, it was not created: "Je suis non-né" (n. 39), the creaturely condition is rejected for man "en tant qu'il doit être compris dans son essence"; independence from creation "signifie son identité avec lui"; the dependence of God on the soul, "La nature de Dieu, son Essence, sa Déité dépendent de l'opération nécessaire qu'il effectue dans l'âme" (n. 40); "Si je n'étais pas, Dieu ne serait pas non plus" (n. 41); "que Dieu et moi sommes un" (n. 42). The paragraph is Eckhart. A formal parallel in SZ is the dependence of being on Dasein: "Sein [...] ist nur im Verstehen", Dasein not "creates" being but being "is" only so long as Dasein is (SZ 212), a thesis Heidegger glosses at once as not idealistic. Henry does not make the connection; he will make a different one, that Eckhart's thesis is a thesis about the structure of revelation and not of cause.

Henry asks whether the identity can be the content of Eckhart's thought if the union is reached only at the end of a progress under contingent conditions (love, poverty, humility); of love, Eckhart says "l'amour ne transporte jamais en Dieu; il ne fait que consolider ce qui est déjà uni" and "l'amour unit dans une opération, non dans une essence" (n. 43), so the ontological unity "se trouve au contraire présupposée par lui"; but love also names God's essence itself, and then God's love for the soul and the soul's love for God are one love: "Dieu s'aime lui-même [...] Mais dans l'amour dont Dieu s'aime lui-même, Il aime aussi toutes les créatures, non en tant que créatures, mais en tant qu'elles sont Dieu" (n. 44). The unit reproduces, in Eckhart's terms, the SZ-type question of what is an existentiell condition of an existential structure: love as an operation presupposes the essence. Henry resolves it the way SZ resolves the relation of *Befindlichkeit* and the moods (SZ 134–137): the existentiell mode is a factical realization of a structure it presupposes.

Henry asks whether poverty and humility are not "des modes déterminés de l'existence, déterminés et en même temps contingents" and conditions of a union that becomes problematic; they require man to renounce world and self until he is nothing, for "c'est seulement s'il n'est plus rien qu'il y a place en lui pour l'opération de Dieu"; this binds the mystic to a duty ("L'homme *doit* être vide", n. 45), a "mystique" "suspendue [...] à une éthique"; even if man is stripped of all things and a place remains where God could operate, he is not yet poor (n. 46); only if God "devrait être lui-même le lieu de son opération" (n. 47); "L'homme humble et Dieu ne font qu'un" (n. 48); "l'homme humble [...] peut commander à Dieu" (n. 49). The ethical reading, in which the union is conditioned by a decision to renounce, resembles SZ's structure of authentic existence as a modification of the Man-self (SZ 130, 267: the authentic Self is "eine existenzielle Modifikation des Man als eines wesenhaften Existenzials"), and Henry's immediate step is to refuse it as the meaning of the poverty. The refusal does not use SZ.

Henry answers by a reversal: humility and poverty may carry an ontological meaning, "appartenant au mouvement d'une pensée qui vise l'essence"; unity cannot designate merely the union of man and God (that the essence of man is in God says nothing about what essence is, being "l'identité extérieure de l'essence avec soi dans la tautologie"); Eckhart's problematic, which presents man's union with God, "se ramène en réalité à la détermination de celui-ci"; humility and poverty "agissent à l'égard de celle-ci comme une destruction"; "parce qu'elles accomplissent le retrait de tout ce qui n'est pas l'essence, humilité et pauvreté mettent à nu la structure de celle-ci"; the exposure is not only liberation but penetration: "elles déterminent sa structure interne." Henry here borrows SZ's term in its destructive sense. *Destruktion* in SZ is the loosening of the tradition's concealing contents so as to win back the original experiences of being (SZ 22); Henry's *destruction* removes whatever is not the essence to expose it (the same operation he described at §37 ¶3 as removal of the covering). SZ's destruction is historical and positive-critical (SZ 23: it "will nicht die Vergangenheit in Nichtigkeit begraben"), Henry's is the subtraction of the other from the same, and he applies the term to Eckhart and, by implication, to his own method (§§1–5). SZ's own word for the work of renunciation, *Entschlossenheit*, is not considered.

Henry says that the determination of the internal structure of essence by unity was understood with a mode of originary receptivity whose content is the essence's reality, which "ne renferme rien d'autre" ("Dieu est immanent à cette pure essentialité de lui-même qui ne renferme rigoureusement plus rien d'autre", n. 50); so Eckhart thinks the absolute "à partir de l'exclusion hors d'elle de l'altérité", that is, of the world and "image" ("Si tu veux trouver la nature sans voile, il faut briser toutes les images", n. 51); the rejection of images is that of the pure milieu of exteriority; "Dieu est en nous, mais nous sommes hors de nous" (n. 52); the reason "pénètre à l'intérieur; rien d'extérieur ne lui suffit, ni bonté, ni sagesse, ni vérité" (n. 53); the rejection reaches forms and attributes, "le rejet de toute création", and the leaving of "tout le créé pour atteindre à ce Fond qui est Abîme" (n. 55), the "quelque chose d'incréé qui échappe aux mesures et à la forme" (n. 57). The paragraph restates §37's picture in Eckhart's idiom. The point where SZ bears on it is Henry's gloss on "images": images are what appears in the world, in representation. SZ's phenomenon is not an image but what shows itself (SZ 28–31); the image-and-representation language is the Kantbuch's *Bild* (GA 3), and Henry's Eckhart is thus read through the same transcendence-as-image-forming that he took from the Kantbuch in §24.

Henry says that Eckhart thinks the approach of the essential as withdrawal: the sculptor "n'ajoute pas au bois, il lui enlève" (n. 58), the parable of the treasure in the field (n. 59); the existential meaning of "renoncement au monde" is the ontological meaning of rejecting the other, so that poverty and humility "appartiennent" to an eidetic analysis; "ici, dans cette pauvreté, l'homme retrouve l'être éternel qu'il a été, qu'il est actuellement et qu'il demeurera éternellement" (n. 60), "je suis ce que j'étais" (n. 61); they are not a preliminary condition foreign to the essence but identical with it: "la vertu qui a nom humilité est enracinée au Fond de la Déité" (n. 62). The point is that the conditions of manifestation belong to the essence. This is the reverse of SZ's relation between the existentiell and the existential, where the existentiell is the factical realization and does not constitute the structure. Henry's claim that the condition is identical with the structure it discloses is the thesis he will make about affectivity in Section IV, and it is here defended through Eckhart's text without SZ.

Henry says that the question whether the rejection of the other is the structure itself is answered by recalling that "c'est *dans l'essence* qu'il n'y a rien d'autre"; Eckhart: "aucune différence n'existe [...] *dans* la nature de Dieu" (n. 63), "rien d'étranger *dans* l'unité" (n. 64), "l'essence de l'être primitif dans son unité simple, où il n'y a de différence d'aucune sorte" (n. 65); God begets man "sans aucune distinction" (n. 66), "tout ce que Dieu opère est unité"; the exclusion of the creature is thus the strict corollary of the identity: "là où finit la créature, là commence l'Être de Dieu" (n. 68); "lorsque l'homme se trouvait encore *dans l'éternelle façon de Dieu*, rien d'autre ne vivait en lui" (n. 69). The condemners of Eckhart lacked, Henry says, "la compréhension de sa pensée." The paragraph is Eckhart; the ontological difference reading of "là où finit la créature, là commence l'Être de Dieu" is Henry's, who identifies the creature with the being (*étant*) and God with being, a reading to which SZ 6 offers the formal analogue (being is not a being) and from which SZ 94 ("Das Sein selbst affiziert uns nicht") keeps its distance by denying that being is a second thing.

Henry says that the unity as essence's internal structure rests on the exclusion of the ontological process underlying distinction, which Eckhart thinks as creation, "activité", "opération" or "médiation"; "Dieu est l'unité dans cette union naturelle *antérieure à toute naissance*" (n. 70); "naissance" is the entry into the world, linked to external images, and its antithetical concept is "virginité" and "pureté", which, like humility and poverty, designate the structure of the essence: "La personne par qui Jésus fut reçu ne pouvait qu'être vierge, [...] *libre de toutes images étrangères, aussi disponible qu'avant sa naissance*" (n. 71); to realize these conditions "c'est donc rejeter le processus ontologique par lequel en général l'étant se phénoménalise, et cela comme une « image »", which monism calls "médiation"; "Car toute médiation est étrangère à Dieu" (n. 72). The "naissance" as entry into the world and "disponible qu'avant sa naissance" refer to what SZ calls *Geworfenheit*: Dasein's "Daß" of being delivered to a Da (SZ 135, 284). Henry's reading puts Eckhart's pre-birth state on the side of the essence (the state in which the soul has no world), while SZ's thrownness is the fact of the world's already being there. The two take the same word, "birth" (SZ's "Geburt" in the sense of the stretch between birth and death, SZ 373, is another matter), in opposite directions: for Henry the entry into the world is what the essence excludes; for SZ it is what Dasein cannot get behind.

Henry says that this mediation, the event in which the being finds its being, is named by Eckhart as activity and operation, and it is against it that he thinks the absolute; the determination, "s'accomplissant à partir du concept de la transcendance et par l'exclusion de celle-ci", brings the distinction between God and Deity: "La Déité n'opère pas, [...] il n'y a point d'opération en elle" (n. 73), "il n'y a plus absolument aucune activité" (n. 74), "l'Essentialité divine où Dieu s'abstient de toute activité" (n. 75); "Dieu opère" (n. 76); "Dieu et la Déité diffèrent comme l'opération et la Non-opération" (n. 77); "avant qu'il y eût des créatures, Dieu n'était pas encore Dieu" (n. 78); "Ce n'est que lorsque l'âme devint créature qu'elle eut un Dieu" (n. 79), "tout ce qui est dans la Déité est Unité" (n. 80). The transposition of God into transcendence and Deity into immanence is Henry's; Eckhart's text is arranged to fit it. SZ is relevant only by contrast: its being is not distinguished into an operating and a non-operating pole, and its nearest structure, the "nothing" in which anxiety discloses the world (SZ 186–187), is not an abyss of inaction but the "nowhere" of the world as such.

Henry says that "tout ce qui est en Dieu est Dieu" (n. 81), that nothing other is in the absolute, gives the essence a content, which explains why Eckhart calls the structure "solitude" and "désert" ("dans le tréfonds de la nature divine et de sa solitude", n. 82; "Dieu dans son unité et dans sa solitude [...] dans son désert et dans son propre fond", n. 83); the exclusion of the forms of being has as correlate the presence in the essence of their ground, the essence's own presence to itself; poverty, purity and virginity thus "ont un contenu", the Deity itself, and are "l'expérience de Dieu". The solitude linked to the essence's self-relation (§37 ¶8) is here assigned to Eckhart as its first holder. The SZ term for solitude is *Vereinzelung* (SZ 188), which individuates a Dasein before its world; Eckhart's "désert" has no world. That Henry makes solitude an ontological category (§37 ¶9) is the point at which he leaves SZ's existential.

Henry says that the possibility for the Deity to constitute its own content is a problem whose ground is unity in the sense not of identity of form and content but of "ce qui rend possible une telle identité", the "pouvoir fondamental de parvenir en soi-même, de se recevoir elle-même et de se réunir ainsi avec soi", which Eckhart thinks as a "work" ("Celui qui veut saisir en son entier *l'œuvre intérieure* [...] se confie à l'unité qui est libre de toute diversité", n. 84; "*à l'aide de l'Égalité*", n. 85; "elle l'accomplit sans cesse, nuit et jour", n. 86); the essence is not the "unité morte de l'étant" but the absolute's "pouvoir originaire et fondamental de parvenir en soi"; the "pureté" in which "Dieu, le Père éternel, puise la plénitude et le Fond sans fond de sa totale Déité" is the "Unité absolue tout en jaillissant en lui-même" (n. 87). This is the same step as in §37 ¶8: unity is power of self-reaching. The "pouvoir de parvenir en soi" has a formal analogue in SZ's *Zeitigung*, in which temporality "zeitigt sich" (SZ 328–329), a self-temporalizing that is not an operation on something else; Henry's "parvenir en soi" and SZ's "sich zeitigen" both have the structure of an event that is its own condition, and differ in that SZ's event is ecstatic.

Henry says that because the absolute "demeure en lui-même", it is immanence, and the essence is rest in the positive sense of reposing "en elle": "Unité reposant en elle-même, [...] ne recevant rien du dehors" (n. 88), the "Fond absolument simple" (n. 89), "Lit", "Ruisseau", "Source" (n. 90), "Source la plus profonde" (n. 91); and the soul is identical with God as "Il est celui qui opère en lui-même" (n. 92), "Dieu et moi sommes un dans l'opération" (n. 93). The paragraph is Eckhart.

Henry says that, operating and resting in itself, the essence "ne veut rien" (n. 94), is naked "sans aucun besoin" (n. 95); this is the "calme" (n. 96) that grows in the soul "coopérant avec Dieu, c'est-à-dire renonçant à toute activité" (n. 97) and brings the essential poverty; what lacks is "l'horizon de l'irréalité et la possibilité de se rapporter en lui à quelque chose d'irréel, la possibilité d'un manque en général"; "Ce Fond secret n'a ni passé ni futur, il n'attend rien qui puisse s'ajouter à lui, car il ne peut ni gagner ni perdre" (n. 98). The absence of past and future in the ground is the negation of SZ's ecstases (SZ 328–329: the future, having-been and present as the unity of care) and of the "Sich-vorweg" of care (SZ 192, 259). Henry reads it as the absence of the horizon of irreality, as in §37 ¶10. SZ would deny that a ground without temporality can be a ground of anything that is (SZ 17–19: being is to be understood from time).

Henry says that reality cannot be determined negatively by the exclusion of the finite power of the horizon; it is the act by which its own content is given in totality ("Dieu ne peut donner peu"; he gives "tout à la fois", "don simple, parfait, sans division, sans relation avec le temps"), and Eckhart says "de cela je suis aussi certain que de vivre" (n. 99), so the gift that is the whole of reality is "l'essence de la vie"; the adequacy of the experience of being, "ce qui remplit touche à tous les bouts et ne fait défaut nulle part" (n. 100), explains "plénitude" and "douceur" (n. 101): "La joie du Seigneur, c'est le Seigneur lui-même" (n. 102). The "sans relation avec le temps" is the clause that sets this against SZ: the giving that has no relation to time is the contrary of Dasein's being as temporal (SZ 17–19). Eckhart's certainty "aussi certain que de vivre" is, again, a different certainty from SZ's, which belongs to the anticipation of death (SZ 265); Henry's certainty of life is certainty of an immanent self-experience.

Henry says that the determination of the experience of being, its plenitude and sweetness, from the ground as unity lacks the explicitation of a presupposition in which lies Eckhart's essential contribution: "l'unité précisément se trouve comme telle déterminer une expérience, l'expérience de soi de l'être dans la jouissance de soi", the presupposition "conformément à laquelle la structure interne de l'immanence est celle de la révélation". The paragraph announces §40. "Jouissance de soi" is the language of §37 ¶8; SZ's counterpart is mood as the way in which Dasein "is" its "Da" and finds itself (SZ 134–135), a finding that, for SZ, is not enjoyment.

### § 40. The fundamental ontological presupposition of Eckhart's thought and the original essence of the logos

Henry says that the understanding of the immanent structure as revelation is a constant presupposition of Eckhart's thought and its most explicit affirmation: the essence that is its own content is interpreted not as the dead being but as Reason, a power of intelligibility whose content it constitutes; "le Seigneur, c'est la Raison vivante, essentielle et existante, qui se comprend elle-même, qui n'est et ne vit absolument qu'en elle-même" (n. 103); the Reason does not reveal itself "conformément au mode de manifestation qui a été pensé dans ces recherches sous le titre de « compréhension »" but according to unity; "Une intelligence une est si pure en elle-même [...] qu'elle comprend sans intermédiaire l'Être divin dans sa Pureté et sa Nudité" (n. 104); and against intuition: "Tant que quelque chose est encore l'objet de notre intuition, nous ne sommes pas encore un dans l'Un [...] là où il n'y a que l'Un, on ne voit que l'Un" (n. 105). The "compréhension" is Henry's name (earlier, §§19–24) for the ontological power of transcendence. It is not SZ's *Verstehen* as such, which is a mode of disclosedness equiprimordial with attunement and projecting (SZ 142–148), and which Henry reads, in this book, as the Kantbuch's pure horizon-forming. Eckhart's denial of mediation ("sans intermédiaire") and of intuition ("l'objet de notre intuition") is, again, a denial of the *Anschauung* that SZ itself called "derivative" of understanding (SZ 147: "Anschauung und Denken sind [...] entfernte Derivate des Verstehens"). SZ and Henry thus agree on the derivative status of intuition and disagree over what is prior to it.

Henry says that what constitutes its own content on the basis of unity was understood as life: since unity grounds a revelation, life is "en elle-même révélation"; "Connaître Dieu seul, voilà la vie éternelle" (n. 106); "personne ne sait mieux ce qu'est la vie éternelle que la vie éternelle elle-même" (n. 107); a knowledge of an overabundant being is overabundant; this is not a particular modality of existence but "une structure ontologique", the "première Image où toutes choses sont unité", where resides the "repos" (n. 108); the soul may "goûter Dieu avant qu'il devienne d'aucune façon Vérité ou cognoscibilité"; "*C'est là qu'elle connaît de la manière la plus pure, qu'elle assume l'être dans sa parfaite Égalité*" (n. 109). "Assume" is the verb for SZ's *übernehmen* (SZ 284), here used by Henry's Eckhart in a pre-world sense; the coincidence is lexical only. The unit's claim, that life is revelation in itself and that the tradition's truth and cognoscibility come after it, corresponds in structure to SZ's priority of disclosedness over truth as correctness: "Sein und Wahrheit gleichursprünglich" (SZ 230), Dasein "in der Wahrheit" (SZ 221). Henry does not cite it.

Henry says that this is the ultimate foundation of the union Eckhart puts at the centre: the union has phenomenological meaning only if the essence manifests itself in the soul as its own, and "cette manifestation dans l'âme de l'essence est le fait de celle-ci: [...] l'essence en réalité qui se manifeste à elle-même"; Eckhart: "si l'homme est heureux, [...] c'est parce qu'il connaît combien Dieu lui est proche" (n. 110); "Dans le même acte de connaissance où Dieu se connaît lui-même, l'âme reçoit sans médiation son essence de Dieu" (n. 111); "rien ne pénètre [...] au cœur de l'homme qu'à travers toute la douceur de Dieu" (n. 112), "c'est de façon divine que prend forme en lui tout ce qui lui advient" (n. 113); man is a "théognoste", "ein Gottwissender Mensch", friend rather than servant (n. 114), "nous adorons ce que nous connaissons" (n. 115). Henry's argument that the union is real only as known repeats SZ's point that the Dasein's being is disclosed to it (SZ 12, 133) and that the disclosure is not an addition to the structure. What SZ calls the Da is "erschlossen" "mit und durch sein Sein" (SZ 12); in Eckhart the divine knowledge is the self-knowledge of God in which the soul shares. The formal parallel is that the knowing is a constitutive moment of the being. Henry's use of "ami sait tout ce que sait son ami" takes up a textual image that SZ itself turns to for another purpose, the "Stimme des Freundes, den jedes Dasein bei sich trägt" (SZ 163), the voice of the friend as the primary openness of Dasein for its ownmost potentiality-for-being; there the friend is Dasein itself in the conscience-like hearing, not God.

Henry says that interpreting unity as constitutive of experience determines the nature of revelation: it arises "indépendamment du processus ontologique de l'objectivation", not as a horizon or exteriority; Eckhart: "L'image est sans image, parce qu'elle n'est pas vue dans une autre image" (n. 116); the soul "comprend et connaît [...] immédiatement et sans image"; yet the image, what is "purifié et criblé dans l'air et la lumière", also appears, so the concept of phenomenality divides: "la connaissance du soir", in images, grasping the creature "comme telle", and "la connaissance du matin", whose structure is unity and which sees creatures "dans l'Unité qui est Dieu même" (n. 117). The division of phenomenality into two orders is the central structural claim of the book, the one SZ's phenomenon concept has no room for (SZ 28–31: phenomenon is one concept, with its *Erscheinung*, *Schein* and *Phänomen* as derivative).
