Eckhart's "soir/matin" is the medieval ancestor of the distinction between the objective visible and the invisible, and Henry's use of it is Re-inscription: Eckhart's *cognitio vespertina/matutina* is a distinction of two ways of knowing the creature, while Henry's is a distinction of two phenomenalities.

Henry says that the same distinction in the structure of ontological knowledge is expressed in the "homme extérieur" and "homme intérieur" (n. 118); the existential sense dropped, the inner man is "noble parce qu'il est un et que dans l'Unité il connaît également Dieu et la créature" (n. 119); Henry holds that the ontological meaning of the distinction is stated by Eckhart himself: "l'âme a deux yeux [...] L'œil intérieur de l'âme regarde vers l'essence et la reçoit *directement* de Dieu; *c'est l'œuvre qui lui est propre*. L'œil extérieur [...] se tourne vers les créatures et les perçoit en images" (n. 120). The inner/outer man corresponds, on the existential side, to SZ's refusal of the inner/outer scheme (SZ 62: Dasein "is always already outside"). Henry's "inner man" is not an inner sphere in the sense SZ criticizes, because the inner is for Henry the opposite of the world and does not belong to a subject that must "get out" to it. The difference from SZ remains that SZ denies there is an inner, and Henry makes it the origin.

Henry says that the revelation of the essence in unity does not merely stand beside the manifestation of the horizon but grounds it: "autre est la puissance par laquelle l'œil voit, et autre la puissance par quoi il connaît qu'il voit"; the latter "est plus noble et plus haute" (n. 121); but all givens have equal right, so the hierarchy remains speculative unless the original phenomenological content of the vision is shown to be the original phenomenological content of the essence, which is unity, so that "Dieu a toutes choses cachées en lui, non pas de telle sorte que ceci ou cela soit distinct, mais toutes choses ne font qu'un" (n. 122); there above, "on connaît vraiment les choses telles qu'elles sont, toutes indivises et proches les unes des autres; les choses qui sont ici-bas éloignées les unes des autres sont rapprochées là-haut, parce que toutes n'y sont que dans le présent" (n. 123); the milieu is not a metaphysical afterworld but "coextensif à leur manifestation"; the soul "possède une puissance dans laquelle tout devient un", "elle rassemble ce qui est dispersé et partagé" (n. 124), "comprend avec unité toute diversité en elle-même" (n. 125). The claim that the seeing of the object has as its ground the knowing that one sees repeats the Kantbuch's and Husserl's thesis of a pre-reflective self-awareness. In SZ the corresponding structure is that every disclosure of beings co-discloses Dasein itself: the understanding of Dasein's "Sicht", which SZ calls transparency, a seeing of itself in every disclosure (SZ 146). Eckhart's "ici-bas éloignées, là-haut rapprochées" is the image by which Henry reaches the contrast with *Ent-fernung* (SZ 105): nearness in SZ is made by a distance-overcoming act in the world, nearness in Eckhart is the absence of distance in the present of the origin.

Henry says that the revelation of the essence in unity, because it grounds the manifestation of the horizon, is "le fondement phénoménologique universel effectif de toute manifestation possible en général, l'essence du Logos"; Eckhart's theory of the Word: the Word is the Son, the revelation in its actual accomplishment is the absolute's work, which comes about in unity: the absolute "l'engendre dans son Fils unique pour que nous soyons aussi le même Fils. Mais tout en engendrant il demeure en lui-même, mais tout en demeurant lui-même, il engendre et *se manifeste*" (n. 126); "Qu'est-ce que la Parole de Dieu, c'est opération de Dieu" (n. 127); "Le Verbe était en Dieu": "Il lui était absolument égal, il était en lui *sans médiation*" (n. 128); but equality is rejected as still on the plane of transcendence: "dans l'Essence où ils sont un, ils ne sont même plus « égaux », car l'égalité suppose déjà la différence" (n. 129). SZ's *logos* is determined as "Sehenlassen" in discourse (SZ 32–33), a letting something be seen from the thing itself, with the structure of an "als" (SZ 33: apophantic synthesis and diairesis). Henry's essence of the Logos is an immanent self-revelation without sight and without "als", which he has just derived from Eckhart's text. It is a Radicalization of the finding of §37 ¶24 and an explicit replacement of SZ's determination, though Henry does not cite SZ §7B. The nearest SZ locus on the Word is SZ's determination of *Rede*: "Das Hören ist für das Reden konstitutiv" (SZ 163), where hearing and speaking co-constitute discourse; Henry's "ce qui écoute et ce qui est entendu est justement même chose" (next unit) is the identity of the hearer and the heard that SZ does not assert.

Henry says that the unity of Father and Son, essence and Word, is not that which the young Hegel recognized between Jesus and God (n. 130), the homogeneity of life, since the Logos would arise for Hegel "sur le fond de la différence, et par la médiation de celle-ci": the Word that "prononcé, laisse aliéné et vide celui qui le prononce, mais est entendu non moins immédiatement" (n. 131); against this, Eckhart: "ce qui écoute et ce qui est entendu est justement même chose dans la Parole éternelle" (n. 132), "tout ce qu'enseigne le Père éternel, c'est son Essence, sa Nature et sa totale Déité", "Il nous révèle tout ensemble dans son Fils unique"; the being-heard of the Word is not the being-alienated of the essence but its reality. The Hegel contrast is independent of SZ. SZ's *Rede* (SZ 161–166) includes hearing as constitutive and is an existential of Mitsein; the "même chose" of speaker and hearer is the opposite of SZ's, in which the speaker and the other who hears are different Dasein (SZ 162: communication as the "Teilung" of co-attunement and of understanding, and never a transport of experiences from one subject's interior to another's). Henry's absolute speaks to itself.

Henry says that the ultimate ontological meaning of the theory of the Logos, determining revelation from unity, is rigorously expressed in Eckhart's three Words: the spoken (angel, man, creature), the thought but unspoken ("je le recueille en moi *à ce niveau précis où la représentation est encore possible*"), and the third, which "reste impensé et inexprimé et ne sort jamais mais demeure éternellement dans celui qui le dit"; "La raison dirige, opère toujours au-dedans [...] même la béatitude de Dieu repose sur l'opération interne de la raison là où le Verbe éternel demeure en lui-même" (n. 133). SZ has a related articulation in *Rede*: the structural moments of discourse, "Worüber", the said as such, communication and manifestation (SZ 162), and the silence of *Schweigen* as an essential possibility of discourse (SZ 164–165: "Schweigen [...] ist ein Modus der Rede"; "Wer im Miteinanderreden schweigt, kann eigentlicher »zu verstehen geben«"). The unspoken third Word in Eckhart is an inner Word; SZ's silence is a mode of discourse for Dasein with others, still in a world. Henry mentions neither.

Henry concludes that the ultimate interpretation reached by Eckhart still requires: (1) developing the structure further to show the totality of its ontological characters and the unity they prescribe; (2) making intelligible why this structure, constitutive of the Logos, "n'a précisément jamais été comprise comme telle", why the revelation conceals itself essentially in the effectivity of its accomplishment; and (3) showing what constitutes, "au sein même de l'acte par lequel elle se « dissimule »", the positive phenomenological content of this revelation. The paragraph is a programme for §§41–51. The second task, why revelation conceals itself, is the nearest to SZ's thesis that phenomena are "zunächst und zumeist" covered up (SZ 35–36), which Henry will meet at §49 and in his account of the invisible.


### § 41. Immanence and absolute situation

Henry says that the elaboration of immanence has shown in it the end of a power, so that immanence is affected by an impotence; this concerns the essence's relation to itself, not its relation to something else, and since "possibility" has meant "surpassing" (freedom, in these researches), the impossibility is that of the being surpassing *itself*. The paragraph is independent of SZ. It prepares the claim of §37 ¶13 and fixes the vocabulary, "possibilité", "dépasser", by which Henry will read SZ's *Seinkönnen* at §42.

Henry says that the impossibility has two senses: first, of "sortir de soi", which usually means transcendence and the relation to the other, so that the impossibility of leaving oneself characterizes the relation to self only negatively, by excluding alterity; and second, positively, if the being gives itself to itself as the term of the relation. The paragraph is independent of SZ. The "relation de l'être avec soi" has in SZ the form of the *Seinsverhältnis* of Dasein to its being (SZ 12), a relation that SZ analyses as ecstatic and that Henry, as in §30 ¶11, wants to read as non-relational.

Henry says that the second sense means that the being "ne peut se séparer de lui-même, se retirer de soi, revenir en deçà de ce qu'il est, ouvrir enfin une dimension de repli", and that this impossibility "constitue encore elle-même le soi de l'être, la possibilité fondamentale et ultime pour que se concrétise en lui quelque chose comme une ipséité." The structure is that of SZ 284: "Existierend kommt es nie hinter seine Geworfenheit zurück" (existing, Dasein never gets behind its thrownness). Henry's wording is nearly a translation of that sentence into the vocabulary of the self; SZ itself says of the Self that it "kann dessen *nie* mächtig werden" (can never become master of its ground, SZ 284). What Henry adds is the equation of this inability with selfhood (*ipséité*), which he postpones ("cette dernière remarque est prématurée") to the problematic of Section IV. SZ's *Selbstsein* is constituted otherwise, as the *Sein des Grundes* of a being that has to take it over (SZ 285).

Henry says that the remark is premature, and that the impossibility can be understood only from the positivity of the relation of being to itself, from "la cohérence de l'essence dans l'unité originaire": impossibility means "unité, nécessité de cette unité, lien, lien qui ne peut être délié"; it signifies "son attachement irrémissible à soi, le caractère irréductible du lien qui l'enchaîne à lui-même." The paragraph is independent of SZ. The chain image is Henry's, and SZ's counterpart is the *Last* of mood (SZ 284: "in seiner Schwere, die ihm die Stimmung als Last offenbar macht"); Henry does not use it here.

Henry asks what this repetition of the structure of immanence yields, and answers: "ce qui ne peut ni se couper de soi ni survoler son être, ce qui n'est pas susceptible de se contempler de l'extérieur, ni de prendre à aucun moment vis-à-vis de soi un libre point de vue, c'est ce qui est situé"; the structure of immanence "fournit son fondement transcendantal au concept de situation." This is where the book's use of "situation" begins, and the word needs fixing against SZ. In SZ, *Situation* is a term of Division Two: "die Situation ist das je in der Entschlossenheit erschlossene Da, als welches das existierende Seiende da ist" (SZ 300), "nur durch und in der Entschlossenheit" (SZ 300), and closed to the Man (SZ 300). Henry's "situation" covers what SZ calls *Geworfenheit* and *Befindlichkeit*, plus the Merleau-Pontian and Sartrean *situation*. The ground of Henry's whole argument in §§41–44 is that the concept of situation, so extended, has its transcendental ground in immanence. SZ's resolute Da is one of the things he will argue has the structure of a derivative.

Henry says that the determinations "impossibilité de survoler son être propre", "de se placer à l'extérieur de soi", "de se couper de soi" belong to being-in-situation as pre-critical consciousness takes it, and that reflection has tried to express them in the critique of classical philosophy's claim to absolute truth, in the rejection of the "point de vue de Sirius", and in the uniqueness of each situation, which reaches others only laterally and cannot survey the whole of possible situations from above. The paragraph names no one; the "point de vue de Sirius" is Merleau-Ponty's and the uniqueness of each existence is Jaspers' and Sartre's language. SZ's nearest locus is *Jemeinigkeit* (SZ 42) and the thrown character of each Dasein; it does not use the Sirius image.

Henry says that without the origin of the impossibility the determinations "flottent en l'air", so that being-in-situation becomes contingent for being, and that this is already visible in classical philosophy of the body, which makes the body a synthetic addition accounting for the "limitation" of being; the idea of limitation requires an intervention external to the original, and philosophies that claim to derive situation from the ontological element itself but keep the idea of limit fall under suspicion; the "surgissement originel de la situation [...] signifie [...] sa propre expansion et la libération de son essence." The classical philosophy of the body is Descartes' and Malebranche's, discussed in the earlier sections. SZ defers the body: "Die Verräumlichung des Daseins in seiner »Leiblichkeit«, die eine eigene hier nicht zu behandelnde Problematik in sich birgt" (SZ 108). Henry's suspicion of any "limitation" applies to SZ's finitude, which he will treat at §42 ¶7 and in n. 157.

Henry says that the determination of the essence of situation from being must be recognized by this: the nature of the determinations of being-in-situation is identical with that of being itself, so that their impossibility is explained by the being's impossibility of surpassing itself, whence the transcendental origin of the concept of situation presupposes the constitution of the structure as immanence; immanence and situation are co-determined, and immanence "reçoit [...] en retour [...] une lumière accrue." The paragraph is Henry's own method statement and independent of SZ.

Henry says that, in the light of "l'essence de la non-liberté où l'impossibilité pour l'être de se dépasser soi-même trouve son origine ontologique ultime", situation is not only positively the "insurmontable et irrémissible attachement à soi de l'être" but negatively "étranger à l'idée de la liberté" and eidetically incompatible with it; liberty is the origin of the world and of the possibility of taking an attitude, whose modes ("accepter", "refuser", "choisir", "prendre sur soi", "souffrir", "assumer") are behaviours inside the world, each with a "vue" and a "tonalité affective", whereas situation "est précisément ce qui ne peut être choisi, ce qu'on ne peut « accepter » ou « refuser »". The list names SZ's vocabulary of authentic existence: "wählen", "übernehmen", "tragen" (SZ 284–285), and the *Entschluß in die Situation* by which resoluteness takes over the factical Da: "Die entschlossene Übernahme des eigenen faktischen »Da« bedeutet zugleich den Entschluß in die Situation" (SZ 383). The paragraph is therefore a preemptive Contestation of SZ's resoluteness, though it names no text. SZ's reply is already in 284: Dasein has to take over what it can never master, which is not a free choice of the situation but an assuming of what is given; Henry's point is that the assuming is itself a behaviour that presupposes the unchosen.

Henry says that this impossibility of choosing is not a limit met by freedom in the exercise of its power, since the issue is not the commonly admitted impossibility of refusing a situation but the commonly refused impossibility of accepting it; the two are one, "l'impossibilité originelle où se trouve l'essence de prendre attitude à l'égard de soi, l'impossibilité de la liberté comme impossibilité constituée, non par celle-ci, mais par son essence, comme impossibilité non constituée"; it is "le non-comportement". The target of "dissertations interminables et habituellement pathétiques" that prescribe the "acceptation" of a situation that is at the same time the product of freedom is the French existentialism of n. 147, which Henry says translates SZ on the psychological plane. The pointer to SZ is to SZ 284: Henry's "non-comportement" corresponds to SZ's thrownness as what Dasein *is* ("Dasein ist ständig – solange es ist – als Sorge sein »Daß«") and not a behaviour; the distinction between the "Daß" and the existentiell taking-over is SZ's own, as at §37 ¶14.

Henry says that the ontological determination of situation in its radical opposition to freedom concerns freedom first: freedom can do its work, being the free surpassing toward a world, only by remaining in itself as what it is and has to be; "La transcendance a un soi, elle est la transcendance de son soi", the self never what it transcends but what transcends; the questions "Comment la transcendance a-t-elle [...] un « soi » ?" and "ce que signifie pour elle « être comme ce qu'elle est et a à être »" cannot be answered from surpassing without tautology or without making transcendence "la seule origine à partir de laquelle elle a [...] à être ce qu'elle est et [...] à assumer, dans ce qui constitue alors son délaissement le plus insurmontable, le mode d'existence qui est chaque fois le sien"; or the determination of transcendence as being-one's-self and as "être-laissé-à-soi" comes "de ce qui la laisse invinciblement à elle-même dans l'impossibilité pour elle de se dépasser"; the "avoir à être" of freedom "ne vient pas d'elle mais de son anti-essence fondamentale". Henry's "avoir à être" is SZ 12's *zu sein hat* ("das Seiende, dem es in seinem Sein um dieses Sein geht" and SZ 284: "daß es ist und zu sein hat"). The question is Henry's own question about SZ's concept: whence does the "has to be" arise? In SZ the answer is thrownness-as-care: the being is "überantwortet" (SZ 284) to itself. Henry reads the "überantwortet" as the trace of the anti-essence; SZ does not name an anti-essence and gives the structure as the being of care. The unit is a Radicalization: the origin of the "has to be" is displaced from SZ's care to a non-freedom beneath it.

Henry says that transcendence is not what it is when delivered to itself on the ground of its own essence, but as determined independently of it by its "anti-essence", its characters being "l'être-soi de ce qui ne peut se séparer de soi, l'être-situé de ce qui [...] ne peut se dépasser soi-même ni échapper à sa « condition »"; surpassing realizes its essence "sur le fond en lui de son anti-essence", so that it can never take back what it accomplishes, "l'éloignement et l'être-éloigné", because it is situated within its law; the "caractère indépassable du dépassement" constitutes being-situated; "Voilà pourquoi « le *Dasein* ne peut vaincre son éloignement du *Zuhanden* », non seulement parce que, comme le dit Heidegger, « il est essentiellement éloignement, c'est-à-dire spatial » (n. 134, SZ 108), mais pour cette raison plus ultime, et explicitée par la problématique, [...] que, sur le fond en elle de son anti-essence, la transcendance ne peut être transcendée." SZ 108: "Diese Ent-fernung, die Ferne des Zuhandenen von ihm selbst, kann das Dasein *nie kreuzen*" and "weil es wesenhaft Ent-fernung, das heißt räumlich ist". Henry's quotation is accurate in sense, and "éloignement" renders *Ent-fernung* as removal, which reverses SZ's meaning: *Ent-fernung* is a nearing, "Näherung" (SZ 105, 108), in which "Entfernen" abolishes the remoteness of things (guard 12). The unsurpassability in SZ comes from this: Dasein cannot cross its de-severance because it takes it along ("es vielmehr mitgenommen hat und ständig mitnimmt"), being de-severing itself. Henry reads the sentence as a symptom of a deeper unsurpassability, and the "plus ultime" ground he gives (anti-essence) is not in SZ; what he keeps is the structure that Dasein's own spatiality cannot be overcome by it. The unit is a Re-inscription of SZ 108 as a case of the book's thesis.

Henry says that, since transcendence cannot be transcended, it is the irrevocable destiny of being; the point of view is irrevocable, but the ontological determination of being-in-situation had shown the *impossibility* of a point of view, so the two seem to conflict; they are reconciled: "insurmontabilité du point de vue et impossibilité d'en prendre un sont solidaires et déterminées entre elles comme la transcendance et l'immanence." The paragraph is Henry's own construction, with no SZ locus. SZ's *Ausrichtung* (SZ 108) is the structure Henry reads as the "point de vue": Dasein has always already taken a direction in a region.

Henry says that the determinations of being-in-situation may appear singular to naive consciousness, yet nourish the representations by which it tries to make its situation accessible, notably the idea of a *center* that specifies perspectives and cannot be changed or exchanged: a fixed point cannot move, "quand il ne peut s'en aller à l'extérieur de soi"; the centre "représente dans l'extériorité ce qui la nie", and this representation "est celle, par la conscience naïve, de l'immanence." The paragraph is independent of SZ. SZ explicitly denies that Dasein is a point or a bodily thing: "Die Näherung ist nicht orientiert auf das körperbehaftete Ichding" and "Die Räumlichkeit des Daseins wird [...] nicht bestimmt durch Angabe der Stelle, an der ein Körperding vorhanden ist" (SZ 108), and "Das Dasein ist [...] zunächst nie hier, sondern dort" (SZ 108). Henry's thought of the center as a fixed point is thus not SZ's model of situation, and what SZ calls *Hier* is derived from a *Dort*; this will matter at §43 ¶1 where Henry cites it.

Henry says that the ontological determination of the essence of situation as immanence refers analysis to history: it is in contemporary philosophy of existence that the concept of situation "se trouve élucidé pour lui-même et constitue à ce titre le thème explicite de la problématique"; that philosophy develops "exclusivement à l'intérieur du champ ouvert par l'idée de la transcendance" and remains blind to the essence that determines it as originally situated, so its failure is foreseeable and its exposition verifies Henry's theses. The target is unnamed: it is SZ and the French existentialism that follows it (nn. 135–205). The claim that the philosophy of existence first treats situation thematically refers to SZ's analysis of the Situation in the resolute Da (SZ 299–300), to Jaspers' *Grenzsituation* and to Sartre. Henry's own statement of the verification proceeds by Section III's analysis of the "ambiguïté de la Nichtigkeit" (§42) and the "hétérogénéité" of situation and temporality (§43).

### § 42. The ontological determination of the essence of situation as immanence and the fundamental ambiguity of the *Nichtigkeit*

Henry says that once it is the explicit theme of a problematic, the concept of situation is understood in its pure ontological meaning, which demands rejecting the realism that attributes to the being the character by which it always shows itself in a situation; Aristotle already thinks place as the limit of the thing and not as its interior; being situated implies a relation to a milieu, generally space, but space is not first objective: "La situation n'est pas primitivement celle des réalités données mais des outils, comme telle elle se réfère nécessairement au Souci. Celui-ci [...] détermine chaque fois une situation, c'est lui qui « décide de la proximité... de l'ustensile... dans le monde ambiant »" (n. 135, SZ 107); and Merleau-Ponty: "Mon corps est là où il y a quelque chose à faire" (n. 136). SZ 107: "Über Nähe und Ferne des umweltlich zunächst Zuhandenen entscheidet das umsichtige Besorgen" (circumspective concern decides on the nearness and remoteness of what is at hand in the surrounding world). The quotation is accurate in its ellipses. The subject, however, is *das umsichtige Besorgen*, which Henry's own text renders earlier as "préoccupation ambiante", while the sentence puts "le Souci" (*Sorge*) in front of it; the shift from *Besorgen* (dealing with things) to *Sorge* (the being of Dasein) makes SZ's analysis of equipment's nearness into an analysis of the being of Dasein as such. SZ does not call the nearness of tools a "situation" in this passage; *Situation* is a Division Two term of the resolute Da (SZ 299–300). The paragraph's thesis, "l'existence se comprend à partir de ce qu'elle projette, [...] elle détermine aussi en cela sa situation", rewrites SZ's *Verstehen* as *Sichentwerfen* (SZ 146) and adds the sense of situation. A Re-inscription at the level of vocabulary.

Henry says that the existential determination of situation "n'a rien d'originel" and betrays its indigence at the origin, found in anxiety: the objects of concern sink into indifference and the nothingness of the world appears: "Le monde ainsi ouvert ne peut plus libérer l'étant que *dans le caractère de la non-situation*" (n. 137, SZ 343), and concern "ne trouve rien à partir de quoi elle pourrait se comprendre"; the collapse of the existential concept of situation shows its ontological ground, since the world in anxiety "la rend chaque fois possible", so the problem of situation concentrates on world and being-in-the-world as such. SZ 343: "die so erschlossene Welt kann nur Seiendes freigeben im Charakter der *Unbewandtnis*" (the world so disclosed can only release beings in the character of non-involvement), and "das besorgende Gewärtigen findet nichts, woraus es sich verstehen könnte" (concernful awaiting finds nothing from which it could understand itself). Henry's "non-situation" renders *Unbewandtnis* with "situation", which is a rendering already carrying his thesis: *Bewandtnis* (involvement) is the being of equipment (SZ 84; see also n. 186 below), and nothing in its sense is situation. The unit is Mediation by a translation. The selection matters as well: the same paragraph of SZ continues that anxiety "bringt zurück auf das pure Daß der eigensten, vereinzelten Geworfenheit" (SZ 343), the thrownness that Henry takes up at §42 ¶¶9–13 without noting that SZ ties it to anxiety here. SZ's world-collapse is the way thrownness is disclosed, not (as in Henry's sequence) a prelude to a ground beyond.

Henry says that the concentration on world and being-in-the-world is not the determination of situation from the world as origin; the power that founds the world is not unsituated: "Ce n'est pas la liberté qui situe originellement mais elle est située"; the problem is to "déterminer la situation de l'être-dans-le-monde lui-même en tant que tel". The paragraph is independent of SZ; it paraphrases the GA 9 argument that transcendence is itself grounded (GA 9 166 ff.). In SZ, the corresponding point is that world is "worumwillen" projected by Dasein while Dasein is thrown into it (SZ 135, 284).

Henry says that the philosophy of transcendence leaves to transcendence the task of founding its own situation, and that this is one of its "visées ultimes encore que le plus souvent cachée"; as a ground, transcendence "apparaît comme située." The paragraph is independent of SZ and sets up the question of ground that GA 9 poses.

Henry says that to found does not simply mean "faire régner un monde"; the world rules over beings only so far as the transcendence that projects it finds itself among beings, "investie par lui", and, "comme le dit encore Heidegger", "accordée au ton de cet existant qui la pénètre", having "pris base dans l'existant" (n. 138, WG 99); founding is therefore "se fonder", where transcendence "ne « donne » pas seulement mais « prend elle-même un fondement »" (n. 139, WG 98). This is GA 9, not SZ: *Vom Wesen des Grundes* (1929) 166, where Dasein "vom Seienden eingenommen so, daß es [...] von ihm durchstimmt ist" and "im Seienden Boden genommen" has, and the three ways of grounding as *Stiften*, *Bodennehmen*, *Begründen* (GA 9 165). Henry's "comme le dit encore Heidegger" gives the passage as a continuation of SZ's doctrine of attunement (SZ 134–138) and of thrownness; the chronology is later and the vocabulary (*Bodennehmen*, *Gründen*) is not SZ's. The two meet in the thought that Dasein is always already attuned in the midst of beings; GA 9 166 says that this belongs to transcendence, which SZ does not, as it keeps attunement as an existential equiprimordial with understanding and speech (SZ 133, 137). (Guard 13.)

Henry says that taking a ground in beings, finding and feeling oneself in the midst of them, is to be situated, and that this investment by the world is identical with the projection of the world: "Le *Dasein* ne pourrait être [...] pénétré par la tonalité de l'existant [...] si cet investissement par l'existant n'était accompagné de l'éclosion d'un monde", "si le *Dasein* est investi par l'existant, ce n'en est pas moins uniquement *comme* être-dans-le-monde" (n. 140, WG 100), the transcendence "éprouve sa situation dans un projet" (n. 141, WG 104), and "bien que se sentant au milieu de l'existant [...] c'est comme un libre pouvoir-être que le *Dasein* se trouve *jeté* parmi l'existant" (n. 142, WG 110 = GA 9 175: "als freies Seinkönnen unter das Seiende geworfen"), and "le *Dasein* fonde, « institue » le monde uniquement en tant que lui-même « se fonde » au milieu de l'existant" (n. 143, WG 100). All from GA 9, none from SZ. Henry's conclusion that being-in-situation belongs to the founding act and not to something added to it is a faithful reading of GA 9 166–167; he then uses it as the position against which he will bring non-founding forward.

Henry says that transcendence is not only invested by the world and thrown into it, but its investment is effective only as privation (the projection implies withdrawal of certain possibilities); transcendence understands in it "la finitude de son destin", and "la finitude transcendantale de l'horizon signifie identiquement la mort" (n. 144: "Parce que l'horizon est celui du temps"); thus *In-der-Welt-sein* is determined as being-toward-death, which "apporte la transcendance dans sa situation et lui rend visible la vérité particulière et concrète de celle-ci." SZ 331 states that original time is finite ("Die ursprüngliche Zeit ist endlich"), and SZ 259–264 ties the wholeness and the finitude of Dasein to anticipation of death; the identification of the finitude of the horizon with death is Henry's inference from the horizon of time (n. 144), not a SZ formula. SZ's own point is the other way round: finite time is the ground of Dasein's being-toward-the-end, not its consequence. The unit is an Extension with an inverted order.

Henry says that the claim that transcendence constitutes the ground of its own situation and its specificity proves illusory when philosophy of transcendence asks radically about the essence and the situation of the ground; situation is "le *Dasein* en tant que l'être de celui-ci se trouve compris ontologiquement comme transcendance", but to be situated is not simply to find oneself in a situation; "Pourquoi la transcendance se trouve-t-elle dans la situation qui est la sienne, c'est-à-dire dans le *Dasein* ?" and does its ground lie in freedom? "*Le « Dasein » a-t-il… décidé librement et pourra-t-il décider là-dessus, à savoir s'il veut ou non venir dans le « Dasein »*" (n. 145, SZ 228). SZ 228: "*Hat je Dasein als es selbst frei darüber entschieden, und wird es je darüber entscheiden können, ob es ins »Dasein« kommen will oder nicht?*" Henry's ellipsis drops "als es selbst" and the quotation is otherwise accurate. In SZ the sentence closes the argument that truth must be presupposed (SZ 227–228): "Wir *müssen* die Wahrheit voraussetzen [...] Das gehört zur wesenhaften Geworfenheit des Daseins in die Welt." Henry lifts it from the setting of truth to that of the ground of situation. This is a fair use: SZ itself uses the question to name thrownness as the reason Dasein cannot decide whether it will exist, and the question is at that point the nearest SZ comes to the thesis of §42. The unit is a Reconstruction with a change of context.

Henry says that the philosophy of transcendence has answered: thrown into the world for the sake of which it exists, transcendence "ne subit pas seulement celui-ci mais, plus fondamentalement, l'acte originel par lequel elle se temporalise en lui", and he quotes: "C'est comme un libre pouvoir-être, disait Heidegger, que le *Dasein* se trouve jeté parmi l'existant. Qu'il soit en puissance un « soi-même » et qu'il le soit en fait proportionnellement chaque fois à sa liberté, que la transcendance se temporalise comme acte proto-historial, tout cela n'est pas au pouvoir de cette liberté elle-même [...] une telle impuissance, le fait qu'il se trouve jeté, abandonné, n'est pas simplement le résultat de l'empiètement de l'existant sur le *Dasein*, cette impuissance détermine l'être de l'homme comme tel" (n. 146, WG 110 = GA 9 175: "Solche Ohnmacht (*Geworfenheit*) aber ist nicht erst das Ergebnis des Eindringens von Seiendem auf das Dasein, sondern sie bestimmt dessen Sein als solches"). The text is GA 9 again, and it is the strongest textual support for Henry's thesis in the corpus: Heidegger in 1929 names the *Ohnmacht* of freedom to give itself its own being, and derives the finitude of Dasein from it. Henry's "le texte que nous commentons" is this one, and the SZ counterpart is SZ 284–285 (the *Nicht* of thrownness). Henry then concludes that situation divides: "Dans l'investissement la transcendance fonde chaque fois sa situation, non le fait pour elle de se trouver dans celle-ci"; the essence of situation lies "dans le fait de se trouver ainsi en elle [...] de telle manière cependant que cette venue ne dépende pas d'elle"; therefore "être situé veut dire, pour la transcendance, ne pas être le fondement de sa situation." The division is Henry's, and it names the point at which the book and GA 9 divide: GA 9 holds both senses under transcendence ("alle Weltentwurf ist geworfener"), Henry assigns them to two origins (transcendence and its anti-essence).

Henry says that "tout cela est contenu aussi dans *Sein und Zeit*", and the more manifestly because the claim to absorb *Befindlichkeit* in the founding activity of transcendence is neither its only theme nor its explicit concern; the *Dasein* appears as a *Seinkönnen* that, by projecting and choosing its possibilities, is delivered to itself, deciding each time of its being "sans recours", so it is "dans son existence, le fondement de son pouvoir-être", and "dans son abandon à lui-même [...] congédié pour être comme soi l'être du fondement", so it is situated; the question follows whether, since being-situated belongs to it as being the ground of its potentiality-for-being, it is also the ground of its situation (n. 147: French existentialism understood this aspect "le mieux", reducing it to "traduction immédiate sur un plan psychologique": "qu'à la guerre [...] il n'y ait pas de victimes innocentes"). Henry's paraphrase matches SZ 284: "Als dieses Seiende, dem überantwortet es einzig als das Seiende, das es ist, existieren kann, *ist* es *existierend* der Grund seines Seinkönnens." The additions, "sans recours", "décidant chaque fois lui-même", "congédié", are decisionist and go beyond SZ 284; n. 147 names their source, French existentialism (Sartre's "pas de victimes innocentes", *L'Être et le Néant*; Henry quotes it in the note), and the unit is a Mediation: the SZ doctrine is first presented in Sartre's psychological register, then corrected against it.

Henry says that being a ground means, as Heidegger himself says, "ne jamais [...] être maître de son être le plus propre" ("Être un fondement veut dire ne jamais… être maître de son être le plus propre"); the Self "qui a comme tel à poser le fondement de lui-même ne peut jamais se rendre maître de celui-ci"; and so "Le *Dasein*, dit Heidegger, n'est pas en tant que soi le fondement de son être" (n. 148, SZ 284–285); how can it be and not be the ground? The concept of situation can keep unity only if it divides; "Donnons ici encore la parole à Heidegger: « En existant le *Dasein* est déjeté, non apporté de lui-même dans son *Da* » (n. 150, SZ 284)", the essence of situation being thrownness, "le fait pour le « Dasein » de ne pas s'apporter lui-même dans son « Da », c'est-à-dire de n'être jamais le fondement de ce qu'il est"; and the *Stimmung* as burden (n. 151). SZ 285: "Das Dasein ist nicht insofern selbst der Grund seines Seins, *als dieser aus eigenem Entwurf erst entspringt*, wohl aber ist es als Selbstsein das *Sein* des Grundes." Henry's quotation "n'est pas en tant que soi le fondement de son être" keeps the first clause of SZ's sentence in a compressed form, and omits what follows the comma: "als dieser aus eigenem Entwurf erst entspringt" (in so far as this ground first arises from its own projection) and "wohl aber ist es als Selbstsein das *Sein* des Grundes". With the omitted clause the apparent contradiction (is and is not the ground) is resolved by SZ in the sentence itself: Dasein does not *lay* its ground, but it *is* the being of the ground. Henry's problem, how can the two determinations "néanmoins [...] s'unir", is thus partly manufactured by the cut (guard 14), and his "dédoublement" of situation is the answer to a question that SZ's qualification already answers in another way. (Rendering: "Seiend ist das Dasein geworfenes" appears in Henry's French as "En existant le *Dasein* est déjeté"; "seiend" is "being", and "existing" is Henry's.) The unit is a Selection, and the n. 147 gloss is what makes the sentence "psychological" in a way SZ 284–285 is not.

Henry says that thrownness differs structurally from the abandonment to oneself of the *Dasein* as ground of its potentiality, and that this reading is possible because being-ground does not simply oppose what determines it as originally situated but "trouve encore dans cette détermination originelle de sa situation authentique, dans le n'être-pas-fondement de soi de son être, son propre fondement"; the true relation of these structures is "de fondation" and not only of opposition: "c'est parce que le « Dasein » n'est pas le fondement de son être qu'il lui est livré pour être, comme cet être dont il n'est pas le fondement, le fondement de son pouvoir-être"; the "pourtant" of SZ 284 is then restored: "Le Soi [...] qui a comme tel à poser le fondement de lui-même ne peut jamais se rendre maître de celui-ci et a *pourtant* en existant à assumer l'être-fondement." The "parce que" Henry reads into the sentence is already in SZ 284: Dasein is "überantwortet" to being and *therefore* "existierend der Grund seines Seinkönnens". The unit is a faithful Reconstruction of SZ's own order of foundation, and the claim that the relation is "de fondation" is SZ's: "Entwurf [...] als je geworfener" (SZ 285). What Henry adds is the naming of the founding term, the "n'être-pas-fondement", as the anti-essence. In SZ it is called *Geworfenheit* and it belongs to care.

Henry says that the abandonment of *Dasein* determining it to take on being-ground rests on its not being its own ground, which is the essential structure of thrownness; "Au n'être-pas-fondement de soi [...] appartient par essence un « ne… pas… »"; "« Ce « ne… pas… » [...] appartient au sens existential de la *Geworfenheit* »"; "Étant fondement, déclare l'auteur de *Sein und Zeit*, il *est* lui-même une *Nichtigkeit* de lui-même" (n. 152, SZ 284 [the passage is SZ 285, "Grundseiend *ist* es selbst eine Nichtigkeit seiner selbst"]); and the effective determination demands that the essence of *Nichtigkeit* be clarified: what does it mean not to be the ground of one's being, and first not to be a ground? Being a ground is being transcendence ("Comme transcendance, et cela d'autant plus clairement que celle-ci constitue son être et en même temps l'essence du fondement"), so "n'être pas ce fondement veut dire ne pas se trouver déterminé dans son être comme transcendance." The quotation is accurate (SZ 285: "Dieses *Nicht* gehört zum existenzialen Sinn der Geworfenheit"), and Henry's page reference is one off (n. 152 gives 284). The equation of being a ground with being transcendence is not in SZ 284–285: it is GA 9's (165–175: *Transzendenz* as *Gründen*). The unit is a Mediation by GA 9 of a SZ passage, and the translation of the *Nicht* into "ne pas se trouver déterminé comme transcendance" is the book's thesis.

Henry says that the non-determination of the being of *Dasein* by transcendence cannot remain negative if it contains the concrete positivity of being-in-situation, and quotes: "*Nichtigkeit* ne veut dire en aucune façon ne pas être donné, ne pas exister (*nicht bestehen*), mais signifie un « ne… pas… » qui constitue cet être du « Dasein », sa « Geworfenheit »", and "La *Nichtigkeit* existentiale n'a en aucune façon le caractère d'une privation, d'un manque…" (n. 153, SZ 284–285); where does this positivity lie? "dans l'immanence. Pour cette raison la « Nichtigkeit » se révèle identique à la « Geworfenheit » et la fonde." SZ 285: "Nichtigkeit bedeutet keineswegs Nichtvorhandensein, Nichtbestehen, sondern meint ein Nicht, das dieses *Sein* des Daseins, seine Geworfenheit, konstituiert" and "Die existenziale Nichtigkeit hat keineswegs den Charakter einer Privation, eines Mangels gegenüber einem ausgesteckten Ideal". Both are accurate. SZ itself gives the *Nicht* a positive determination immediately: "Nicht *durch* es selbst, sondern *an* es selbst *entlassen* aus dem Grunde, um *als* dieser zu sein" (not by itself but released to itself from the ground, to be it, SZ 285). The "an es selbst entlassen" is the structure Henry will name immanence; SZ gives it without the name, and with the stress on release, which Henry's "livré à soi" renders. So SZ has, contrary to Henry's next paragraph, a determination of the *Nicht*'s positivity. What it lacks is a ground for it beyond care, and SZ 285–286 says so.

Henry says that, because the positivity of the *Nichtigkeit* lies in immanence, it cannot be recognized with the presuppositions of Heidegger's philosophy and is therefore travestied "de deux façons": first, since *Dasein* as ground of its potentiality understands itself from its projected possibilities, the projection is a withdrawal: "Pouvant-être, dit Heidegger, le *Dasein* se tient [...] dans l'une ou l'autre possibilité, constamment il n'est *pas* l'autre et s'est privé d'elle dans son projet existentiel" (n. 154, SZ 285); this *Nichtigkeit* refers to the finitude of the projecting of possibilities, that is, to freedom: "La liberté, est-il dit, *n'est* que dans le choix d'une possibilité, c'est-à-dire dans le fait d'assumer le non-choix [...] de l'autre" (n. 155, SZ 285); it "n'a cependant rien à voir avec celle qui désigne [...] l'impuissance de la transcendance à se fonder elle-même", whence "l'ambiguïté fondamentale de la *Nichtigkeit* heideggerienne". SZ 285: "seinkönnend steht es je in der einen oder anderen Möglichkeit, ständig ist es eine andere *nicht* und hat sich ihrer im existenziellen Entwurf begeben", and "Die Freiheit aber *ist* nur in der Wahl der einen, das heißt im Tragen des Nichtgewählthabens und Nichtauchwählenkönnens der anderen." Henry's "assumer" renders *Tragen* (bearing), which tilts the clause toward the *Übernehmen* of the thrown ground; the quotation is otherwise accurate. The charge of ambiguity is stronger than the text allows in one respect: SZ states that there are two sources of nullity, "Der Entwurf ist nicht nur als je geworfener durch die Nichtigkeit des Grundseins bestimmt, sondern *als Entwurf* selbst wesenhaft *nichtig*" (SZ 285), and the distinction Henry reads as a confusion is SZ's own articulation, which SZ then unites in care. What the text does not do is show that the two nullities are one *Nicht*; SZ 285 asserts it ("In der Struktur der Geworfenheit sowohl wie in der des Entwurfs liegt wesenhaft eine Nichtigkeit") and admits in the same passage that the ontological sense of the *Nichtheit* "noch dunkel" is. Henry's Contestation is therefore well placed at the unification, less well placed at the distinction.

Henry says that the text "le projet n'est pas seulement, comme déjeté, déterminé par la *Nichtigkeit* de l'être-fondement, mais comme *projet* même il est essentiellement *nichtig*" (n. 156, SZ 285) must be read in the light of the two significations "finalement confondues par Heidegger"; the *Nichtigkeit* of thrownness "diffère totalement de celle qui appartient au projet en tant que tel", the proposition "dans la structure de la *Geworfenheit* aussi bien que dans celle du projet se trouve essentiellement une *Nichtigkeit*" (n. 157, SZ 285) being equivocal; the existential theme of guilt, "la détermination existentiale formelle de la culpabilité comme être-fondement d'une *Nichtigkeit*", and "Le *Dasein* comme tel est coupable" (n. 158, SZ 285), and "le Souci lui-même est dans son essence traversé de part en part par la *Nichtigkeit*" (n. 159, SZ 285) all depend on this ambiguity and are "inutilisable[s] sur le plan philosophique". The quotations are accurate. This is the unit's decisive Contestation: guilt in SZ is defined as "Grundsein einer Nichtigkeit" (SZ 284), and Henry argues that the definition, unifying two nullities, has no determinate content. The test against SZ: SZ's defence of the unification is that both are modes of the same *Nicht* in care, the thrown projecting that is "des (nichtigen) Grund-seins einer Nichtigkeit" (SZ 285), which gives the unity of the *Nicht* by the unity of care, not by a common ground outside it. Henry's note 157 extends the charge to finitude: GA 9 175 derives "ce qu'est essentiellement la finitude du *Dasein*" from the impotence of freedom, and the idea of finitude then "marque en fait la fin de toute finitude" yet is "immédiatement rapportée" to the founding mode and "complètement oubliée" — a claim about GA 9, with the SZ link to SZ 331 (finite time) left unexamined.

Henry says that Heidegger also attempted a general interpretation of *Nichtigkeit*, not related to the finitude of projecting, which is a second travesty: the general *Nichtigkeit* determining being and grounding guilt is of uncertain character, for he himself says that "le sens ontologique de la *Nichtheit* de cette *Nichtigkeit* existentiale demeure obscur" and "Cela vaut [...] de l'essence ontologique du « ne… pas… » en général" (n. 160, SZ 285); ontology and dialectic use negation without founding it; the remedy is "un éclaircissement thématique du sens de l'être en général" (n. 161, "SZ, 386"), "identique précisément à celui du néant", which in "l'heideggerianisme" is done by the discovery of transcendence and its essence; so the *Nichtigkeit*, whose ultimate philosophical meaning is the questioning of transcendence's power over its own being, "se trouve-t-elle renvoyer finalement à celle-ci, [...] à la transcendance elle-même comme à ce fondement", and "le phénomène essentiel auprès duquel elle se trouve apportée par son progrès [...] se trouve dès lors être perdu." SZ 285: "Trotzdem bleibt der *ontologische Sinn der Nichtheit* dieser existenzialen Nichtigkeit noch dunkel. Aber das gilt auch vom *ontologischen Wesen des Nicht überhaupt*." SZ 286 adds that the conditions of the problem of the *Nicht* are to be found "in der thematischen *Klärung des Sinnes von Sein* überhaupt" (Henry's n. 161 gives "386", a misprint for 286). What Henry says SZ then does, "par la découverte de la transcendance", is outside SZ: SZ leaves the thematic clarification of the sense of being unwritten, ending on its open questions (SZ 436–437), and it is the 1929 texts (GA 9; *Was ist Metaphysik?*) that treat the *Nichts* in terms of transcendence. Retrospectively marked, this makes Henry's "heideggerianisme" the Heidegger of 1929, with SZ's open question as the place where the slide begins. Henry's observation that SZ admits its obscurity at exactly the point where his own thesis locates the unresolved ground is fair, and is the most precise textual hold of §42 on SZ.


### § 43. Situation and temporality: the ontological heterogeneity of their original structures and its interpretation in the philosophy of transcendence: the idea of contingence and the fall of the *Dasein*

Henry says that Heidegger tried to determine the essence of situation further within his own presuppositions: to understand oneself from the possibilities one projects means to return upon oneself from them, and this return discovers one's situation; spatial situation presupposes a space which *Dasein* disposes and from which it "détermine chaque fois son propre lieu, de telle sorte qu'il revient de l'espace mis en place sur la place qu'il a occupée" (n. 162, SZ 368), so that "le *Dasein* conformément à sa spatialité n'est jamais d'abord ici mais là-bas; c'est à partir de ce là-bas qu'il revient sur son ici, [...] qu'il explique son être se souciant pour… à partir du *Zuhanden* qui est là-bas" (n. 163, SZ 107–108). Both quotations are accurate. SZ 368: "Es bestimmt je seinen eigenen Ort so, daß es aus dem eingeräumten Raum auf den »Platz« zurückkommt, den es eingenommen hat"; SZ 108: "Das Dasein ist gemäß seiner Räumlichkeit zunächst nie hier, sondern dort, aus welchem Dort es auf sein Hier zurückkommt." Henry's step is the generalization: the "return" in SZ's analysis of spatiality is made the structure of situation as such. SZ itself ties the return to temporality in the second passage (SZ 368 belongs to the temporal interpretation, §70), and it calls the spatial structure *Ent-fernung* and *Ausrichtung*, not situation; the term in SZ is reserved for the resolute Da (SZ 299–300; guard 15). An Extension that carries a spatial return into a general structure, which SZ licenses by its own use of *zurückkommen* for the temporal structure (SZ 326).

Henry says that the structure shows in fear, which is not the intuition of a threat but a return upon the self from it: "le s'attendre de la peur laisse le terme redoutable revenir en arrière sur le pouvoir-être factice se souciant", so that fear is "une « peur pour »" (n. 164, SZ 341); and in anxiety, where *Dasein*, facing death and breaking against it, "se trouve rejeté dans l'angoisse sur son existence factice" and sees the inescapable character of its condition, as being-toward-death. SZ 341: "daß das Gewärtigen der Furcht das Bedrohliche auf das faktisch besorgende Seinkönnen *zurückkommen* läßt" and "das Fürchten vor... je ein Fürchten *um*... ist". Accurate. In the anxiety clause the text Henry describes is SZ 385 ("frei für seinen Tod an ihm zerschellend auf sein faktisches Da sich zurückwerfen lassen") in the context of resolute anticipation, not of anxiety as treated at SZ 343, where anxiety "bringt zurück auf das pure Daß der eigensten, vereinzelten Geworfenheit"; Henry's paragraph merges the two moods and the two accounts. The unit is a Reconstruction, with the merger noted.

Henry says that the situation of *Dasein* arises in the act by which it returns upon itself from what it projects, and asks what that from-which is: never a being, even in fear, since the menacing term approaches only if the horizon of the future is open; from that horizon *Dasein* returns on itself and discovers itself "comme étant déjà, comme l'étant qui, en tant qu'il est, est déjà été"; the act of returning from the projected future on the been-ness of that act is temporality; fear and anxiety are grasped by Heidegger as modes of it, disclosing "l'abandon du *Dasein* dans la *Geworfenheit*, sa situation"; the concrete character of this abandonment, as deliverance to death since birth, is not just the return of future on past but is determined by the finite horizon of death. SZ 326: "Die Gewesenheit entspringt in gewisser Weise der Zukunft" (having-been arises, in a certain way, from the future), and "Das Vorlaufen in die äußerste und eigenste Möglichkeit ist das verstehende Zurückkommen auf das eigenste Gewesen" (SZ 326). The reconstruction is faithful, and it uses SZ's own structure of temporalizing (SZ 326–328) as the lever. "Dès sa naissance livré à la mort" refers to the stretch between birth and death (SZ 373–374), which Henry does not cite.

Henry says that situation is not confounded with temporality but arises in it: in the projection of the future there is only death; only in returning from it does *Dasein* understand its abandonment, which therefore appears in the ecstasis of the past; hence the link of situation with the past: "Dans la *Befindlichkeit* le *Dasein* est surpris comme l'étant qu'étant encore il était déjà, c'est-à-dire qu'il est constamment été. Le sens existential premier de la facticité repose dans la *Gewesenheit*" (n. 165, SZ 328), and "L'être-déjà est ouvert dans l'horizon du passé" (n. 166, SZ 365); one must distinguish the pure horizon of the past from what appears within it, *Dasein* as already there, which manifests itself as "antérieur [...] à l'ouverture de cet horizon". Henry concludes: "Loin que le découvrir ekstatique comprenant qui revient en arrière puisse fonder l'être-déjeté du « Dasein », sa situation, il la découvre au contraire comme ce qui ne dépend pas de lui ni de l'acte ontologique de sa liberté." SZ 327–328: "In der *Befindlichkeit* wird das Dasein von ihm selbst überfallen als das Seiende, das es, noch seiend, schon war, das heißt gewesen ständig *ist*. Der primäre existenziale Sinn der Faktizität liegt in der Gewesenheit." SZ 365: "im Horizont der Gewesenheit das »Schon sein« erschlossen". Both accurate. The distinction between *Vergangenheit* and *Gewesenheit* is SZ's (SZ 326–328). What Henry draws from it is the independence of the been from its discovery. This independence is not against SZ; it is SZ's own, stated in the very passage the next unit quotes (SZ 340). The unit is a Radicalization of an SZ point, with the explanation (immanence) added.

Henry says that the anteriority of the been in relation to the opening of the ecstasis means independence, which is that of immanence: the been is "située indépendamment de cette découverte elle-même, parce que sa structure est comme immanence celle de la situation"; this alone explains the distinction between *Vergangenheit* and *Gewesenheit*, and the "profondeur" of the past hides "l'origine de l'être". He then reads Heidegger's text: "L'acte d'apporter devant le fait (*das Dass*) de la déréliction propre – que ce soit authentiquement en découvrant ou inauthentiquement en cachant – ne devient existentialement possible que si l'être du *Dasein* est, conformément à son sens, constamment été. L'acte d'apporter devant l'étant déjeté qu'on est soi-même ne crée pas seulement l'être-été (*das Gewesen*), mais son ekstase rend seule possible le se trouver sur le mode du se trouver en situation" (n. 167, SZ 340), and says that the explicit meaning of these propositions "doit en fait être renversée": the ecstasis creates only the horizon and the mode of discovery of *Befindlichkeit*, not its internal structure. SZ 340: "Das *Bringen vor* das Daß der eigenen Geworfenheit – ob eigentlich enthüllend oder uneigentlich verdeckend – wird existenzial nur möglich, wenn das Sein des Daseins seinem Sinne nach ständig gewesen *ist*. Das Bringen vor das geworfene Seiende, das man selbst ist, schafft nicht erst das Gewesen, sondern dessen Ekstase ermöglicht erst das Sich-finden in der Weise des Sich-befindens." The French reproduces the first sentence and renders the second with two displacements: *schafft nicht erst* ("does not first create") appears as "ne crée pas seulement", and *Sich-befinden* appears as "se trouver en situation". The second brings "situation" into the quoted sentence, which is the Corbin-or-Henry rendering that fits guard 15. On the substance: SZ's sentence already says that the ecstasis does not create the been (*das Gewesen*); it makes possible the *Sich-finden* (finding oneself), the way of being disclosed to oneself. So the "reversal" Henry demands is SZ's own order. What SZ adds, in the next sentence, is exactly what Henry will not grant: "die *Befindlichkeit* [...] zeitigt sich *primär* in der Gewesenheit" (SZ 340): attunement itself is temporal. The unit's true opposition is there. Henry grants the ecstasis only the horizon of disclosure and denies that the inner structure of attunement is ecstatic; SZ states that it is.

Henry says that the internal structure of situation, which lies in immanence, shows itself when the philosophy of transcendence is forced to determine the ground of being-situated by the idea of a ground that is not its own ground, the *Nichtigkeit*; the abandonment of existence to itself, residing in the essence of the original *Nichtigkeit*, is foreign to anything of ecstatic form; so the claim that facticity is a state of concealment (*Verschlossenheit*) which "co-détermine le caractère ekstatique de l'abandon de l'existence au fondement *nichtig* d'elle-même" (n. 168, SZ 348) "doit être rejetée", as must the idea of "un rapport ekstatiquement temporel du *Dasein* au fondement déjeté de lui-même" (n. 169, SZ 345): "L'incompatibilité éidétique de l'ekstase temporelle et du propre fondement « nichtig »" makes the idea unable to show the essence of *Geworfenheit*; "Loin de pouvoir se fonder sur la temporalité, le concept ontologique originel de l'être-en-situation se trouve au contraire brisé par elle." SZ 348: "Diese Verschlossenheit aber ist keineswegs nur ein tatsächlich bestehendes Nichtwissen, sondern konstituiert die Faktizität des Daseins. Sie bestimmt mit den *ekstatischen* Charakter der Überlassenheit der Existenz an den nichtigen Grund ihrer selbst." SZ 345: "Gehobene, besser hebende Stimmung ist ontologisch nur möglich in einem ekstatisch-zeitlichen Bezug des Daseins zum geworfenen Grunde seiner selbst." Both quotations are accurate, and this is an explicit Contestation: Henry names the SZ sentences and rejects them on the ground that "ecstasis" and "nichtig ground" are eidetically incompatible. The test against SZ: SZ's thesis is that the *Überlassenheit* has the character of a having-been, which is ecstatic in the sense of the "Außer-sich" (SZ 329) and that the ground is "nichtig" because Dasein exists "nie *vor* seinem Grunde, sondern je nur *aus ihm* und *als dieser*" (SZ 285). SZ thus thinks the ground as something Dasein is out of, not as a region outside it. Henry's incompatibility claim presupposes that ecstasis excludes any inwardness (§30 ¶2, §37 ¶3), the book's thesis, not an argument SZ must accept; SZ's resources (temporalizing as the unity of care) are not met.

Henry says that the temporal definition of situation breaks the prescriptions that define its essence, the most basic being the impossibility of taking an attitude toward oneself, whereas in the return the dimension opens in which *Dasein* can "assume" what it discovers; so free determinations of existence enter the definition: "L'appel du « Gewissen » par exemple est-il autre chose qu'un appel à la liberté" if the *Dasein* is invited to take its situation up and "assumer" the fallen being it is; Heidegger calls the call "un appel *en avant* dans la possibilité d'assumer soi-même en existant l'étant déchu qu'il est, *en arrière*, dans la *Geworfenheit*, pour la comprendre comme le fondement *nichtig* qu'il a à reprendre dans l'existence" (n. 170, SZ 287); "Seul un étant qui [...] est co-originairement ayant-été, peut, en se transmettant à lui-même la possibilité dont il hérite, assumer sa propre déréliction et, dans l'*Augenblick*, être pour « son temps »" (n. 171, SZ 385); the repetition of the been "d'une façon immédiate [...] par ekstasis temporelle" and birth taken up "pour que celle-ci accepte, libre d'illusion, la déréliction du *Da* propre" (n. 172, SZ 391); fate as "super-puissance impuissante", "dans la super-puissance de son projet… sur la dette qui l'engage en propre" (n. 173, SZ 385); and the statement that *Dasein* "ne revient jamais en deçà de sa déréliction" (n. 174, SZ 284, 383), which sits oddly beside the claim that it returns upon it "au-delà et en deçà" in an act of "hyper-pouvoir" composed with the impotence. The quotations agree with the German: SZ 287: "Der Anruf ist vorrufender Rückruf, *vor*: in die Möglichkeit, selbst das geworfene Seiende, das es *ist*, existierend zu übernehmen, *zurück*: in die Geworfenheit, um sie als den nichtigen Grund zu verstehen, den es in die Existenz aufzunehmen hat"; SZ 385 joins "ohnmächtige, den Widrigkeiten sich bereitstellende Übermacht des verschwiegenen, angstbereiten Sichentwerfens auf das eigene Schuldigsein"; SZ 391: "damit diese freilich nur die Geworfenheit des eigenen Da illusionsfreier hinnehme" (the comparative "illusionsfreier" appears in French as "libre d'illusion"). Henry's complaint that SZ juxtaposes heterogeneous determinations is a description SZ half accepts: its own "ohnmächtige Übermacht" names the paradox, and SZ 284 says the same of the self that "kann dessen *nie* mächtig werden und hat doch existierend das Grundsein zu übernehmen". What SZ offers is a single structure (care, fate) in which the two belong together; Henry offers the diagnosis that the free assuming cannot belong to the thrown ground. This is a Contestation of SZ's resoluteness as the existentiell side of the thrown ground, and its force depends on §41 ¶¶9–10: that assuming is a behaviour, not the structure. Against SZ's text the point is whether *übernehmen* is a behaviour of a freedom that stands over its ground; SZ 284 says the self takes over a ground it cannot become master of, i.e. an assuming without mastery, which is not Henry's "hyper-pouvoir".

Henry says that, with the incompatibility, the problematic returns to the doubling of situation: what is defined in the ecstatic bursting of temporality is not the original reality of situation but its *understanding*; since understanding is based on freedom, it can occur in various ways, each tied to a determinate existentiell state; hence "la découverte de cette situation dans sa vérité s'accomplit finalement à l'intérieur d'une détermination existentielle particulière en même temps que privilégiée", the resolute decision: "L'élan anticipateur de la possibilité *dernière et la plus propre*, dit Heidegger, est le revenir comprenant sur l'être-été *le plus propre*" (n. 175, SZ 326); "la situation a son fondement dans l'*Entschlossenheit*", the "*Da* ouvert dans l'*Entschlossenheit*"; "Il n'y a de situation que dans et par l'*Entschlossenheit*" (n. 176, SZ 299–300); "Avec le phénomène de l'*Entschlossenheit* nous sommes conduits devant la vérité… de l'existence" (n. 177, "SZ, 307"); the certainty of this truth is not that of a state of affairs but implies "décision authentique pour la répétition d'elle-même" (n. 178, SZ 308); and to the *Man* "la situation est essentiellement fermée" (n. 179, SZ 300). SZ 300: "Die Situation ist das je in der Entschlossenheit erschlossene Da, als welches das existierende Seiende da ist [...] *ist* die Situation nur durch und in der Entschlossenheit [...] Dem Man dagegen ist die Situation wesenhaft verschlossen." SZ 308 (Henry gives 307): "Mit dem Phänomen der Entschlossenheit wurden wir vor die ursprüngliche *Wahrheit* der Existenz geführt." The quotations are accurate, and this is Henry's most exact use of SZ's term *Situation*. The charge that follows, "l'essence de la situation, dont la structure ontologique est définie par son indépendance radicale à l'égard de tout ce que l'existence peut faire ou être [...] se trouve dépendre au contraire de l'authenticité de celle-ci", is, however, an equivocation of the kind guard 15 notes. SZ's *Situation* depends on resoluteness; SZ's *Geworfenheit*, the structure Henry means by situation, does not. SZ 340 and 345 make attunement and thrownness universal across authentic and inauthentic moods (the "fahle Ungestimmtheit der Gleichgültigkeit" of SZ 345 is a thrown disclosure too), and SZ 308 adds that resoluteness "*gibt* sich die jeweilige faktische Situation und *bringt* sich in sie" (gives itself the factical situation and brings itself into it), the formulation that comes nearest to the freedom Henry says SZ attributes to the discovering of situation. The unit is correct on SZ's text of *Situation* and unfair in treating it as SZ's account of the unchosen.

Henry says that the philosophy of transcendence does not fall into subjectivism by introducing existentiell themes: authenticity and inauthenticity are related by foundation; "C'est la réalité objective de la situation qui se découvre dans l'*Entschlossenheit*", and inauthentic existence flees it; "Tous les modes de l'existence attestent également sa déréliction", whose structure is that of temporality, which returns on itself from its finite horizon and constitutes situation both as what discloses and as what is disclosed; the passage from ontological to existentiell is no objection, so the philosophy of transcendence can claim a definition of being-in-situation as temporality, which it then misses, "si la structure originelle de la situation se définit par son indépendance radicale à l'égard de la temporalité." The paragraph concedes what the previous one contested: Henry grants the SZ's existential–existentiell order (SZ 12–13) and adds that thrownness is attested in all modes, which is SZ 340/345. The concession dissolves the claim of dependence on authenticity made in the previous paragraph. The remaining dispute is single and exact: whether the structure so attested is temporal.

Henry says that the philosophy of transcendence recognized this independence but, unable to understand it from the ontological determination of being-in-situation, "elle l'interprète comme celle de l'étant"; *Dasein* surges as a being in returning on itself in the ecstasis of the past, as the surpassed: "Ce qui est dépassé, dit Heidegger, c'est précisément et uniquement l'étant lui-même, [...] par conséquent aussi et précisément cet étant qu'il est lui-même par son existence" (n. 180, WG 64); thence "une chute essentielle": what is primitively designated by the ecstatic relation is understood in it as the "ce sur quoi" and as a being; the situation is freed with the beings and as theirs, so that "l'hétérogénéité ontico-ontologique de l'étant à l'égard de la temporalité recouvre l'hétérogénéité ontologique de celle-ci et de ce qui [...] la fonde et la situe, et lui est substituée." The quotation is from GA 9 (the *Vom Wesen des Grundes* account of *Überstieg*), not SZ, and the "chute" is Henry's term, taken up at §43's title for the way the ontological structure is read as ontic. The nearest SZ is *Verfallen* (SZ 175–180), where Dasein understands itself from the world; Henry does not link them. SZ's own safeguard is the ontic–ontological distinction at the opening: Dasein is "ontisch ausgezeichnet" by being ontological (SZ 12–13), so that Dasein is a being whose structure is not that of other beings; Henry's reproach that the philosophy of transcendence slides from the one to the other is an objection to that exposed point, with GA 9 as the text.

Henry says that this substitution confuses the ontological impotence of transcendence relative to itself with its metaphysical impotence relative to the being (it is not "créatrice"), and obliges the problematic to include the being in the *Dasein* and so let transcendence fall to the rank of a being; it appears when Heidegger says of man that "ordonné à l'étant qu'il n'est pas, il n'est pas non plus fondamentalement maître de l'étant qu'il est lui-même" (n. 181, *K* 284), and in Sartre: "le pour-soi se saisit dans l'angoisse, c'est-à-dire comme un être qui n'est fondement *ni de son être*, ni de l'être de l'autre, *ni des en-soi* qui forment le monde…" (n. 182, *EN* 642). What is not founded by transcendence is contingent; thus the being shows itself with contingence, which affects *Dasein* itself manifesting as a being; its foundation lies in the independence of the being-*Dasein* from the act by which it returns on itself; "La contingence transit l'essence de celle-ci comme sa détermination la plus propre", the abandonment is then "sa propre culpabilité" in an ethical and metaphysical perspective. The Kantbuch quotation (GA 3, Henry's "K 284") and Sartre are the sources of the confusion; SZ 284–285 is not. SZ's *Nicht* of thrownness is explicitly not a relation to a missing being: "Nichtigkeit bedeutet keineswegs Nichtvorhandensein, Nichtbestehen" (SZ 285), and guilt is not measured against a lacking present-at-hand (SZ 284). The reproach holds for K and EN; its extension to SZ (that "culpabilité" is a derivative of contingence) is not supported at SZ 284–285, where "schuldig" is defined as *Grundsein einer Nichtigkeit* and the *Mangel*-reading is expressly refused.

Henry says that the concept of situation is thus determined from contingence, from the heterogeneity of the being relative to transcendence taken as ground; because the founding activity cannot absorb the being, *Dasein* shows itself as something absurd with the contingence that co-determines facticity, *Gewesenheit*, *Geworfenheit*; "le *Dasein* « se trouve toujours seulement comme fait déjeté »" (n. 183, SZ 328) and "l'acte d'apporter devant l'étant déjeté qu'on est soi-même… crée l'être-été (*Gewesen*)"; "C'est parce que le rapport du « Dasein » au fondement déjeté de lui-même est compris comme ekstatiquement temporel que ce fondement apparaît précisément comme déjeté"; behind "contingence" hides "le n'être-pas-fondement de la transcendance à l'égard non de l'étant mais d'elle-même", "le règne de l'anti-essence"; the same applies when freedom is understood as ground and then described as "non-fondement" and "abîme" ("la liberté est l'Abîme du *Dasein*", n. 184, WG 109 = GA 9 174); and in Sartre "le fait que le pour-soi n'est pas son propre fondement" is reduced to "la nécessité" of being "un être contingent engagé parmi les êtres contingents", tied to a body, "l'en-soi dépassé par le pour-soi néantisant" (n. 185, EN 371–372). SZ 327–328: "Es »findet sich« immer nur als geworfenes Faktum" (accurate). The second quotation reverses SZ: SZ 340 says the act of bringing-before "schafft *nicht* erst das Gewesen" (does *not* first create the been), and the French of §43 ¶5 renders "ne crée pas seulement"; here the sentence is given as "crée l'être-été", the negation dropped by ellipsis (guard 14; here the cut reverses the sense). SZ's claim is the opposite of the one the paragraph attributes to it: the ecstasis makes possible finding oneself, not the been. The "Abgrund" of GA 9 174 is separate: freedom as ground is the abyss of Dasein (GA 9 174, "Freiheit ist der Grund des Grundes"), not an SZ term. The treatment of Sartre in the last sentence is Mediation: Henry argues that the French existentialist heir of SZ reduces the *Nicht* to bodily contingence. The unit's general claim that "contingence" conceals the ontological non-foundedness is a thesis about GA 9 and Sartre, and its use against SZ rests on the cut quotation.

Henry says that when the original structure of being as being-situated, the non-foundedness of transcendence, is confused under "contingence" with the irreducibility of the being, the question of determining the situation of transcendence itself is no longer posed; the characters of being-situated are attributed to the being, so that "C'est l'être-déjeté de l'étant qu'il est qui confère au *Dasein* sa déréliction"; this is absurd if the being has its situation from transcendence, if the concept concerns "non l'étant lui-même, mais sa place", if "le fait d'avoir une situation est la détermination ontologique de l'être de cet étant, non une proposition ontique sur celui-ci" (n. 186, SZ 84). SZ 84: "Mit ihm als Seiendem hat es je eine Bewandtnis. Dieses, daß es eine Bewandtnis hat, ist die *ontologische* Bestimmung des Seins dieses Seienden, nicht eine ontische Aussage über das Seiende" (to be involved is the ontological determination of the being of this being, not an ontic statement about it). The sentence is about the equipment (*Zeug*) in the analysis of the surrounding world, and it says "Bewandtnis" (involvement), which Henry's French renders "situation". The sentence is true and well chosen as an SZ model of an ontological determination being predicated of a being, and it supports the structure of Henry's argument: that having a determinate involvement is the way of being of the being, not a property. The substitution of "situation" for "Bewandtnis" attributes to SZ a thesis about Dasein's being-situated that the sentence does not state. The unit is a Re-inscription by rendering, the last of three in this section (nn. 137, 167, 186).

### § 44. The concept of situation in existentialism. The failure of ontology and realism: "nature and freedom"

Henry says that, by determining the essence of situation from the being, for want of being able to do so from transcendence, the problematic moves in a circle, and that the idea of a "dialectical" concept of situation, as a dialectic of freedom and nature, reveals the impotence it comes from; Sartre defines situation from freedom (the project's end lights the situation: "il n'y a pas de situation inhumaine", n. 187, EN 639), and, since freedom is grounded in the nothingness of transcendence, as a power of tearing away, "à la lumière d'un non-être" (n. 188, EN 511), separating the subject from all content; hence the problem of "engagement" and the proposition that "la condition humaine… ne peut… être congédiée" (n. 189, Jeanson), which Sartre can ground only by linking freedom to the in-itself it denies, "écrasé sur ce qu'il nie" (n. 190, EN 225), so that the surpassed in-itself "demeure et le hante comme sa contingence originelle", the for-itself "ne peut s'empêcher d'être à distance de soi ce qu'il est" (n. 191, EN 162), as "facticité", "passé"; facticity of one's place is revealed only in the free choice of one's end, and the end illuminates the situation only as modification of it (n. 192, EN 574–575; n. 193, 590; n. 194, 635–636): "la situation [...] est un phénomène ambigu dans lequel il est impossible au pour-soi de discerner l'apport de la liberté et de l'existant brut" (n. 195, EN 568). SZ is not cited. Sartre takes *Faktizität*, *Geworfenheit*, *Entwurf* and *Dasein* (as *réalité-humaine*) from SZ through the French reception (guard 10), but replaces SZ's *Nicht* with the *néant* of the for-itself. SZ's "Nichtigkeit bedeutet keineswegs Nichtvorhandensein" (SZ 285) and the thesis that the *Nicht* belongs to the being of Dasein as a thrown project do not make Dasein a negation of the in-itself; Sartre's circle between freedom and the given is a circle his own concept of negation makes. The paragraph therefore bears on SZ only indirectly, as an account of what its heirs did; Henry's phrase "la faillite de l'ontologie" extends to SZ only if the *Nicht* of SZ is read as Sartre's (the point §43 ¶¶11–12 has argued from K and GA 9, with the limits stated there).

Henry says that the same uncertainty appears in Jaspers, where the essence of situation is described as a dialectic of nature and freedom, the union being a "paradoxe" that proves the reality's truth; historicity is "l'union de l'existence et de l'être empirique" (n. 196), of "la liberté et de la nécessité, de la possibilité sans résistance et du donné irrécusable, du temps" and "de l'éternité" (n. 197), a "synthèse du monde en tant qu'il affecte la liberté, et de la liberté en tant qu'elle assume le monde" (n. 198); the necessity that determines situation is interpreted from man's insertion as an objective being into the objective world, as the necessity of things, which becomes external to freedom, "assumée par celle-ci"; "la faillite de l'ontologie laisse le champ libre au réalisme." SZ is not cited. The paragraph depends on SZ in one way: SZ 132 refuses the "Zwischen" as the model of Dasein ("das *Dasein ist das Sein* dieses »Zwischen«" is itself called misleading), and SZ's treatment of being-in-the-world as a unitary phenomenon (SZ 53–62) is its answer to unions of heterogeneous terms. Henry's critique of Jaspers' union is of the kind SZ makes of subject-object.

Henry says that Merleau-Ponty's *Phénoménologie de la perception* has "le pressentiment d'une philosophie de l'immanence" on the problem of situation: reflection "ne s'emporte jamais elle-même hors de toute situation" and the analysis of perception does not make disappear "l'ecceité du perçu, l'inhérence de la conscience perceptive à une temporalité et à une localité" (n. 199); "La réflexion radicale [...] est conscience de sa propre dépendance à l'égard d'une vie irréfléchie qui est sa situation initiale, constante et finale" (n. 200); Henry says that if the unreflected life is the situation of reflection because it is its internal structure, one must determine that situation, but life is for Merleau-Ponty only transcendence; defined negatively against thought, it is "aveugle", a substrate of anonymous functions; "La présupposition empruntée à Heidegger" (n. 201, SZ 107) "selon laquelle celles-ci ne peuvent s'accomplir que sur le fond en elles de la transcendance qui les lie au monde, devient sans objet quand elle ne signifie plus identiquement le surgissement effectif de la phénoménalité", so that the tie becomes "naturel" and situation is the insertion of consciousness in empirical nature; "la situation de l'ego réside nécessairement dans sa structure même et lui est identique"; Merleau-Ponty's "autre sujet au-dessous de moi" is the body as system of anonymous functions (n. 202, PhP 294), "l'expérience vitale du vertige et de la nausée qui est la conscience… de notre contingence", and a dualism relapses (n. 203). The note's SZ 107 sentence is accurate: "Sehen und Hören sind Fernsinne nicht auf Grund ihrer Tragweite, sondern weil das Dasein als entfernendes in ihnen sich vorwiegend aufhält." In SZ the sentence belongs to the analysis of *Ent-fernung* and says that the senses of distance are what they are because Dasein de-severs; it is not an assertion about functions or the body, and SZ defers the body (SZ 108). Merleau-Ponty's use (PhP 294) and Henry's account of it is a Mediation of SZ: Henry says Merleau-Ponty keeps the Heideggerian form (transcendence as the ground of bodily functions) while losing its phenomenological content, and the diagnosis fits the one SZ sentence at issue, though SZ itself never says the "functions" are anonymous.

Henry says that the concept of situation has an ontological meaning, which means it can be understood only from pure phenomenality as constitutive of the possibility and effectivity of a situation; so after showing how the existentiell situation of the being-*Dasein* is determined, how "le « ici » d'un « ici-je » se comprend toujours à partir du « là-bas » d'un *Zuhanden*", Heidegger adds: "« ici » et « là-bas » ne sont possibles que dans un *Da*, c'est-à-dire s'il existe un étant qui a ouvert la spatialité comme être du *Da*", an entity that "porte dans son être le plus propre le caractère de n'être pas caché (« *den Charakter der Unverschlossenheit* »)"; "L'expression « Da » signifie cette ouverture essentielle" (n. 204, SZ 132–133); temporality is, according to Heidegger, the original mode of this opening: "La temporalité ekstatique éclaire le *Da* originairement" (n. 205, SZ 351; also SZ 350, 408: "Parce que la temporalité constitue d'une façon ekstatique horizontale l'être-éclairé du *Da*, elle est originairement toujours déjà explicable et par suite connue dans le *Da*"); the concept's purity requires that the being-there be grasped in what grounds it ultimately, "le donnant originellement à lui-même"; the confusion arises because "la structure constitutive [...] l'essence originelle de la révélation, demeure cachée", which meets the second question of the problematic. SZ 132: "»Hier« und »Dort« sind nur möglich in einem »Da«, das heißt wenn ein Seiendes ist, das als Sein des »Da« Räumlichkeit erschlossen hat. Dieses Seiende trägt in seinem eigensten Sein den Charakter der Unverschlossenheit. Der Ausdruck »Da« meint diese wesenhafte Erschlossenheit." SZ 351: "Die ekstatische Zeitlichkeit lichtet das Da ursprünglich." SZ 408: "Weil die Zeitlichkeit die Gelichtetheit des Da ekstatisch-horizontal konstituiert, deshalb ist sie ursprünglich im Da schon immer auslegbar und somit bekannt." All three are accurate, and this is Henry's most open statement of the SZ answer to the question of revelation: the original opening of the Da is ecstatic-horizonal temporality (SZ 350–351, 365), and what SZ calls *Erschlossenheit* (SZ 133, 220) is the Da's own lighting, *Lichtung*, not something to which a light is added. Henry's reply is that this is the transcendental opening and that it presupposes the ground it does not show: "Pourquoi cette chute se produit-elle ?" The reply is the book's thesis, and the paragraph's honest function is to state that SZ has an answer, temporality as the lighting of the Da, which §§45 ff. must show to be insufficient. The paragraph ends by passing to the second of the three tasks announced at §40 ¶10: why the revelation conceals itself.
